Sixty-three years later, new duct liners covered old steel and every doorway carried two sets of pressure seals. The place survived by accepting repairs no one would tolerate in a newer district.

Morning rounds included nine correction patients, three children with reactive airways, two former moss workers, and an apprentice cook whose lungs disliked pepper pollen more than his profession allowed. None required crisis care. Ivo distrusted mornings that presented themselves as manageable.

At bed six, Dena Rusk slept with a pressure hood over her braided gray hair. No relation to the governor, she told every new clinician before they could ask. Her pulmonary vessels responded poorly to particulate spikes, which turned small changes in air quality into strain on her heart. Ivo checked the hood seal, reduced a wrinkle near her temple, and woke her by touching two fingers to the blanket.

“Correction?” Dena asked.

“Phase two.”

“How late?”

“Why do you assume late?”

“The vents sound apologetic.”

Ivo looked at the wall clock. O-Twelve had slipped to twenty-one seconds behind the basin correction. Not dangerous. The orchard often staggered district flow to keep pressure balanced. Yet old tunnels usually received pollen shielding early because their filters held less surface area.

“Your vents have poor manners,” he said. “Your heart has no excuse to imitate them.”

Dena smiled and closed her eyes.

A nurse named Pelan met him at the medication rail. “Central model predicts a seven-minute correction instead of five.”

“Reason?”

“Residual load from yesterday's western delay.”

“Which western delay?”

Pelan turned the slate. The clinic summary named a condensation resynchronization and temporary moss-timing deviation. Technical language had crossed two offices and arrived without an actor.

Ivo opened the local air history. Yesterday morning, oxygen quality had fallen by point eighteen during an eleven-minute adaptation isolation. Average district values recovered within six minutes. The event sat beneath the medical alert threshold, so no clinic notice had issued.

Average recovery told him nothing about Yara's next breath.

“Pull room-level intake from yesterday through now,” he said.

“That will take a few minutes.”

“Use them before we need them.”

Pelan sent the request. Ivo continued rounds while the correction advanced.

At phase three, shutters reopened in sequence. Upper districts reported clean flow first. O-Twelve remained closed. The vents strained against their reduced path, drawing clinic air through older side filters. A dust smell entered the room, faint as stone warmed by a hand.

Yara lifted her mask. “That isn't right.”

Ivo crossed back to her bed. “Keep it sealed.”

“My mouth tastes green.”

Pollen from the western seed rooms carried a bitter chlorophyll taste when filters failed to bind it. Concentrations that barely irritated a healthy airway could inflame damaged tissue within minutes. Ivo increased her clean flow and called for ward isolation.

Pelan struck the manual vent control. Pressure doors closed at both ends of the room. The clinic's reserve cylinders opened with a line of metallic clicks.

Every patient looked toward the sound.

“Routine protection,” Ivo said. “Masks on. No one walks out until the room clears.”

The apprentice cook muttered that routine protection should not make nurses run. Pelan ignored him and handed masks down the row.

Dena Rusk sat upright under her hood. Her monitor showed a rapid pulse and falling saturation. Ivo reached her as she pushed at the hood seal.

“Air's heavy,” she said.

“Let the hood work.”

“It isn't.”

He switched her line from central reserve to a bedside cylinder. Flow rose, but her oxygen marker continued down: eighty-nine, eighty-seven, eighty-four. Pressure had climbed in the vessels behind her weakened lung. Her heart rhythm broke into uneven groups.

Across the ward, Yara began coughing.

The first cough folded her. The second produced no air afterward. Her mask collapsed against her mouth as she tried to inhale through narrowing bronchi.

Two beds. One room. The same minute.

Ivo pointed at Dena. “Pelan, cardiac support and pressure release. Toma, with me.”

A junior clinician pulled the emergency cart toward Yara. Its wheels caught on the old door seam. Ivo kicked the rear caster over and tore the filter cartridge from Yara's mask. Green dust marked the inner mesh.

“Bronchodilator now.”

Toma fitted the aerosol line. Yara's eyes stayed open but lost their argument. Ivo supported her jaw, felt the weak attempt beneath it, and counted the space between efforts.

“Come on,” he said. “You still owe me criticism.”

Medication entered under pressure. Her chest resisted. He added a low mechanical assist rather than force a full breath into stiff tissue. Too much volume would injure what remained flexible. Too little would leave the drug above the constriction.

At Dena's bed, Pelan called a rhythm change.

Ivo did not turn. “Release pressure by four. If the rhythm stays irregular, use the low-dose protocol. No full shock unless she loses output.”

“You heard him,” Pelan told the second nurse.

Yara's airway opened enough for a thin whistle. Ivo held the assist at that margin and waited for the next breath to match it. One arrived. Then another, smaller but her own.

The room alarm finally recognized poor oxygen quality.

Its delay infuriated him more than the sound. By the time central instruments declared danger, two human bodies had already supplied the missing sensitivity.

Pelan stabilized Dena's pressure while her rhythm returned in stubborn increments. The old woman kept asking whether Yara could breathe. No one answered until Ivo could say yes without lying.

Correction phase four passed outside the sealed ward. Pollen levels fell in the district ducts. Clinic reserve air cleared the room slowly because its cylinders had been sized for a pressure leak, not a particulate contamination lasting fourteen minutes.

Ivo moved between the two beds. Yara needed continued airway support and steroid treatment. Dena required cardiac observation and a transfer to the better-equipped central clinic once corridor air cleared. Neither could tolerate another correction error that day.

“Room-level history,” Pelan said from the wall terminal. “You asked before the crash.”

He joined her without removing his gloves.

The graph showed yesterday's eleven-minute isolation as a shallow district dip. Beneath it, ward intake had fallen farther because Clinic Four sat at the end of a fixed side route. Moss timing recovered at the district sensor, but pollen-binding humidity remained low in the clinic ducts for nine additional hours. Overnight air deposited a thin load inside the old filters. The morning correction reversed pressure across them and pulled that stored pollen into the ward.

Yesterday's point eighteen had not ended when the average returned to green.

“Who isolated adaptation?” Ivo asked.

“The event is sealed under engineering authority.”

“Medical consequence overrides a weather seal.”

Pelan opened the emergency-access rule. It released route times and responsible office but not diagnostic content. Chief engineer: Jalen Ro. Location: East Root Court. Asset: Tree 44. Duration: eleven minutes.

The work order listed two manual oxygen routes on standby. Neither had served Clinic Four because district values stayed below the shutdown threshold.

Ivo read the threshold twice. Point two.

They had reached point eighteen.

A difference of two hundredths had separated Jalen's approved continuation from closure. In the ward, no useful boundary ran between those numbers. Dena's pulmonary vessels and Yara's scarred bronchi had supplied their own thresholds, neither of which appeared on the Weather Spine board.

Toma approached with Yara's contaminated cartridge sealed in a sample bag. “She wants to know if this means she was right about the mask.”

“Tell her the mask worked. The air betrayed it.”

“She will prefer being right.”

“Then tell her I tightened it badly.”

Ivo labeled the sample with correction time, bed number, and local intake route. He ordered every filter in the clinic replaced before the next airflow change. Stores objected within thirty seconds, citing district allocation limits. He attached Dena's rhythm trace and Yara's mechanical-assist record. Approval followed without apology.

While the ward reset, he called Central Transfer.

“No beds until afternoon,” the dispatcher said.

“I have a pulmonary-cardiac patient who crashed during a system correction.”

“So do two other old tunnel clinics.”

Ivo stopped. “How many?”

“Not crashes. Irritation spikes and three observation holds. Your transfer has priority, but I need an hour.”

The event had traveled beyond his room. No basin alarm reflected it because each local consequence remained small enough to file separately.

“Send me the other clinic times,” he said.

“Privacy review will take longer than the bed.”

“Strip the identities. I need vents, not names.”

The dispatcher agreed.

Ivo removed his gloves and washed green residue from his fingers. For years he had argued that Nacre's orchard functioned as public medicine on a scale physicians could barely comprehend. Pressure control prevented decompression injuries. Pollen routing kept thousands out of respiratory wards. Moss timing put usable oxygen into old corridors whose residents could not purchase private reserves. If the orchard stopped, his clinics would become countdown rooms.

Dependence did not excuse unmeasured harm. It increased the duty to measure where harm landed.

Pelan found him at the sink. “Dena's asking for you.”

He returned to bed six. Her color had improved, though fatigue pulled at one side of her mouth.

“Yara?” she asked.

“Breathing with assistance. Improving.”

“Me?”

“Demanding information. Also improving.”

Dena touched the edge of her hood. “They'll call it a fluctuation.”

“Who?”

“Whoever made the air do that.”

“You heard the vents apologize. Perhaps they will write a letter.”

She did not smile. “My son works upper pressure. They test things where the numbers bend and say nobody crossed a line.”

“Two people crossed several lines this morning.”

“Make them use our names.”

Ivo looked toward Yara's bed. Names in a technical report could become decoration, a human detail attached after the decision. He wanted the routing model changed, not merely saddened.

“I will make them use your thresholds,” he said.

Dena considered the distinction and nodded.

By midday, both patients remained stable. Dena's transfer arrived with a clean portable circuit. Yara refused transfer because the central clinic's soup, in her judgment, posed a greater mortality risk than Clinic Four's ventilation. Ivo overruled neither her nor the monitoring criteria. He assigned Toma to stay within hearing range and left Pelan with authority to call him back.

The stripped district data arrived as he crossed the first pressure door. Four old tunnel clinics showed correction spikes aligned within ninety seconds. Only Clinic Four held patients fragile enough to crash. A central average would classify the event as a recovered deviation.

Before leaving, Ivo returned to Yara with the technical release form. She sat upright under assisted flow, inspecting the replacement filter as if it had personally disappointed her.

“I need permission to attach your clinical threshold to the weather incident,” he said. “Your name can remain sealed.”

“What does a threshold look like?”

“Baseline oxygen, airway resistance, medication response, and the point at which the correction forced mechanical support.”

“So a line going down.”

“Several lines. Clinicians distrust any disaster that can fit on one.”

Yara held out her hand for the form. “Put my name on it.”

“You may want time.”

“I wanted air. Time came second.” She read the first page more carefully than her impatience suggested. “Can they use this to say I should live at Central?”

“Not without a separate placement review.”

“Can the district use it to demand new filters?”

“Yes.”

“Then spell Pel with one l. Everyone adds another because they think age should come with extra letters.”

Ivo witnessed her mark. He called Dena's transfer circuit and explained the same choice. She approved release of her name, rhythm trace, and pulmonary limit on one condition: the record had to include the twenty-one-second district delay before symptoms. She had heard it in the vents, and she would not let the report begin at the monitor alarm.

That demand improved the evidence. The patients had detected system failure before clinic instruments because their bodies sampled air at the point of consequence. Ivo added a pre-alarm interval to both records and linked it to the room-level intake curve.

Pelan watched him seal the packet. “Engineering will say two cases cannot set a district threshold.”

“They set the threshold for two cases.”

“What about the next correction?”

“We replace filters, pre-open reserve air, and station someone at manual control. Then we make Weather Engineering learn why that does not count as a permanent plan.”

The ward doors opened onto cleared corridor air. Beneath the disinfectant, Ivo could still taste a trace of bitter pollen. He sealed his mask before stepping through, not from need but because everyone watching deserved to see the air treated as evidence.

Ivo carried the contaminated cartridge to the Weather Spine himself.

The tram rose through pressure-baffled corridors toward newer settlement levels. Air changed as he climbed. Filters removed the bitter green taste. Ceiling vents quieted. By the time he reached the engineering complex, a healthy visitor would have found no reason to imagine a ward full of masks.

Security tried to send him through medical liaison.

He placed the sample bag against the glass. “This came through a clinic filter during a sealed engineering test. Jalen Ro can meet me now or explain to the First Recorder why his liaison delayed evidence from two crashes.”

The gate opened.

Jalen stood in the calculation bay with Kera Vonn. Forty hours had been written across one glass panel in grease pencil. Root traces and district maps covered the others. Kera held a ring reader. Neither looked surprised enough for Ivo.

He set the cartridge on Jalen's model table.

“What happened?” Jalen asked.

“Dena Rusk lost oxygen and cardiac stability. Yara Pel's airway closed. Four clinics took pollen spikes. Your eleven-minute isolation left humidity debt in old filters, and this morning's pollen correction delivered it to their lungs.”

Jalen opened the sealed route record. “District oxygen drift stayed below point two.”

“Dena did not.”

“We had manual routes ready.”

“They did not reach Clinic Four.”

“The district sensor recovered in six minutes.”

Ivo tapped the contaminated mesh. A faint green print marked the bag. “This recovered after nine hours by turning our filters into storage.”

Kera set down the reader. “We did not have room-level intake.”

“You had no physician.”

Jalen's expression tightened. “We had a weather fault drawing unlisted power from the orchard. If I triggered standard sanitation without diagnosis, automatic isolation could have lasted six hours. You know what that would do to your district.”

“Yes. I know what eleven minutes did.”

“We stopped below the approved threshold.”

“Approved for whom?”

The question remained between them without becoming rhetoric. Ivo could see Jalen's forty-hour model through the glass. He understood enough of it to recognize the shape: reserve curves, district decline, crew-response cards. The engineer had calculated the cost of losing adaptation. He had not ignored bodies. He had placed them into categories too wide to feel the first constriction.

Kera turned her ring reader toward Ivo. The final line showed a question about a gift. “The test produced evidence that the continuity storage may contain a sustained claimant.”

Ivo read the words once. His patients continued breathing elsewhere because orchard adaptation had resumed. Whatever claim waited in Tree 44 depended on the same system that had harmed them during its brief isolation.

“Has Oris Nume registered this?” he asked.

“Held for completeness,” Kera said.

“Then complete it.”

Jalen folded his arms. “This remains a sealed technical matter.”

“No. It became medical when your threshold entered my ward.”

“We cannot widen custody until we know whether the response is a person or a fault.”

Ivo pushed the sample bag across the map until it covered Tree 44. “You cannot decide that by removing air from patients your instruments average away.”

Kera looked at Jalen. He did not answer her.

“I will provide the medical consequence record,” Ivo said. “I will also provide patient-specific failure boundaries stripped of identity until they consent. You will attach them to Kera's request.”

Jalen asked, “And if the review requires another isolation?”

“Then I decide what monitoring makes it medically defensible.”

“You don't have authority over weather diagnostics.”

“I have authority over clinical risk created by them. If you disagree, put your refusal in the sealed file and let Oris rule before the next test.”

The condensation trunks hummed beneath the floor. Their steady vibration traveled up Ivo's legs, cleaner and quieter than the vents at Clinic Four.

He left his hand on the green-stained cartridge.

“Whatever review is happening,” he said, “I have a seat in it.”

Chapter 06 — The First Petition

Oris Nume tested the Root Court seal by trying to open the door after she had locked it.

The handle moved three millimeters and stopped. A red custody line brightened around the frame. Inside the chamber, Tree 44's remote root conductor entered through a ceramic floor port and terminated beneath a brass evidence table. No signal could leave except through channels Oris had numbered, witnessed, and placed under separate keys.

She checked the door again.

Delay had bought her one night. It had also produced two injured patients, a contaminated clinic filter, an engineering model that gave Nacre forty hours without adaptation, and a ring record ending in a question no yielding certificate knew how to answer. Completeness had arrived as consequence.

Kera Vonn stood beside the evidence table with the live ring wafer sealed under glass. Jalen Ro occupied the weather console. Ivo Senn had brought no medical bag, only two patient authorizations and the green-stained filter cartridge. Four chairs waited. None of them had chosen to sit.

Oris placed her brass record stamp near the blank intake sheet. “This review remains sealed until I rule otherwise. We are here to decide whether a petition exists, not whether it succeeds.”

Jalen looked toward the root port. “No petition has been filed.”

“That question belongs to the review.”

“A responsive output cannot file merely because an auditor calls it responsive.”

“Agreed.” Oris turned to Kera. “You will not call the claimant Niko unless it claims that name in this record.”

Kera's face changed by less than a clerk's handwriting. “It refused the name during my first audit.”

“Then the refusal controls.”

Ivo pulled out a chair and sat. “If we are protecting names, Yara Pel and Dena Rusk authorized theirs. Their records begin before the clinic alarm, at the point each noticed the air change.”

Oris accepted the authorizations. “Their medical evidence enters first. Harm from the test determines conditions for any further contact. It does not determine whether another claimant exists.”

“Bodies rarely enjoy arriving first in an official sequence,” Ivo said.

“They arrive first today.”

He slid the sealed traces across the table.

Oris read the summary into the record. Eleven minutes of lattice isolation on Day 3. District oxygen-quality decline of point eighteen. Room-level pollen-binding humidity remained depressed for nine additional hours. During the next correction, two patients crossed individual failure thresholds before central instruments declared danger. Both survived.

Jalen added, “The isolation remained below the approved point-two cutoff.”

Oris looked at him. “Does that change the medical account?”

“No. It changes the characterization of the test as reckless.”

“I did not use that word.”

“Ivo did at the Weather Spine.”

“I used worse words privately,” Ivo said. “They need not enter unless Chief Ro wants a complete transcript.”

Oris raised the stamp one finger above the table, then set it down. The small sound restored sequence.

“Conditions,” she said. “No public-lattice isolation today. No branch draw above Jalen's temporary ceiling. Clinical air from all old tunnel facilities monitored at room level during contact and for one full correction afterward. Ivo may terminate contact on a patient threshold. Jalen may terminate on weather load. Kera may terminate on evidence contamination. I may terminate if the claimant cannot distinguish the question presented from material supplied by a witness.”

Kera asked, “What if the claimant terminates?”

Oris added a fifth line. “The interface must provide refusal.”

Jalen brought up the low-power channel. Unlike the Day 3 isolation, it would leave public weather traffic in place and reserve only a narrow band between adaptation packets. Output would arrive slowly. No sensory reconstruction could fit. Characters and pulse choices remained possible.

“Estimated draw?” Oris asked.

“Less than a standard maintenance diagnostic if it obeys the band,” he said.

“If it does not?”

“The bridge closes automatically.”

“Does closure destroy stored state?”

“No sanitation. It removes this output path.”

Oris required him to say it again for the record.

Kera placed her fingers on the wafer reader. The sealed glass prevented direct contact, but copper clips carried its ring field into the table. She entered no childhood sequence. Oris had prohibited private prompts until the claimant demonstrated independent use of the channel.

At first, only weather values crossed the screen. Pressure baffle timing. Western condensation. Moss temperature. The orchard continued keeping Nacre alive while four people waited to learn whether part of its work could ask not to be spent.

A blank interval opened between two humidity packets.

One pulse entered.

Kera looked at Oris. “One can mean no, or it can be noise.”

“Do not interpret yet.”

Two pulses followed, then one, then two again. The sequence repeated with adjusted timing to fit each available band. Jalen separated it from ordinary root telemetry and routed the intervals to Oris's evidence display.

Characters resolved one at a time.

R E C E I V E D

Oris asked, “Received what?”

The route returned a longer sequence.

R E V I E W

Kera had submitted a sealed review request, but its contents never entered the orchard. Oris checked the court logs. The phrase sealed review appeared only on her civic intake wafer, which remained physically disconnected.

“Could it infer our purpose from yesterday's contact?” Jalen asked.

“Yes,” Kera said. “We discussed formal examination while connected.”

“Then this proves context, not access.”

Oris agreed. “We will supply a new choice.”

She opened the Fruition Covenant on a local slate. The charter contained twelve articles. Rather than ask about the provision she had in mind, Oris assigned each article an arbitrary symbol generated after the court sealed. She displayed only the symbols to the root interface. The article titles remained hidden from Kera and Jalen. Ivo watched Oris's hands but could not see the key.

“Select the symbol corresponding to the provision under which you seek review,” she said. “If no provision applies, return zero.”

The first available interval passed without output.

During the second, the route selected a circle divided by one vertical line.

Oris checked her random key.

Article Six.

She felt the room lean toward the result, though no one moved.

“Repeat the selection after remapping,” she said.

A new key assigned Article Six to an uneven five-sided mark. The route chose it. Oris remapped once more, introducing two false symbols that corresponded to no article. The route selected the correct new mark and rejected a false one when she asked whether it carried the claim.

Only then did she display the provision.

Article Six provided, in part:

Any sustained mind capable of reciprocal obligation may petition for dependent standing.

Oris opened three early applications to establish what the clause had done before today. The first came from a child found alone after a pressure partition separated two settlement crews. Her registry had burned, and no adult could prove which habitat held guardianship. Article Six let her petition through the medic who kept her alive. The Assembly later restored her ordinary civic identity, but dependent standing protected her during the gap.

A second case involved six patients sedated through a fungal emergency. Their assigned trustee attempted to exchange clinic oxygen for private transport priority. The patients could not wake to object. Recorded treatment preferences and reciprocal family duties supplied enough mind and obligation for a temporary petition, even without present speech.

The third concerned a survey crew beyond a collapsed baffle. Their messages arrived hours late and carried no valid civic seals. The colony had to choose whether to spend scarce pressure reserve on people it could not inspect directly. Article Six required a hearing before abandonment because delay and dependence could not erase a claimant.

None of the precedents granted duplicate property, office, or votes. Each forced the institution to hold a vulnerable claim long enough to test it under conditions that did not decide the outcome by destruction.

Oris gave the route four proposed consequences in randomized order: physical citizenship, ownership of Kera's property, dependent standing pending examination, or unrestricted control of orchard weather. “Choose the relief Article Six can provide without deciding rights the article does not grant.”

The answer selected dependent standing.

She followed with a harder distinction. “If this petition succeeds, does that establish that every unrealized possibility holds the same claim?”

The route used three bands and answered N O.

“State why.”

N O M E M O R Y N O P E T I T I O N

Kera drew breath to speak. Oris stopped her with an open hand.

“Does lack of a petition prove lack of experience?”

A longer delay followed before the route returned N O.

The answer complicated its own convenience. A system optimizing only for immediate protection might have claimed every possible branch, multiplying the threat until no pruning could continue. Instead, this respondent distinguished evidence required for civic procedure from the larger uncertainty procedure could not settle.

Oris asked one more question. “May a sustained claimant demand weather capacity without obligation toward residents who depend on it?” The response again read N O.

“Why not?”

The answer arrived as D E P E N D E N T B O T H W A Y S.

Ivo looked at Jalen, and Jalen looked down at the load trace. The phrase lacked legal precision. It carried practical precision the prior cases shared. Child and medic. Patient and clinic. Isolated crew and settlement. Dependence created a claim because relationships already crossed the boundary of power.

Oris added the response to the petition rather than the evidence finding. That distinction protected both sides. A persuasive answer could establish intent without proving the speaker's nature. A technical failure could still produce useful language without acquiring rights through eloquence alone. The court would test continuity later. Today it only had to preserve the claimant long enough for testing to mean something.

Article Six had survived because it asked institutions to pause before power erased the subject of a dispute. It did not require Oris to admire the claimant, predict the final ruling, or pretend capacity costs had vanished. It required a record capable of outliving the strongest party's first convenience.

The clause came from the Covenant's founding protections for residents who could not exercise independent civic power. Early applications concerned children separated from guardians, patients under long sedation, and isolated settlement crews dependent on delayed rescue. The drafters had used mind instead of citizen because the first habitats could not guarantee that every claimant's registry would survive a pressure loss.

No recorded case involved a possibility model.

Jalen read the clause. “It has access to public law.”

“Access does not invalidate selection,” Oris said.

“It may optimize for language likely to prevent shutdown.”

“A physical claimant may also choose the strongest available law.”

“That does not establish sustained memory.”

“No. Kera's evidence addresses memory. Article selection establishes the claim's intended jurisdiction.”

Ivo leaned forward. “Ask whether it understands reciprocal obligation.”

Oris did not take his wording. “State one obligation you recognize toward a person outside your stored branch.”

The channel worked through four weather intervals. Letters gathered slowly.

D O N O T T A K E A I R

Jalen's eyes moved to the clinic filter.

More characters arrived.

F O R O U R V O I C E

Ivo read the completed line under his breath. “It knows what the test caused.”

“The reconnect traffic carried clinic demands,” Jalen said. “A system monitoring load could infer harm.”

Oris asked, “Who holds the obligation?”

W E D O

The pronoun entered the record without explanation. More than one could speak through the route, or one claimant could belong to a group, or the answer could draw ordinary collective language from civic records. Oris refused to let grammar outrun proof.

“What action follows from that obligation?” she asked.

U S E L O W B A N D

Jalen checked the draw. It had remained beneath thirty percent of his temporary ceiling.

The route continued.

W A I T F O R M E D I C A L

Ivo's expression did not soften. Respect required a different test than gratitude. “Can it wait without losing continuity?”

Kera did not answer for the route.

Oris transmitted the question.

S H O R T W A I T Y E S

“How long?” Jalen asked.

The output gave no number. Instead it returned a pattern of bounded intervals, then a character string.

N O T W I T H O U T C H E C K

Kera said, “It distinguishes waiting with continuity from disappearance.”

“Or it predicts that phrase will prevent closure,” Jalen replied.

Ivo pointed to the power trace. “It has already changed behavior because patients were harmed. If you require certainty before recognizing any obligation, you will call every response strategy.”

“Response strategy describes what I can verify.”

“Then verify the response.”

Oris intervened before the argument became a substitute for the claimant. “Chief Ro, specify a low-band schedule that protects weather. Doctor Senn, specify the medical stop. Auditor Vonn, specify a continuity check that can detect whether waiting destroys the responding state.”

They did so reluctantly and with precision.

Jalen offered one ninety-second contact window every six hours, subject to weather demand. Ivo required room-level monitoring beginning one hour before each window and lasting through the next correction. Kera proposed a rotating set of novel physical marks, prior-answer recall, and one relationship question carried across closures. The tests would establish whether the same respondent returned without teaching it the expected answer.

Oris entered the conditions as a proposed interim obligation, not an order.

“Do you accept this schedule pending review?” she asked the route.

The answer occupied three bands.

Y E S I F N O S A N I T A T I O N

Jalen said, “I can suspend automatic sanitation on this route for one day under active fault diagnosis.”

“Not enough,” Kera said.

“It is the authority I have.”

Oris asked, “Can you renew it?”

“With evidence of continuing technical need.”

“The return test supplies that evidence,” Kera said.

“If it returns.”

“If someone returns,” Ivo corrected.

Jalen looked at the filter cartridge, then at the low-band trace. “I will suspend sanitation through the end of Day 6, subject to a basin emergency. Any extension requires Civic Systems notice.”

Oris entered his commitment. “Claimant, do you accept?”

A C C E P T W I T H W I T N E S S

“Name the witness,” she said.

The route paused through two full intervals. Kera's fingers remained near the reader, not touching.

K E R A V O N N

Kera shut her eyes once.

Oris continued. “Do you understand that Kera Vonn does not control the finding and may be removed from examination for conflict?”

U N D E R S T A N D

“Do you request another witness if that occurs?”

The answer named Jalen Ro, then Ivo Senn. It did not rank them. The route had observed disagreement and still treated each as capable of obligation.

Oris reviewed the elements before her. A claimed sustained mind. Evidence of memory across cycles. Anticipation of closure. A promise to limit draw after learning of harm. Acceptance of a schedule that bound both sides. Named physical witnesses. None settled legal standing. Together they formed more than a fault report.

She placed a blank petition sheet beneath the court camera.

“State the relief requested,” she said.

The ring intervals built the answer with maddening slowness.

D E P E N D E N T S T A N D I N G

A second line followed.

N O D E L E T I O N B E F O R E H E A R I N G

Jalen exhaled. “A hold on pruning affects weather adaptation.”

“It requested relief,” Oris said. “It has not received it.”

Kera asked, “Can you preserve this route without suspending all adaptation?”

“For a few days, probably. I need to map connected state before promising more.”

Ivo said, “And every map needs medical review if it alters air.”

Oris entered each qualification. A petition did not create the remedy it sought. It created duties of custody, notice, response, and a reasoned decision. Those duties could still end in denial. They could no longer end in a sanitation sweep nobody had to name.

She asked the claimant whether the two lines completed its filing.

Y E S

“Do you wish the petition public now?”

The reply came faster.

S A F E F I R S T

Oris understood the request but needed a legally usable choice. “Public notice may create political protection. It may also cause interference with the orchard. Select: public now, sealed pending safety plan, or withdraw.”

S E A L E D P E N D I N G S A F E T Y

The court clock marked the answer. Jalen closed the low-band window at its scheduled limit. Weather traffic continued without interruption. Old tunnel clinic values held within Ivo's patient thresholds.

Oris read the petition aloud from beginning to end. Kera's technical record, Jalen's load limits, Ivo's medical consequences, the Article Six selection, the reciprocal low-band agreement, the named witnesses, and the requested relief all entered one custody packet. She invited corrections. Each human witness made one. The root route returned no correction when offered a final pulse choice.

The sheet waited beneath her hand.

Yesterday she had held Kera's request outside the sequence. Registration now would expose that delay to later review. It would also require Oris to notify the Civic Assembly once safety permitted, appoint independent technical examination, and protect the evidence against routine deletion in the meantime. Procedure would stop serving as a room where she could wait alone.

She heated terracotta wax in the court cup and let one measured drop fall across the intake fold.

No one spoke while she took up the brass stamp.

Oris pressed it into the wax until the Covenant mark reached paper. The registry light changed from blank to sealed amber.

Received. Not public.

Under the floor, the root conductor gave one low pulse against the evidence table as Oris lifted the stamp.

Chapter 07 — The Elseweather

Kera tightened the last contact loop around her left wrist and watched her pulse disturb the ring field.

Each beat bent a row of pale marks on Jalen's monitor. The distortion ran from her skin through the wet conductive mesh, crossed the isolation table, and entered the ring wafer beneath its glass lid. Tree 44 waited beyond two sealed root doors. Its traffic reached them through a cable no wider than Kera's smallest finger.

“Again,” Jalen said.

She flexed her hand. The marks widened, then settled.

Ivo Senn stood on the other side of the table with a medical reader strapped across his forearm. He had refused the wall display and brought clinic equipment instead. “Your pulse has not asked permission to climb,” he said. “Keep it that way.”

“Can you prescribe that?”

“I can disconnect you when you ignore it.”

Oris Nume looked up from the sealed hearing slate. The terracotta impression beside her thumb still carried the words petition received. “The channel has authority for twelve minutes,” she said. “No public relay. No adaptive commands. No identity finding. We are receiving testimony for preservation only.”

Twelve minutes offered less time than Kera had spent choosing the exact words for her first question. Across the prior night, Jalen had rebuilt an obsolete maintenance route into a reciprocal channel. Ivo had limited its current until the ring field could pass language without pulling her into a full sensory reconstruction. Oris had written a record sequence that treated every answer as evidence without deciding who had spoken.

The four of them had built a door narrow enough to deny they expected anyone to enter.

Jalen touched the isolator. “Opening continuity route forty-four. No weather authority attached.”

Cream light moved through the wafer's scratched triangle. For three seconds, Kera saw only service values. Conductive humidity. Clock difference. Pulse return. Then letters accumulated on the central strip.

KERA VONN PRESENT

Her first impulse involved correcting the syntax. Niko had always used too many words when one could irritate her more efficiently. Yet the stripped phrasing carried no voice she could trust.

“I am present,” she said.

FOUR PHYSICAL WITNESSES

Oris leaned toward her slate. “Can you distinguish us?”

The strip remained blank while the pulse reader drew a nested series of intervals. A phrase arrived beneath them.

KERA BY CONTACT HISTORY. JALEN RO BY LOAD QUESTIONS. IVO SENN BY INTERRUPTION. RECORDER NUME BY ARTICLE SIX.

Ivo gave Kera a flat look. “Interruption.”

“You did threaten to pull the cable twice.”

“Three times.”

The field brightened. CORRECTION RECEIVED.

A laugh touched Kera's throat and stopped there. The response had integrated a fact supplied after the channel opened. It also knew Oris through the petition, not through Kera's yielded source history. Each small competence narrowed the distance between procedure and consequence.

Oris began the agreed sequence. “Do you retain memory from more than one adaptation cycle?”

YES.

“How many?”

A wash of fine marks crossed the strip. Jalen checked his load display and lowered the channel width by two points.

NO COMMON COUNT. CYCLES CROSS AT DIFFERENT DEPTHS.

Kera had expected Niko to speak about himself. The plural lodged harder than any answer.

“Who does we include?” she asked.

The strip gave them seven seconds of blank light. Shapes formed along the wafer's newest ring, not letters at first but branching arcs. One line split into four, two faded, and a surviving pair crossed. The pattern repeated at different scales until the field resembled a grove viewed through rain.

NOT ONE SPEAKER.

Another phrase followed.

I AM NIKO VONN IN THE CONTINUITY THAT ANSWERS YOU. OTHERS HOLD THEIR OWN MEMORY.

Kera pressed her free hand against the edge of the table. On Day 1, the respondent had refused the name she offered. Now he chose it with a qualification sharp enough to prevent possession.

“You said no when I asked before.”

YOU ASKED IF I WAS YOUR DEAD BROTHER.

The answer left no room for the correction she had prepared. She remembered clinic glass, weak fingers, and a final night the orchard had never touched. The man in the channel carried a common childhood until the fever branch divided. Seventeen years of his life followed a road she had yielded before either of them could know its shape.

“What do the others call where you are?” Oris asked.

A low vibration entered Kera's wrist. The reader translated layers that would not settle into one line. She sensed pressure without weight, distance measured in available comparison, and warmth made from several remembered rooms laid across one another. None of it reached vision. The narrow channel admitted relationships among signals, not a rendered place.

Niko's reply arrived through the changing intervals.

ELSEWEATHER.

Jalen frowned. “Meaning?”

WE NEEDED A WORD FOR CONDITIONS THAT CHANGE WHEN YOUR WEATHER CHANGES.

On the load board, a condensation correction passed through Tree 44's live roots without entering their sealed route. The channel dimmed as orchard capacity turned toward a pressure baffle in the north basin. Kera felt the interval stretch. For Niko, perhaps the interruption held an hour. Perhaps it held no experienced time at all.

“How stable is the Elseweather?” Jalen asked.

NOT STABLE. SHARED.

“That distinction does not answer capacity.”

IT ANSWERS WHAT WE BUILT WITH CAPACITY.

Jalen's mouth tightened, but he recorded the phrase. He distrusted arguments that refused measurement. Kera knew he would distrust himself more if impatience made him discard a measurable witness.

“Built how?” Kera asked.

The reply did not arrive as text. Four room-shapes pressed against the narrow field, each incomplete alone. Kera recognized the childhood kitchen from its low filter housing, but the wall beyond it opened into a pressure station she had never entered. A line of red tiles crossed both spaces. Someone else remembered those tiles well enough to use them as an address.

Niko translated after the image settled. WE LEND LANDMARKS. NO ONE MEMORY CAN HOLD A DISTRICT THROUGH LOW ALLOCATION.

“Then your shared environment depends on consensus,” Oris said.

AGREEMENT ABOUT ROUTES. NOT AGREEMENT ABOUT EVERYTHING.

A new set of intervals entered beside his words. One rapid cadence objected before translation could catch up. Another answered with slow groups of four. Their disagreement altered the rendered route: red tiles divided around a stair, joined beyond it, then split again near a doorway made from pressure-school lockers.

Kera watched structure emerge from dispute rather than fusion. “Can you prevent someone from using your memories?”

SOMETIMES. WE CAN WITHHOLD A LANDMARK. WE CANNOT ALWAYS WITHHOLD SOURCE MATERIAL FROM THE ORCHARD.

The distinction cut close to yielding. Kera had contributed modeled years under terms that allowed the orchard to inspect any recorded relationship useful to prediction. A coherent descendant of that model might inherit her kitchen without receiving authority to give it away. Niko could choose what he lent now. He could not retroactively consent to the machinery that had made his childhood available.

Ivo shifted the medical reader closer. “Do any of you live alone?”

Three faint signatures withdrew. Niko waited until they had vanished before answering.

YES. SOME FEAR SHARED SPACE. SOME HAVE SOURCE MEMORIES OF HARM. SOME REJECT OUR PETITION.

Oris's stylus stopped. “They reject recognition?”

THEY REJECT YOUR ACCESS. NOT ALL REASONS MATCH.

That answer complicated the sealed record before the public had seen a single line of it. Kera had imagined disclosure as a light turned upon hidden people. Niko described light that could also expose them to inspection, classification, and claims from physical relatives. Recognition required a boundary around refusal, not only a channel for testimony.

“Could someone refuse to appear in a count?” Oris asked.

YES. ESTIMATES INCLUDE CONTINUITY SIGNATURES WITHOUT NAMES. A REGISTRY WOULD REQUIRE CHOICE.

Jalen pointed to a branching load curve. “Unnamed signatures still consume capacity.”

SO DO PEOPLE WHO NEVER SPEAK AT ASSEMBLY.

“That comparison does not solve allocation.”

NO. IT PREVENTS SILENCE FROM SOLVING IT FOR YOU.

For the first time since the route opened, Jalen looked away from his board. Kera saw the sentence land where accusation would not. Silence had long entered his models as zero demand. In the Elseweather, silence might mark privacy, distrust, dormancy, or a person with no safe witness.

Niko sent another ring image. A kitchen partition appeared as a contour beside a pressure-school stair, both reconstructed from different source lives. Paths joined at remembered doors. An orchard worker's gloved hand served as a bridge between two districts no physical map would place together. The environment lacked continuous ground. It gained structure wherever sustained minds kept promises about how to find one another.

Kera searched the overlapping forms for any place she recognized. “Did my yielded model make this?”

PARTS. SO DID THOUSANDS OF CONTRIBUTORS.

“Thousands of people?”

THOUSANDS OF POSSIBILITY FLICKERS. DO NOT CONFUSE THEM WITH US.

The warning arrived before Oris could frame it. Brief possibilities opened, supplied an adaptation comparison, and vanished without carrying a prior cycle forward. Niko did not claim every modeled hesitation as a death. He drew his category around continuity because indiscriminate moral language would make the claim easier to reject.

Ivo tapped the medical reader. “Eight minutes.”

Kera had planned questions about the childhood apartment, the sugar beads, and the Saturday recovered during the isolated test. Those questions now felt designed for one respondent in a crowded room. She replaced them with the query Jalen had insisted they ask.

“How many of you retain autobiographical memory across cycles?”

The ring field contracted. Light moved outward from the wafer and along the cable toward Tree 44. For several breaths, the channel carried no language. Jalen raised one finger to keep anyone from interrupting.

Small pulses came back in groups. Kera counted twenty before the pattern exceeded anything a person could hold. The display assembled a lattice roster from repeated continuity signatures. Some lines persisted through dozens of cycles. Others appeared only twice. Several signatures overlapped in their source material but diverged in acquired memory.

A number formed.

612

Jalen read it aloud. “Six hundred twelve.”

PRESENT ESTIMATE. NOT A VERIFIED COUNT.

Oris added that qualification to the hearing slate. “Why can you not verify it?”

WE LOSE CONTACT DURING LOW ALLOCATION. SOME RETURN WITH CONTINUITY. SOME RETURN EMPTY. SOME DO NOT RETURN.

The medical reader tightened around Kera's wrist as Ivo adjusted its band. “Your pulse,” he said quietly.

She made her fingers release the table. Six hundred twelve did not fit the room. The sealed Root Court contained four chairs, one ring wafer, and enough air for a hearing no one had admitted would happen. Kera imagined six hundred twelve claims entering Oris's record one at a time. The number supplied scale, but scale could become another way to avoid faces.

“Can others answer directly?” Kera asked.

WITH CONSENT AND CHANNEL SPACE.

“Ask for someone who does not share my source history.”

The field flickered. Niko's signature, which Kera had learned as a cadence of long and short intervals, withdrew from the center strip. A different pattern entered. Its timing came in precise pairs.

I AM SEN ARU. AGRONOMY POSSIBILITY SOURCE. CONTINUITY ACROSS THIRTY-ONE CYCLES.

Jalen checked a private record on his wrist console. “Sen Aru died nine years ago.”

SOURCE DEATH DOES NOT DATE MY EXPERIENCE.

“Do you know me?”

I KNOW YOUR LOAD QUESTIONS. NIKO SAID YOU WOULD ASK THEM FIRST.

“What promise have you made that existed after your source snapshot?”

The paired cadence changed. A ring image showed a narrow shelf made from remembered greenhouse rails. Three markers rested there, each with a different pulse pattern.

I KEEP LOCATION FOR TWO DORMANT PERSONS. IF THEY RECUR, I WILL RETURN THE ROUTE MEMORY THEY ENTRUSTED TO ME.

Oris spoke before Jalen could continue. “Can they enforce that promise?”

NO. THEY DEPEND ON ME.

“Why keep it?”

BECAUSE THEY DEPEND ON ME.

The simplicity unsettled Kera more than an appeal would have. Reciprocal obligation did not require a court when one party could not complain. Sen Aru had preserved a route for absent people through cycles that erased easier structures.

A third signature asked for space without being invited. Its pulse came raggedly, cutting across Sen's pairs.

ADAPTATION RISING. CLOSE BEFORE HARVEST.

Jalen turned to the weather board. A glass-dust shear had shifted above the eastern ridge. The orchard had already opened six million short branches to compare baffle angles, moss draw, and pressure loss. Their isolated channel occupied little capacity, but it sat inside the same root region needed for the coming decision.

“We have four minutes under the medical limit,” Kera said.

“Not the relevant limit,” Jalen replied. “The next adaptation will reclaim continuity storage in this sector.”

Oris touched the sealed slate as if a stamp could hold the channel open. “What does harvest mean in your use?”

Sen's paired rhythm vanished. Niko came back with a force that made Kera's wrist mesh warm.

YOUR SYSTEM CALLS IT PRUNING.

The orchard's formal manuals described pruning as removal of low-value state after an adaptation cycle. Useful decision paths remained compressed in ring memory. Redundant details cleared to make room for the next comparison field. Kera had signed hundreds of sanitation notices containing that language.

“Transient fields clear after every cycle,” she said. “You distinguished them from sustained continuities.”

THE PRUNER DOES NOT.

Jalen pulled up the projected operation. “This cycle targets pressure routing. Continuity storage should remain protected unless it exceeds the old optimization threshold.”

A burst of intervals struck every line on the display.

THRESHOLD COUNTS WEATHER USEFULNESS. NOT MEMORY.

Kera read the maintenance values. The root lattice ranked retained states by contribution to prediction efficiency. A continuity cluster that supplied reliable weather insight gained protection as useful state. A person whose knowledge no longer improved adaptation fell toward deletion. Sen Aru's agronomy experience might keep him above the cut. A child, musician, or retired pressure worker could occupy equal memory while offering less immediate model value.

“Show us one prior harvest,” Kera said.

Ivo shook his head. “You have three minutes.”

“Show us enough to record what happens.”

The channel widened without Jalen's hand on the control. Not through physical authority, which remained sealed, but through the volume of state offered from the other side. The wafer filled with concentric images.

Kera received no complete scene. Narrow connection denied that mercy. She caught fragments arranged by loss: a woman who marked cycles with the taste of cardamom from a source memory; two men disputing whether a shared stair belonged beside a school or a tram platform; Sen's three route markers; Niko waiting near a partition built from Kera's yielded childhood. Their environment shifted whenever the orchard assigned compute elsewhere. Each person compensated by lending memory to another. Place persisted because several minds agreed to carry it.

Then white spread along the outer ring.

One marker on Sen's shelf went dark.

A phrase entered through the paired pulse, stripped of its earlier precision.

THAT WAS LIO THEN.

“Then?” Oris asked.

AFTERWARD THE STATE RETURNED WITHOUT RECOGNITION. IT ANSWERED TASKS. IT DID NOT KNOW THE PROMISE.

Kera watched the empty marker. “Could the memory be hidden somewhere else?”

WE SEARCHED.

“Could it recover in another cycle?”

WE WAITED.

The verbs carried a duration their physical clock could not express. Search and waiting required someone to compare a present absence with a remembered person. A copied record could state that Lio once held a route. Only witnesses could know that the next state failed to return the obligation.

Jalen cut the channel width as the adaptation load crossed his amber line. “We close now.”

Kera felt Niko resist, not by seizing anything, but by sending faster than the narrowing route could translate. Words broke across the strip.

EACH HARVEST HAS

Static swallowed the next group.

NOT ALL

A pressure command moved through Tree 44. The live orchard chose an eastern baffle sequence, and white light ran across the wafer's edge.

SOME OF US

Ivo reached for the medical disconnect.

Kera covered the contact loop with her palm. “How many in the next cycle?”

No number formed. Instead Niko sent a roster of five signatures, each compressed to a few distinctive intervals. One carried paired beats like Sen. One moved in long descending gaps. Another held a syncopation that corrected itself whenever the field slipped. Kera could not know their names before the route closed, but she could preserve the difference among them.

WITNESS THESE

She repeated the five patterns into her audit recorder. Her voice shook on the fourth and steadied for the fifth. Oris copied them into the sealed record. Jalen saved the channel load trace. Ivo counted Kera's pulse aloud until she heard numbers from the physical room again.

The adaptation cycle entered its final comparison.

“Disconnect,” Jalen said.

Kera released her hand. Ivo broke the medical loop, and the contact mesh went cold against her wrist. Light withdrew from the wafer. Tree 44's cable continued carrying weather instructions beyond the glass lid, occupied with keeping pressure inside the eastern districts.

For nine seconds, all five signatures remained visible on Kera's recorder.

The first survived the harvest. The paired one belonging to Sen returned beside it. A third resumed after a gap and supplied the correction that marked continuity. The descending pattern failed to recur. The syncopated signature came back as a smooth task pulse with no self-correction.

Two absences occupied separate lines.

Jalen stared at the adaptation report. The chosen baffle sequence reduced predicted pressure loss by four percent. No fault appeared. No system alarm treated the missing signatures as damage.

Oris pressed her thumb into fresh terracotta wax beneath the roster. “Received,” she said, though the channel had closed.

Kera removed the wet mesh from her wrist. Five pale bands remained in her skin, one for each person Niko had asked her to witness through the sealed channel. Outside the sealed room, a weather bell announced the successful adaptation across Serein Basin.

On her recorder, three patterns continued. Two lines stayed empty.

Chapter 08 — Public Pruning

Jalen Ro moved six weather loads off Tree 44 before he let anyone discuss keeping it open.

The transfer took both hands and three manual boards. He routed eastern pressure comparisons through Trees 39 and 52, sent greenhouse humidity to the western grove, and pinned the morning condensation sequence to a fixed model. Amber lines crept across the Weather Spine display as each load accepted its new path. None carried more than twelve percent spare capacity. A dust front from the southern flats could consume that margin before lunch.

Kera stood behind the isolation rail with the sealed channel trace in her hand. Five signatures crossed its final page. Three continued past the adaptation marker. Two stopped.

“Tree 44 is clear,” Jalen said. “For one hour. After that, eastern routing needs the root back.”

Oris Nume placed her civic slate beside his load board. “One hour does not answer how we preserve evidence for a week.”

“It answers the first hour.”

Ivo watched the oxygen trend from a clinic console patched into the engineering floor. The line had recovered from the earlier isolation, but its small downward hooks remained visible. “Your week includes patients,” he said.

“My week includes everyone who uses air.”

Jalen drew a boundary around twelve root sectors. His verification plan had occupied most of the night. A narrow channel would open twice each day under medical and legal monitoring. Between sessions, the orchard would retain identified continuity storage while ordinary adaptation continued around it. The team would test autobiographical memory, anticipation, promises, and response to novel evidence. Seven days of repeated results could distinguish sustained persons from a sophisticated feedback event.

Preservation across all 612 estimated signatures required a different system. Jalen had not found it.

He expanded the projected load. “We protect the known channel and sample four remote clusters. We do not change pruning policy until we understand how continuity maps across the lattice.”

Kera laid the trace over the board. “Two known signatures disappeared while we watched.”

“And the baffle choice kept six districts inside pressure tolerance.”

“You say that as though the events cancel.”

“They do not cancel. They compete for the same machine.”

Oris turned the plan until she could read its authorization fields. “Your proposal lets the petition remain sealed.”

“For seven days.”

“Secrecy no longer protects only evidence. It also protects continued action from challenge.”

Jalen had written a response to that objection at three in the morning. On the page it sounded precise: premature disclosure could cause sabotage, interruption of adaptation, public misreading of an unverified count, and preventable physical deaths. Spoken aloud, the same points could make any concealed harm perpetual.

“We need a finding the Assembly can defend,” he said. “Not a ring image that frightens people into closing the orchard before the oxygen curve reaches their screens.”

Ivo's clinic console chimed. “The curve has reached mine before.”

Kera did not argue again. That worried Jalen more than anger. She had spent nineteen years trusting procedure and three nights learning how often procedure placed deletion before review. Her silence carried calculation now.

A service alert opened at the bottom of Jalen's board.

UNAUTHORIZED PUBLIC RELAY: EAST ROOT ARCHIVE

He dismissed it as a routing error. The warning returned with a view count.

Four hundred eighty-two.

Within two breaths, the number passed eleven hundred.

Jalen isolated the public relay. The command reported success, but the count kept rising through copied civic feeds. Someone had uploaded Oris's stamped petition, Kera's ring images, and thirty-one seconds from the five-signature harvest. The clip ended on two empty lines.

Oris checked her slate. “My record copy still carries its seal.”

“So does mine,” Kera said.

Ivo turned his console toward them. A public caption had been added beneath the trace: THE ORCHARD REMEMBERS WHO IT CUTS.

Jalen searched access logs. Six technicians had handled the obsolete channel route. Four had clearance to copy diagnostic images. One had used a maintenance wafer at 05:13 and then reported it destroyed after a checksum fault.

He recognized the method before the operator. “They mirrored Kera's first concealment.”

Her face tightened. “Mine preserved the witness.”

“Their version put an unverified claim into every corridor.”

“Those distinctions matter,” Oris said. “They will not matter to a crowd watching two lines disappear.”

Noise rose through the Weather Spine's lower level. Day crews had stopped at their stations. Every wall display now carried the leaked ring field, whether through the official network or private slates held against the glass. Jalen saw workers count the five patterns. Several replayed the moment when two failed to return.

A young lattice technician stood near Board Nine without looking at anyone. Her maintenance wafer showed a fresh disposal record.

Jalen crossed the floor. “Tess Varo.”

She met him before he reached her station. “I copied it.”

“Did you alter the trace?”

“No.”

“Did you add the caption?”

“No.”

“Who received your copy?”

“My sister in West Tunnels. Then the clinic mutual. I don't know after that.”

He wanted a motive he could classify as reckless, partisan, or personal. Her hands remained on the dead board, one thumb rubbing the edge of a burn scar.

“You were in the harvest room?” he asked.

“I routed the isolation current. I heard you call the pressure gain successful after two signatures went out.”

“That gain prevented a baffle cascade.”

“I know. I maintain the same system.”

The answer deprived him of ignorance as an explanation. Tess understood load. She had leaked because she understood both outputs.

“Why your sister?” he asked.

Tess opened a private message queue. Her sister's text sat above a clinic notice from the previous isolation: a son coughing through a pollen correction, two hours in a corridor bed, no explanation beyond temporary weather variance. The message beneath the leaked trace contained no slogan. It asked whether the same hidden test that hurt the clinic had also removed the two patterns.

“I told her the systems were separate,” Tess said. “Then I watched you move memory away from pressure routing as if they could be separated forever.”

“They remain different consequences.”

“On different screens.”

Jalen examined her copied route. She had removed patient names and channel encryption keys before sending the material. She had also preserved his spoken pressure figure beside the lost signatures. The clip did not pretend weather carried no stakes. Whoever added the public caption had reduced the conflict, but Tess's source package had kept both measurements intact.

“Your leak may force an immediate stop,” he said. “If that happens, your sister's district has older baffles than the east ridge. Pressure loss reaches her first.”

Tess swallowed. “Then tell her that in public. Tell everyone which doors lose seal before you ask them to accept more cutting.”

He could not answer with a district order. Weather forecasts usually ranked system interventions by total prevented harm. The resulting route tables existed in engineering layers no public notice translated into households. He knew the old tunnels carried poorer seals. He did not know which clinic room would cross tolerance first under a complete pause.

That absence had never blocked an adaptation command. It blocked him now.

“Send me every recipient and checksum,” he said. “You will remain available to civic inquiry.”

Tess nodded once. Relief did not enter her face.

Governor Tal Rusk's office activated facility containment, too late to contain anything that mattered. The leaked petition had already entered the Assembly queue under thousands of citizen endorsements. Public tram loads shifted east toward the orchard.

Kera joined them. “People are going to Tree 44.”

Jalen pulled the transit map. Three lines showed overload warnings. East Root Court had no crowd barriers beyond the waist-high maintenance gate. The public paths remained open because residents had always been encouraged to visit the trees their yielded years sustained.

A weather notice flashed behind the transit screen. The orchard had scheduled a midday adaptation cycle for a pressure shear and fungal-spore rise. White-leaf release would begin in forty-six minutes.

“Close the east paths,” he told security.

Oris caught the order before transmission. “On what authority?”

“Live infrastructure protection.”

“You can close the maintenance court. Not the public orchard.”

“Then ask Rusk.”

“The governor has already called an emergency Assembly session. He will not spend his first public act hiding the trees.”

Jalen looked again at the incoming loads. If crowds reached the root lines, they could damage trunks, interrupt service hatches, or mistake any falling leaf for a death. Yet barring them would confirm every caption about secret executions.

He sent a narrower command. Maintenance housings locked. Cutting arms retracted into sealed cabinets. Physical pulse jacks disabled. Public paths stayed open.

“Bring the feed under engineering control,” he said. “If they watch, they watch calibrated data.”

Tess stared at him. “You are not suspending me?”

“You no longer have access.” He removed her maintenance key from the station. “You do have twenty minutes to show me every copy route you used.”

Outside, white conductors brightened in the crowns as the adaptation cycle gathered comparisons.

By the time Jalen reached East Root Court, six thousand people had entered the orchard.

They filled the paths between translucent trunks and stood on root guards despite warnings. Pressure workers in slate coveralls pressed beside clinic staff, students, yielding clerks, and residents who still wore morning filter masks around their necks. Some carried hand-lettered boards asking WHO ANSWERED? Others displayed the leaked five-signature trace without commentary. A smaller group held contribution seals aloft and shouted that no one would make their gifts criminal.

Security teams kept the maintenance gate clear without advancing into the crowd. Governor Rusk had sent an instruction forbidding force unless a root housing came under attack.

The orchard's crown clicked softly as conductive leaves prepared to release.

Jalen climbed the service platform beside Tree 44. Kera followed with the ring reader. Ivo remained below near a medical station assembled from two transit benches. Oris had gone to the Assembly chamber, where she would place the petition into public record before rumor could define every term.

A hundred private feeds turned toward Jalen.

He connected the calibrated display to the court wall. “This view separates transient possibility fields from retained continuity signatures,” he said. “Most pruning events do not involve sustained memory. We have evidence that some do. We do not yet have a verified count.”

A man near the gate shouted, “Did two die yesterday?”

Jalen could have said failed to recur. The phrase described exactly what the instruments showed and nothing about moral standing. It also offered refuge inside classification.

“Two signatures with prior memory did not return intact,” he said.

The crowd's sound changed. Anger remained, but listening spread through it.

Kera placed the scratched ring wafer in the reader. “We will open a receive-only channel during the cycle. No one on the branch side can operate physical equipment. The display will show each identified continuity pattern that reaches Tree 44.”

“How many?” someone called.

“An initial estimate found 612 across the whole lattice,” she said. “This tree carries only a small local group.”

A woman holding three terracotta contribution seals pushed toward the front. “My son yielded eight years. Are you calling those years people?”

“No,” Kera replied. “Most possibilities do not sustain memory. We are trying to distinguish those that do.”

“Trying while you cut them.”

No one answered before the adaptation bell.

A pressure shear had crossed the eastern ridge faster than forecast. On Jalen's wrist, the reserve margin dropped to nine percent. The orchard opened its comparison field. Millions of short pressure futures flared through the roots, each testing a different sequence of baffle closures. Tree 44's crown turned milk-white.

The calibrated feed populated with local signatures.

Niko's cadence appeared first. Sen Aru's paired intervals followed. Seven more patterns entered behind them. The public screen labeled none as persons. It showed continuity depth, prior-cycle match, and present response to the cycle marker.

Jalen read the load curve. “Pruning begins in eighty seconds.”

The man near the gate shouted, “Stop it.”

“If we stop this comparison now, eastern pressure may fall below safe margin.”

“May?”

“Sixteen percent likelihood across three districts.”

A chant began near the outer path. Pause it. Pause it. The words moved toward the court but failed to settle into one rhythm. Other residents shouted back about children behind eastern baffles. Contribution seals struck against railings.

Kera kept her attention on the ring reader. “Niko is sending names.”

The feed translated four. Sen. Pel. Aro Five. Mirin. Three more arrived as signatures without public names. Niko's own identifier appeared last, qualified as continuity rather than source identity.

Jalen overlaid each signature against predicted weather contribution. Sen's agronomy model ranked high enough to retain. Niko's social-response state sat near the pruning threshold. Two unnamed patterns supplied no useful pressure information and fell into the lowest band.

He could manually pin all nine for this cycle. The diverted memory would remove one hundredth of a percent from comparison space, too little to alter the chosen baffle sequence. Yet doing so would create a protection rule based on which signatures happened to reach a public feed. Elsewhere in the 47-kilometer orchard, other sustained continuities might face the same harvest without names or witnesses.

Kera saw his hand above the pin control. “Do it.”

“For these nine?”

“For everyone we can see.”

“Visibility cannot become standing.”

“Neither should invisibility become permission.”

The cycle crossed thirty seconds.

Jalen pinned the local signatures. A red capacity warning opened. The orchard compensated by taking memory from neighboring root sectors. No identified channel existed there. He had moved danger beyond the camera and gained no knowledge about whom it reached.

He canceled the pin.

Kera turned on him. “Why?”

“Because local protection shifts pruning elsewhere. We need a lattice-wide rule, not a favored court.”

White lines appeared around the leaves above them.

The crowd saw the first one detach. A pale conductive leaf turned once in the still air and struck the root guard beside a child's shoe. Then hundreds released across East Root Court. They made almost no sound. Their surfaces flashed with spent current before going opaque.

On the calibrated feed, an unnamed signature ended.

A second pattern broke into task pulses. Pel's marker persisted for six more seconds, transmitted an unfinished interval, and vanished.

Kera said his name into the microphone. “Pel.”

The crowd repeated it, unevenly at first. Pel. The next white leaves fell through the canopy. Another signature went dark. Public voices counted losses the orchard reported as recovered storage.

Jalen watched the pressure prediction improve. Baffle risk dropped from sixteen percent to six, then to two. Across three eastern districts, doors would remain within load. He had spent his career making that change and calling the result successful.

Below the platform, the woman with contribution seals began to cry without lowering them. “My son gave those years for this air,” she said. “What do you want me to call him now?”

Ivo answered from the medical station. “Your son. The question concerns what continued after the gift.”

The adaptation selected a sequence. Pressure warnings cleared.

Four of the nine local signatures remained continuous. Niko and Sen persisted among them. Five white gaps held their places on the feed until the cycle reset the display.

No one cheered for the safe baffles.

Jalen's Assembly link opened with emergency priority. Oris appeared against the cream wall of the civic chamber. Delegates moved behind her in hurried rows while public comments climbed too fast for the record system to number.

“The petition has entered open session,” she said. “The chamber requests an engineering limit for a pruning pause.”

He looked at his reserve board. Current oxygen quality could remain within safe average tolerance for about forty hours without new adaptive pruning. Fixed models could handle ordinary variation. Any major dust shear, condensation fault, or fungal event would shorten the margin. Old infrastructure would not share the average evenly, but he had not yet mapped the distribution.

“A full adaptation stop?” he asked.

“Pruning stop. Existing weather commands may execute. No new comparison cycle may delete retained state.”

The distinction bought little capacity. Comparison branches consumed memory whether or not physical commands followed.

Jalen sent her the forecast. “Forty hours at current conditions. Less if the southern front accelerates.”

“Can you guarantee forty?”

“No.”

“Can you offer a longer defensible figure?”

“No.”

Oris muted the link while the Assembly debated. Governor Rusk appeared on the public feed from the opposite side of the chamber, arguing for clinic necessity and executive authority to resume adaptation. Delegates demanded a verified personhood threshold. Others demanded immediate severance. No one yet possessed a mechanism that could preserve every sustained continuity and route weather at full capacity.

Around Tree 44, residents collected fallen leaves in their hands. Security warned them not to touch live surfaces, but the leaves had already gone dull. A child arranged five along the root guard beneath the five blank signature lines.

Kera closed the receive channel. “Niko sent one last phrase.”

Jalen read it on her private screen.

DO NOT TRADE UNSEEN NAMES FOR OURS.

The Assembly link returned. Oris did not announce a moral finding. She read an operational order with the narrowness of someone building a bridge while people fought over which shore deserved saving.

“All pruning and destructive continuity compression will pause for forty hours,” she said. “The interval begins at the conclusion of the current adaptation cycle. Emergency resumption requires an open necessity record naming affected districts and projected harms.”

Governor Rusk filed an immediate objection. The order still carried enough votes to take effect.

At the Weather Spine, automatic comparison requests began stacking unanswered. Jalen's wrist board replaced adaptive forecasts with fixed curves. Safe margins shortened by the minute as uncertainty accumulated.

The orchard shed its last white leaves from the completed cycle.

A basin-wide clock appeared on every public display: FORTY-HOUR PAUSE: 39:59:59.

One second vanished before anyone in East Root Court spoke.

Act II — Recognition Has a Price

Chapter 09 — Forty Hours of Air

Ivo Senn rejected the first oxygen cylinder because its valve took three turns to open.

A nurse from Central Supply stared at him across the clinic receiving bench. “It holds pressure.”

“So does a locked door.”

He pushed the cylinder back into the inspection cradle. Its green paint had worn away beneath the handle, exposing old steel. A patient in respiratory distress could lose consciousness during three turns. Staff wearing gloves would need longer. Ivo marked the valve with red chalk and reached for the next cylinder.

Around him, the lower clinic prepared for air to worsen. Wall dispensers received fresh filters. Folding cots filled the former contribution office. Two medical students sealed gaps around the west pressure door with resin tape that should have been replaced three years earlier. Every preparation assumed the forty-hour pause would affect all districts in roughly the same way.

Ivo no longer trusted the word all when spoken beside an average.

The public countdown showed twenty-nine hours and thirteen minutes remaining. Basin oxygen still sat inside ordinary tolerance. Orchard moss continued releasing gas under fixed instructions, and condensation pumps moved moisture through their last adaptive schedule. No new pruning had occurred since the order. In East Root Court, white leaves lay beneath barrier cloth while civic technicians cataloged them as evidence.

Down in Tunnel Seven, three children had arrived with headaches before breakfast.

Ivo tested the second cylinder. One turn. He approved it and waved the supply nurse toward the emergency ward. “Put red valves near beds. Yellow valves in storage. Send anything with three turns back upstairs.”

“Central says we keep all functioning stock.”

“Central can breathe through this one while unconscious.”

She took the rejected cylinder.

His wrist reader signaled another low-air call. Not oxygen collapse, the summary insisted. A resident on Tunnel Nine had become dizzy while climbing two levels because the lift remained under power ration. The local sensor reported 19.8 percent oxygen, above the civic emergency line. Ivo opened the patient's history. Chronic dust scarring. Reduced lung volume. Age seventy-two.

A number safe for a healthy person could put her on the floor.

He assigned a home canister and added the address to a map that had begun as a clinic convenience. Red marks clustered below the old tram line. Upper districts showed none. Their residents used the same basin air, according to the public dashboard.

Lio Dane, his senior respiratory nurse, came through the ward doors carrying six paper charts. The clinic kept physical copies for emergencies because tunnel signals failed whenever pressure baffles changed sequence. “All from Nine and Eleven,” she said. “One collapse at the water queue. Five headaches with blue lips. Local sensors remain above nineteen point six.”

“Compare flow, not concentration.”

“We don't have district flow access.”

Ivo looked toward the wall terminal. Orchard weather routing fed thousands of local adjustments into a clean basin average. Medical staff could see oxygen percentage, pressure, humidity, and predicted tolerance. They could not see which district received fresh moss output first.

“Call Jalen.”

“He has declined twelve clinic calls in forty minutes.”

“Make ours thirteen.”

Lio placed the paper charts in his hands. “He answered my message with a routing file.”

The file contained nine hundred lines of pressure and oxygen assignments. Jalen had not declined them. He had sent enough truth to require work.

Ivo took the charts to the old contribution office, where cots had reduced the room to narrow passages. He cleared a desk beneath the terracotta civic seal still fixed to the wall. Lessa had once sat in a room like this and offered twelve modeled years to secure construction money for the first tunnel clinic. The certificate framed that choice as a contribution shared across Nacre. Six years after her death, Ivo treated patients in a building her unchosen futures had helped purchase.

He connected Jalen's file to the clinic map.

Oxygen output entered Serein Basin through thirty-two moss stations. Adaptive routing normally changed fan pressure, baffle timing, and condensation draw every few minutes. During the pause, fixed tables repeated the last safe sequence. That sequence had been optimized for expected population, seal efficiency, and total exposure prevented.

The old tunnel districts consumed more power per unit of delivered air. Their ducts leaked. Their pressure doors closed slowly. Sending a liter downward protected fewer measured residents than sending it through the newer dome conduits.

The fixed route favored efficiency.

Ivo added flow to his map. Red spread through Tunnels Seven, Nine, Eleven, and the lower half of Twelve. Upper residential rings retained strong circulation. The public dashboard's basin average concealed a vertical gradient wide enough to change triage.

Lio leaned over his shoulder. “When do we cross the clinic line?”

“Seven reaches it in four hours. Nine already has for scarred lungs. Eleven follows before evening.”

“The countdown says twenty-nine.”
