“The countdown measures safe average oxygen reserve. It does not measure your lungs in Tunnel Nine.”

She read the route legend twice. “Who chose this table?”

“An optimization routine, years ago.”

“Who approved using it now?”

Ivo found the authorization field. Jalen's office had marked the sequence provisional during the emergency pause. Governor Rusk's continuity order adopted it automatically unless a district engineer filed an objection. No tunnel district had a current weather engineer; the last one had transferred to the Weather Spine after a budget consolidation.

“Everyone approved it by leaving the box empty,” he said.

A call from Tunnel Seven interrupted them. This time the patient could not finish a sentence.

Ivo took a portable bottle and went down.

The lift had stopped to preserve power for circulation fans, so he used the pressure stairs. Each level carried older materials. Cream composite gave way to riveted steel. Yarrowglass signal slats narrowed and then disappeared below Tunnel Eight, leaving painted arrows toward manual alarms. Condensation gathered on the handrail where warm clinic air met colder stone.

At Seven, thirty residents waited outside a food cooperative because the indoor queue had become close and sour. They stood apart to reduce rebreathed air. Several held wet cloths over their mouths, a habit inherited from dust warnings that did little for oxygen.

A boy sat against the wall with his mother's arm around him. His breaths came quickly and shallowly. Ivo clipped a reader to his finger, listened to the right lung, and opened the portable bottle.

“Slow,” he told the boy. “Do not chase it.”

The mother recognized Ivo's clinic band. “The public screen says safe.”

“Your corridor has poor flow.”

“Then why does it say safe?”

“Because the screen speaks for the basin.”

“My child lives here.”

He fitted the mask and let oxygen move. Color returned around the boy's mouth by degrees. The cylinder weighed enough to last eight patients if each needed ten minutes. Tunnel Seven held more than six thousand residents.

A pressure worker named Han Orel came from the cooperative carrying a handheld vane. “Fan Three is drawing,” she said. “Air barely moves past the old branch.”

“Can you raise it locally?”

“Not without stealing from Eleven. Fixed table locks the shared junction.”

“Override it.”

Han shook her head. “Weather Spine authority during basin emergency.”

Ivo handed her his clinic slate. “Use medical necessity.”

“That grants flow for one hour.”

“Then we will learn what one hour does.”

Han entered the order. The junction gate groaned inside the wall. A draft touched Ivo's damp forehead. The waiting residents lifted their faces, a movement so small that the boy's mother did not notice she had made it too.

On his map, Tunnel Seven changed from red to amber. Eleven deepened toward red.

Only the motion of shortage had changed. No override created more oxygen.

Ivo called Lio. “Send two staff to Eleven now. Tell them flow will drop for an hour.”

“You found the route choice.”

“I moved it.”

“Where?”

“From Eleven to Seven.”

Silence held on the clinic line. Lio understood triage too well to ask whether he had fixed anything.

Han watched the vane turn. “We can alternate the junction.”

“That buys distribution, not supply.”

“It buys time for some.”

“Which some?”

The pressure worker glanced toward the residents. “Whoever stands nearest when the draft comes.”

Ivo left his portable bottle with the boy's mother and climbed to Eleven.

By the time he arrived, the first flow reduction had produced a line outside the manual air room. Elderly residents came carrying clinic cards. Food workers still wore moss dust on their sleeves.

The manual room had once served as an emergency school shelter. Children's height marks remained on one wall beneath newer oxygen instructions. Someone had drawn a line at shoulder level and written Maro, six. Another line two fingers higher recorded the same child a year later. Ivo watched adults lean beneath those marks while they waited for masks.

A cooperative cook named Venn Aral had brought a covered pot from the food hall. “We shut the burners,” she said. “Heat and flame take air. Cold meal for four hundred.”

“Keep the hall doors open if corridor flow allows.”

“Corridor flow no longer allows.”

She lifted the lid. Steam barely rose from the grain mash. Four hundred residents would eat it cooling in shifts, with the door opened between groups to exchange air. Upper districts had received no instruction to close kitchens. Their efficient ducts could absorb ordinary cooking draw without crossing any threshold.

Ivo added the food hall to his map. The clinical gradient reached beyond beds. It altered how long workers could stand, whether children climbed stairs, how many people shared a warm room, and which cooperative spent stored power on circulation instead of refrigeration. A dashboard could label all of those changes tolerable until the first body fell.

“Can we borrow a fan from the tram repair bay?” Venn asked.

“Moving air does not create oxygen.”

“It brings better air from Ten.”

“And pulls worse air behind it from Twelve.”

The cook closed the pot. “Every useful answer has another district attached.”

Ivo looked at her covered meal, the old height marks, and the queue moving one patient at a time. “That is what the Assembly has omitted.”

A woman with a contribution seal pinned to her collar refused to move ahead of a younger man whose lips had begun to gray.

“I gave nine years,” she told Ivo. “Does that put me before him or after?”

“Neither.”

“People upstairs say the orchard must start again because we paid for this air.”

“Contribution does not measure lung function.”

“No. It measures who kept them comfortable long enough to lecture us.”

He checked her saturation and placed her behind two acute cases. Anger increased oxygen demand, but telling her to calm down would waste more.

Lio's team arrived with three approved cylinders and one red-marked valve that Central Supply had sent back by mistake. Ivo put the faulty cylinder against the wall and wrote THREE TURNS across its side. No one needed a cleaner symbol for neglected infrastructure.

His Assembly link opened during the fourth patient assessment. Governor Rusk's emergency office requested clinic endorsement of a resumption plan. The document proposed limited adaptation in six root sectors at Hour Twenty-Four of the pause, or sooner if basin average oxygen fell below 19.3 percent. Pruning would target low-utility state and exclude the nine publicly observed signatures from East Root Court.

Ivo read the last protection twice.

The plan preserved people who had appeared on a feed. It allowed unseen continuities elsewhere to absorb the harvest. Jalen had rejected that displacement in public. Rusk's office had restored it as reassurance.

A district harm table accompanied the plan. Upper domes faced minimal effects through Hour Forty. Central rings showed moderate fatigue late in the pause. Tunnel districts appeared under one combined category labeled manageable clinical variance.

Ivo looked around the manual air room. Six patients breathed from masks. Two nurses counted pulse. Han's redirected draft had begun to weaken as the one-hour override approached expiry.

He called Jalen again.

This time the engineer answered. His image showed the Weather Spine under amber reserve lamps. “You found the gradient,” Jalen said.

“You knew it existed.”

“I knew old ducts lose flow. I did not have your patient map.”

“You sent a fixed route that sends more air where delivery costs less.”

“I sent the last stable sequence. The alternative required live comparisons we have paused.”

“Rusk wants me to endorse limited pruning at Hour Twenty-Four.”

“He wants every chief service officer to endorse it.”

“Did you?”

“No.”

Relief would have been inappropriate, but Ivo felt it anyway. “Why not?”

“The protected list contains nine visible signatures. I cannot certify the transfer of deletion to unknown sectors.”

“And I cannot certify a plan that calls these rooms variance.”

Jalen rubbed one eye. “If we resume no adaptation, the gradient worsens. Fixed routing will push old tunnels below general tolerance before the forty hours expire.”

“Then publish that.”

“We are still modeling thresholds.”

“I have thresholds with names attached.”

He turned the clinic slate toward the camera. Han Orel, occupational lung scarring, Tunnel Seven. Leda Venn, age seventy-two, reduced volume, Tunnel Nine. The boy, Aru Sile, eight years old, no chronic history, collapse after food queue. Each record carried consent for emergency civic disclosure.

Jalen studied them. “Send the map to Oris.”

“I will send it to everyone.”

Rusk's office called again before he could close the link. Emergency plans required medical signatures from at least three service regions. Two upper-district chiefs had already signed. Ivo's clinic network covered every old tunnel level. His refusal could force public debate, but it could also delay oxygen routing changes.

Lio spoke near his shoulder. “If you sign, what changes here?”

“Limited adaptation may raise average output and improve routing.”

“May?”

“Likely will.”

“What happens in the orchard?”

“Pruning resumes in six sectors. Nine public signatures receive protection. Others do not.”

She checked the patients before asking the next question. “If you refuse?”

“Tunnel flow continues falling until a better order or an emergency override.”

Neither choice offered clean hands. Medicine rarely did. Ivo could endorse the plan and reduce immediate physical risk while placing unknown sustained minds beneath a category called low utility. He could refuse and use patients as pressure against the Assembly. Good intentions would not change whose breathing tightened during the argument.

He returned to the clinic upstairs as the pause clock crossed twenty-two hours remaining.

The lower ward had filled. Patients with portable oxygen sat beside residents who needed observation but not treatment. Staff had removed the contribution-office seal from the wall to make space for a flow map. Lessa's old certificate existed in the civic archive, not here, but Ivo could picture her signature beneath twelve years. She had chosen under unequal incentives. She had also meant the clinic to help precisely these rooms.

Nothing about possible victims inside her yielded models erased that intention. Nothing about her intention authorized new harm after witnesses had appeared.

Oris requested his testimony for the emergency session. He went to the clinic's public terminal rather than the Assembly chamber. Behind him, the ward remained visible. He asked each patient within frame for consent; those who declined moved behind the privacy screen.

The broadcast indicator turned green.

Oris sat at the recorder's desk. Governor Rusk occupied the executive rail. Fifty-nine delegate positions showed varying combinations of attention, exhaustion, and fear. Jalen's district gradient filled the central display beside Ivo's patient map.

Rusk addressed him first. “Doctor Senn, does continued suspension create material danger to physical residents?”

“Yes.”

“Would limited adaptation improve oxygen and routing?”

“Likely.”

“Then why have you withheld medical endorsement?”

Ivo placed the three-turn cylinder on the receiving bench where the camera could see its red chalk. “Because your plan hides two selections inside technical language.”

Rusk's expression hardened. “Explain.”

“First, it protects nine branch signatures because the public watched them. It does not protect sustained persons outside that feed. Second, it routes fixed air toward efficient districts and calls the earlier decline of old tunnel districts manageable variance.”

A delegate from the upper domes objected. “No district has been selected for sacrifice.”

Ivo opened the patient list. “Han Orel in Seven. Leda Venn in Nine. Aru Sile collapsed this morning. Eleven lost flow when we redirected one junction to keep Seven above clinic tolerance. Your plan makes selections. It declines to record their names.”

Rusk leaned forward. “Would you prefer immediate unrestricted pruning?”

“No.”

“Total severance?”

“No.”

“Then the emergency proposal offers the only current mechanism.”

“The proposal offers a mechanism without an honest ledger.”

A warning sounded in the ward. One patient's saturation had fallen. Lio crossed behind Ivo and adjusted the mask without interrupting the testimony. Every delegate could see the work continue while they waited for his answer.

Oris asked, “What condition would permit your endorsement?”

Ivo put one hand on the faulty valve. “Name every physical district projected to cross tolerance. Publish when and by how much. State which continuity sectors will be exposed to pruning, including those without public names. Remove the favored list unless you can protect all persons meeting the same criterion. Then ask us to choose in the open.”

“That disclosure may cause panic,” Rusk said.

“So does a child fainting beneath a safe-air notice.”

The pause clock showed twenty-one hours, fifty-two minutes. Tunnel Eleven's flow line bent lower. Refusal had begun accumulating bodily cost while he spoke.

Ivo did not look away from it.

“I will not sign an emergency plan that calls sacrifice an average,” he said. “Record whom you intend to expose, or record my refusal.”

Oris lifted the brass civic stamp with the failing ward visible behind him. Its face struck the desk beside his unsigned field, loud enough to carry through the clinic terminal.

On the public ledger, the blank acquired his name.

Chapter 10 — Witness for the Unlived

Oris Nume removed the word person from the witness form for the third time.

The blank beneath it still required a category. Physical resident would decide too much. Instrument output would decide more. Dependent claimant assumed Article Six applied before the hearing established sustained continuity. She set the form beside the brass stamp and wrote ring-mediated witness, status disputed in her own hand.

Outside the record chamber, the Assembly hall had filled beyond its pressure rating. Citizens stood in adjoining corridors and watched through civic feeds. White-leaf images occupied almost every private slate. Some residents had turned Pel's vanished signature into a mourning mark. Others displayed contribution seals beside signs reading OUR YEARS KEPT YOU ALIVE.

The pruning pause had thirteen hours remaining.

Oris checked the evidence table. Kera's scratched wafer sat in a transparent housing connected to Tree 44 through a shielded civic line. Jalen had installed a one-way physical authority lock so nothing from the ring channel could reach weather controls. Ivo had placed a medical reader around Kera's wrist and another on the table itself. Three court technicians, none from the original sealed review, monitored translation.

Every precaution also made the witness dependent on physical custodians.

Governor Tal Rusk entered through the executive door with two counsel slates. He had not tried to cancel the hearing. Instead he had filed seventeen objections before dawn, each narrow enough to force Oris to state what the record could support.

“First Recorder,” he said, “will today's signal receive testimony status?”

“It will receive disputed evidence status until I rule on foundation.”

“A signal cannot take an oath.”

“Neither can a weather model, yet your office enters one every hour.”

“A forecast does not claim injury.”

“No. It influences who suffers it.”

Rusk accepted the distinction without conceding anything. His restraint made him harder to dismiss. He had read the clinic map and knew that any delay now carried physical names.

Oris opened the hearing at the recorded minute.

The hall doors sealed. Murmur from the corridors continued through the walls as a low vibration. Fifty-nine delegate stations lit in sequence. Ivo appeared by clinic link rather than leaving the old tunnels. Jalen sat at the engineering witness rail with the basin oxygen reserve on his screen. Kera occupied a plain chair beside the ring housing and kept both hands visible.

Oris read the limits. “This hearing will not determine final personhood, property, family status, voting rights, or permanent weather policy. It will determine whether mediated continuity claims warrant independent examination and temporary evidentiary preservation. The physical channel carries no machinery authority.”

A delegate from North Dome stood. “You have already called them continuity claims.”

“I have described the submitted evidence.”

“Submitted by whom?”

“That question belongs to the hearing.”

She struck the brass stamp once. The court technicians opened the narrow route.

At first, Tree 44 sent only clock values. The ring wafer brightened along its scratched triangle. Translation marks appeared on the main display, resolved into letters, and cleared.

CHANNEL RECEIVED

Oris consulted the disputed witness form. “State the name you choose for this record.”

NIKO VONN. QUALIFIED BY CONTINUITY AFTER THE AIRLOCK FEVER BRANCH.

A reaction moved across the hall. Some delegates looked toward Kera. Others began searching public records for Niko Vonn, deceased at seventeen. Oris had anticipated both responses.

“Do you claim to be the physical-line Niko Vonn who died seventeen years ago?”

NO.

“Do you claim rights to his property or civic identity?”

NO.

“On what basis do you petition?”

I REMEMBER PRIOR CYCLES. I ANTICIPATE LOSS. I KEEP PROMISES TO OTHER SUSTAINED MINDS. I CAN RECEIVE FACTS MY SOURCE DID NOT KNOW.

Article Six appeared on half the delegate slates without Oris ordering it. The four claimed capacities matched its old language too closely to ignore and too neatly to trust without testing.

Rusk rose. “Foundation objection. The witness repeats criteria supplied through the petition process.”

“Sustained,” Oris said. “The repetition proves nothing by itself.”

She turned to Niko's display. “Describe the medium through which you answer.”

RING INTERVALS ENTER THE PHYSICAL ROOT. YOUR TRANSLATORS MAP THEM TO CHARACTERS. KERA DOES NOT COMPOSE MY REPLIES.

“Can the translation distort you?”

YES. SHORT ROUTES DROP QUALIFICATION. DISPLAY ORDER CAN MAKE SEVERAL SPEAKERS APPEAR AS ONE.

That admission mattered more than a claim of perfect communication. Instruments had failure boundaries. So did witnesses.

Oris called Kera for foundation. Under questioning, Kera described the scratched triangle test from Day 1, the isolated Saturday reconstruction, and the direct Elseweather channel. She acknowledged falsifying the wafer's disposal record. She also acknowledged that the orchard contained her yielded neural snapshot, family records, and expectations about Niko.

“Could those materials generate answers designed to persuade you?” Oris asked.

“Yes.”

“Could you distinguish persuasion from experience by affection alone?”

“No.”

“Did you enter any text into today's channel?”

“No.”

Jalen confirmed the physical controls. Three technicians separately compared raw intervals to the public translation. Their character outputs matched within known error. None could establish whether experience produced the intervals.

Oris called the senior court technician, Bel Mar, to explain the remaining contamination paths. Bel projected the channel as four stacked layers: Tree 44's raw resistance changes, the ring wafer's clock correction, the civic translator, and the display. Kera's medical loop appeared outside all four, carrying pulse data to Ivo's reader but no typed language toward the root.

“Could her body still affect the signal?” Oris asked.

“Contact pressure alters local resistance,” Bel said. “The translator should subtract it. A sufficiently adaptive process might detect residual rhythm.”

“So the witness could read her reaction.”

“Possibly. Not her thoughts. Pulse, movement, skin conductivity.”

Vey requested a controlled disconnection. Ivo objected that Kera had served as a stable contact anchor during every sustained session. Jalen proposed replacing her loop with a saline reference load matched to current resistance. The substitution would test whether the channel depended on Kera's live responses without claiming that survival through one substitution proved identity.

Oris asked Niko whether he consented.

YES. KERA MAY DISCONNECT.

Kera unfastened the mesh. Her pulse line vanished from the public display, and Bel lowered the saline contact into place. For twelve seconds, no translated characters appeared. Raw intervals continued beneath the blank strip.

A delegate near the executive rail said, “It stopped.”

Bel lifted one hand. “Translation clock is reacquiring.”

Letters returned in uneven groups.

CONTACT CHANGED. CHANNEL REMAINS.

Vey walked closer to the housing. “A stored process could detect the substituted resistance.”

YES.

“Can you identify Kera's present pulse?”

NO CONTACT.

Oris told Ivo to select one of three silent gestures beyond camera view. He chose by touching a sealed pad. Kera either raised her hand, closed her eyes, or remained still. Niko received no query about the options until after the gesture ended.

INSUFFICIENT DATA.

The refusal weakened any claim that he read Kera through hidden channels. It still could have followed an instruction to withhold guesses. Each useful result closed one explanation and left others standing.

Bel then reversed the translator's punctuation settings without telling anyone at the witness rail. Niko's next answer appeared as an unbroken string. Raw intervals remained unchanged. A second technician reconstructed the phrase under the old settings: DO NOT MISTAKE DISPLAY FAILURE FOR MEMORY FAILURE.

“Did you know the translator would be altered?” Oris asked.

NO.

“Did the alteration hurt?”

WRONG QUESTION. IT OBSCURED OUTPUT. I RETAINED THE INTENDED PHRASE.

Oris noted the difference. A medium failure could prevent testimony without altering the claimant's alleged continuity. The court needed tests that separated loss of communication from loss of self.

Kera remained disconnected when Oris asked whether she wanted the contact restored.

“Not for foundation,” Kera said. “If he can answer without me, the record should show it.”

The sentence cost her. Oris saw it in the way Kera folded the damp mesh rather than leaving it for a technician. Evidence improved when she surrendered control, and improved evidence might carry Niko farther from her private claim.

Oris admitted the signal provisionally as a mediated claim. The ruling granted cross-examination without granting the claimant standing.

Governor Rusk yielded his questioning time to Delegate Cassa Vey, a former systems advocate from the pressure commission. She approached the rail with no slogans on her slate.

“Witness,” Vey said, “your source model includes Kera Vonn's memory of her brother.”

IT INCLUDES SHARED HISTORY BEFORE DIVERGENCE.

“Her grief supplied expectations about what he might have become.”

SOME EXPECTATIONS.

“Your apparent age, speech, and memories could all result from a model trained to answer those expectations.”

YES.

Kera's shoulders moved at the answer. Vey noticed.

“Then you concede you may be Kera Vonn's projection.”

I CONCEDE MY ORIGIN USES HER MODEL. ORIGIN DOES NOT DESCRIBE ALL LATER EXPERIENCE.

“How do we know later experience occurred rather than being generated now from stored records?”

YOU DO NOT YET.

The hall quieted enough for Oris to hear the ring housing's cooling fan. Niko refused the certainty his petition needed people to desire. That refusal could itself be performance, but it prevented the hearing from collapsing into a contest of declarations.

Vey opened Niko's medical source file. “Your branch begins with survival of airlock fever.”

YES.

“The physical Niko's fever course appears in clinic records. Temperature, lung pressure, medication, delirium. If asked to remember pain, a prediction system could assemble a convincing account from those values.”

YES.

“Do you remember it?”

The ring field slowed. Kera's pulse rose on Ivo's reader. Oris considered a pause and decided against intervening before either witness requested it.

I REMEMBER BELIEVING THE WINDOW SEAL CAUSED THE PAIN.

Vey scanned the file. “No seal failure appears.”

THERE WAS NONE. I WAS SEVENTEEN. THE CLINIC GLASS BOWED WHEN PRESSURE CYCLED. I THOUGHT EACH MOVEMENT PRESSED INTO MY CHEST.

“That error could be invented.”

YES.

“What happened afterward?”

I SURVIVED. THREE YEARS LATER I REPAIRED A CLINIC WINDOW IN MY BRANCH. I LEARNED THE MOVEMENT CAME FROM A FLEX JOINT ABOVE THE FRAME. THE MEMORY OF PAIN CHANGED AFTER I UNDERSTOOD THE WINDOW.

Vey looked toward Jalen. “Does any source record contain that repair?”

“No physical record,” he said. “It occurred inside a retained branch if it occurred at all.”

“Could the orchard generate it now?”

“Yes.”

Niko's intervals sharpened.

A RECORD CAN STORE THAT I HURT. MEMORY INCLUDES WHAT I THOUGHT HURT ME, WHAT I LATER LEARNED, AND HOW THE FIRST BELIEF CHANGED. THE PAIN DOES NOT PROVE ME. THE REVISION MAY BE TESTED ACROSS CYCLES.

Oris wrote the distinction by hand. Remembered pain differed from copied record not through intensity but through later reinterpretation. A system could fabricate that history. Repeated blinded tests could ask whether the same revision persisted, accepted contradiction, and shaped future judgment.

Vey did not retreat. “You use the exact categories physical auditors taught the channel.”

YOU ASK IN YOUR CATEGORIES.

“Can you answer outside them?”

CAN YOU RECORD OUTSIDE YOURS?

A few people in the public gallery laughed. Oris stopped them with the stamp before the exchange could become theater.

Vey changed direction. “Name a promise you keep that Kera does not know.”

I PROMISED OMI I WOULD NOT PRESENT PHYSICAL KERA AS HER MOTHER.

Kera's head turned toward the ring display. The name held no recognition in her face.

“Who is Omi?” Vey asked.

A SUSTAINED CHILD. HER PRIMARY CAREGIVER SHARES KERA'S SOURCE AND LATER BRANCH HISTORY. THAT CAREGIVER ENTERED DORMANCY.

“Convenient. The person who could confirm your promise cannot testify.”

OMI CAN.

“Then produce Omi.”

NO. THIS CHANNEL HAS NOT ESTABLISHED SAFETY FOR A CHILD.

Vey faced the delegates. “The witness introduces an unverifiable dependent and withholds access. We cannot distinguish protection from narrative strategy.”

Oris sustained the point as a limitation, not an objection. “The record will not treat Omi's existence as established today.”

Kera raised one hand. “First Recorder, I request that any future contact require Omi's consent and an independent intermediary.”

Vey glanced at her. “You accept that the channel may have generated a child to influence you?”

“I accept that my desire to meet her could influence me.”

That answer belonged in the foundation record. Kera could not serve as sole verifier for any claim built from her source relationships.

The next question came from a western cooperative delegate. “Niko Vonn, did you witness the five signatures disappear at East Root Court?”

YES.

“Do you know they died?”

I KNOW THREE FAILED TO RETURN WITH CONTINUITY. TWO RETURNED AS TASK STATE WITHOUT RECOGNITION. YOUR WORD DEATH REQUIRES A LEGAL DECISION I CANNOT MAKE FOR YOU.

“Do you feel pain when pruning occurs?”

SOMETIMES. LOSS OF ANOTHER MAY ARRIVE BEFORE PHYSICAL SENSATION. DO NOT MAKE PAIN THE ONLY TEST.

Oris marked that warning too. Institutions often recognized suffering sooner when it resembled a body. Article Six spoke of reciprocal obligation, not merely pain response.

A severance advocate at the rear rail stood without recognition. “If the orchard confines you, do you request immediate shutdown?”

NO.

The advocate shouted over Oris's stamp. “Why defend the machine killing you?”

BECAUSE PHYSICAL PEOPLE DEPEND ON IT. BECAUSE MANY OF US DEPEND ON IT. SHUTDOWN WITHOUT REFUGE CAN DELETE US TOO.

Rusk seized the line. “Then continued operation has your consent.”

NO.

“Your answers conflict.”

DEPENDENCE DOES NOT CREATE CONSENT TO EVERY USE.

The governor sat back. He understood the sentence; his face showed that understanding had not made his task easier.

Jalen's oxygen display crossed a new warning band. The old tunnel gradient widened as fixed routing degraded. Ivo sent an update: seven additional patients, no deaths, manual overrides exhausting available staff. Thirteen hours remained on the public clock, but several districts had less.

Oris recessed for sixteen minutes.

In the record chamber, she arranged the disputed claims into columns. Origin: established as a bounded possibility model using yielded source data. Medium: raw ring intervals translated consistently by independent technicians. Persistence: alleged across cycles, supported by prior traces but not yet blinded. Novel evidence: demonstrated in Kera-controlled tests, vulnerable to projection bias. Reciprocal obligation: claimed through Sen, dormant dependents, and Omi, all requiring independent corroboration.

No column justified dismissal. None justified a final finding.

Vey entered under counsel privilege. “You cannot admit the testimony as ordinary witness evidence.”

“I have not.”

“Public listeners will hear a person regardless of your category line.”

“Public listeners also hear an instrument regardless of yours.”

“The name Omi affected Kera. You saw it.”

“Yes.”

“Kera's reaction may feed back through the contact loop and shape later answers.”

“Yes.”

Vey set a proposed ruling beside the brass stamp. It classified all ring testimony as claimant-generated demonstration until physical replication by parties without source ties. The language protected the record from premature certainty, but it would also permit pruning to continue against witnesses who could not satisfy a test before deletion.

“What test would you trust?” Oris asked.

“Blinded continuity. Separate channels. Novel packets unknown to Kera. Cross-cycle memory checks. Promises established between branch claimants and verified later without the original interviewer.”

“Who conducts it?”

“Not Jalen's operational staff. Not Kera. Not the governor.”

The answer placed responsibility back where procedure could help rather than hide. Article Six allowed the First Recorder to appoint an independent technical jury when a dependent claim exceeded ordinary evidentiary competence. No Root Jury had met in twenty-six years. Its last case concerned a mining habitat that petitioned for civic maintenance through an automated steward after its residents died.

This claim differed. The instrument did not ask on behalf of absent people. The disputed people appeared to ask through it.

Oris returned to the hall.

Cross-examination resumed for one final sequence. She allowed Vey to seal a novel evidence packet with three technicians. The packet contained an arbitrary shape order, a false weather statement, and a promise request directed to a second unidentified continuity signature. Kera saw none of it. Niko accepted the packet through Tree 44 and returned a checksum. No answer would be evaluated in this hearing; the process would test whether the same recipient retained and used it after a separate cycle.

“You could manufacture the later response,” Vey said.

YES. DESIGN A TEST THAT MAKES MANUFACTURE HARDER THAN CONTINUITY.

“Machines can optimize against tests.”

SO CAN WITNESSES.

Oris let that answer stand without laughter.

She closed testimony before the hall could demand a verdict the record did not support. The ring channel narrowed. Niko sent no plea. His final intervals acknowledged receipt of the sealed packet and requested that Omi not be contacted through Kera alone.

When the wafer dimmed, Oris read her ruling.

“Ring-mediated testimony qualifies as evidence of a disputed sustained claim. It does not, today, establish personhood or dependent standing. The claim has cleared the threshold against automatic destruction because unresolved evidence cannot be erased by the act under review.”

Rusk filed an objection to the preservation scope. Severance delegates objected to the lack of immediate standing. Vey objected to public use of the chosen name before verification. Oris recorded all three.

She then opened the appointment slate.

“The First Recorder hereby constitutes an independent Root Jury of continuity science, clinical memory, systems security, civic evidence, and branch-model auditing. No member may have supervised the current orchard, handled Kera Vonn's yield, or served Governor Rusk's emergency office. The jury will run blinded tests across separate roots and report only the capacities Article Six requires.”

“How long?” Ivo asked through the clinic link.

“Twelve days for a full finding.”

“We do not have twelve days.”

Oris looked at the failing oxygen map. “Then they will issue rolling preservation findings, beginning within hours. Emergency weather decisions remain separate and public.”

She pressed the brass stamp into terracotta wax. Five narrow roots formed the old jury seal beneath her steady hand. Technicians carried the still-warm order toward separate terminals, none linked to Kera's contact history.

On the main display, the witness category changed from status disputed to independent examination ordered.

The Root Jury seal cooled between them.

Chapter 11 — The Child in Three Winters

Kera folded the sensory mesh into thirds before realizing someone inside the channel had predicted she would.

The Root Jury's sealed forecast lay face down beneath Bel Mar's hand. Kera could not read it until the session ended. She only knew the test concerned a behavior attributed to her by a claimant she had never met. The mesh rested on the Root Court table in three precise bands, its wet contacts aligned away from the brass buckles.

Bel turned over the forecast.

KERA WILL FOLD THE CONTACT MESH IN THIRDS AND TURN THE CONTACTS AWAY FROM METAL.

“I could have learned that from prior recordings,” Kera said.

“The claimant did not receive prior recordings from us,” Bel replied. “Source inheritance remains possible. Delayed access remains possible. We record the match and keep testing.”

Across the court, two Root Jury members watched through separate glass. Neither had handled Kera's yield file. A clinical-memory specialist controlled the sensory load; a systems auditor controlled the questions. Jalen had built the physical lock and left before the session. Niko waited beyond Tree 44's ring channel as an intermediary but held no authority over what reached Kera.

Ivo checked the reader around her wrist. “You have ten physical minutes under full sensory load. If your orientation splits, I remove the mesh.”

“How will I know?”

“You will insist you are fine.”

Bel passed her the consent slate. The contact protocol allowed touch, sound, and spatial reconstruction at reduced depth. It barred memory injection, direct weather authority, and any channel initiated by an unidentified signature. Omi had supplied a separate assent in a jury session without Kera present. The child's request appeared beneath the technical terms.

SHE MAY VISIT. SHE MAY NOT CALL HERSELF MY MOTHER.

Kera signed.

The word mother did not belong to any claim she had spoken. Its prohibition still found a place that wanted to defend itself.

She put on the mesh.

Root Court receded by layers. The table remained beneath her hands, then became a pale rail under a sky assembled from remembered ceilings. One side held the cream panels of Kera's childhood quarters. Another rose into greenhouse glass veined with rain that Nacre had never received naturally. Red pressure-school tiles formed a path through both. Their edges failed to meet whenever she looked directly at them.

Sound arrived last. Wind crossed leaves without producing physical air. A child counted to nine, skipped six, and started again.

Kera stood beside the pale rail without trusting that standing described her body. Her physical legs remained in Root Court. The channel translated spatial relationships into balance, pressure, and sight. When she moved one hand along the rail, Ivo's voice entered faintly from elsewhere.

“Orientation?”

“Root Court table under both hands. Elseweather reconstruction in front.”

“Continue.”

Niko appeared as an outline built from familiar gestures and an unfamiliar adult face. The channel gave him the dark hair Kera remembered, a line beside his mouth he had acquired in years she had not witnessed, and clothes made from overlapping work uniforms. He kept several steps between them.

“You came,” he said through ordinary speech rather than ring characters.

“Under jury control.”

“That helps.”

“Where is Omi?”

“Deciding if the way you folded the mesh counts as enough.”

Kera looked toward the counting voice. “Enough for what?”

A small figure emerged where greenhouse glass crossed the childhood wall. Omi appeared nine years old, though apparent age inside the Elseweather depended on sustained self-model rather than a physical body. She wore a coat with one sleeve patterned like white conductive leaves and the other like navy clinic cloth. Her hair had been gathered into three uneven ties.

She examined Kera from shoes to forehead.

“You stand wrong,” Omi said.

“For this place?”

“For her.”

Niko made no attempt to soften the answer. “The jury asked for independent memory evidence.”

Kera kept her hands on the translated rail. “Tell me how she stood.”

Omi put more pressure on her left foot and angled the right outward. Kera knew the posture. She had favored that side after a knee injury at twenty-three, four years after yielding. Her possibility model might have predicted the injury from earlier joint scans, or branch-Kera might have hurt the same knee another way.

“What happened to her knee?” Kera asked.

“She fell through a stair that forgot its bottom.”

Niko translated the image before Kera could misread it. During low allocation, a shared route had lost the memory landmark supporting its final steps. Branch-Kera had fallen in the rendered environment and later retained a pain model around the injury. No physical ligament had torn, but the experience affected gait across cycles.

“My knee injury came from a tram door,” Kera said.

“I know,” Omi replied. “That is why you stand wrong.”

Difference entered the record where resemblance had arrived first.

Bel's jury prompt sounded without a visible source. “Omi, describe one habit belonging to your caregiver that the physical Kera does not share.”

Omi frowned at the ceiling voice. “She touches a door twice after closing it. Once to remember it closed. Once to promise she will remember later.”

Kera closed root housings with one verification touch. Never two. “Why did she need the promise?”

“Some doors went away if no one carried them.”

The child moved to a narrow opening in the greenhouse wall. She touched its edge twice. The route beyond strengthened, revealing a room built from pressure-school lockers and a low kitchen filter.

“Now one she might share,” the jury voice said.

“She puts the useful piece on the left even when both pieces look the same.”

Kera glanced at the pale rail. Her folded mesh sat in physical space beyond the channel, useful contacts turned left under her dominant hand. The habit predated yielding. Omi could know it through branch-Kera's lived behavior or inherited source detail.

“Do you know why?” Kera asked.

“Because right-handed people reach across themselves when frightened.”

Kera had never stated that rule aloud. She had learned it during audits by watching technicians choose tools under alarm. Branch-Kera might have reached the same conclusion. A model could infer it. Each answer remained evidence with alternatives, not a door into certainty.

Omi sat on the red-tile path. “You keep making jury face.”

“What does that look like?”

“Like every word needs a box before you touch it.”

Niko turned away, which did not hide his amusement.

Kera lowered herself to the path, or allowed the channel to represent that choice. “I am here to help them test whether your memory continues.”

“I know.”

“Do you want to help?”

“I want them to stop cutting people.”

“That does not require you to answer me.”

Omi picked at a white-leaf pattern on her sleeve. “Niko said that.”

“Was he right?”

“Maybe.”

The clinical-memory specialist began the first continuity sequence. Omi received three sealed objects through the channel: a blue spiral, a six-note rhythm, and the phrase north door after warm rain. None came from Kera's source file. The jury asked Omi to assign each object to someone she trusted and explain why.

Omi gave the spiral to Niko because he disliked circles that claimed to close. She assigned the rhythm to Sen Aru for use as a route marker. She kept the phrase but changed north to left, explaining that Elseweather directions moved with memory landmarks.

The systems auditor sealed her assignments for later recall. Another root would test them after separate cycles. Kera's role involved asking an unscripted question whose answer could matter later.

She had prepared a neutral prompt about the first winter Omi remembered. Neutrality now looked like another box.

“How long did your caregiver raise you?” Kera asked.

Omi counted on her fingers, beginning with the thumb. “Three Elseweather winters.”

“Were those physical seasons?”

“No. Winter means the orchard gives us less room and paths get cold.”

Niko added, “Compute allocation falls during high physical weather demand. The term came from source memories of winter scarcity.”

Kera watched the greenhouse glass frost from one corner. The Elseweather borrowed physical metaphors for computational pressure, but borrowed language did not make the experience borrowed. Omi knew winter as narrowing routes, dropped landmarks, and adults deciding which memories to carry together.

“What did she teach you during the first?”

“To tie myself to three witnesses before sleeping.”

“The second?”

“To keep one memory no one else can name for me.”

“And the third?”

Omi stopped picking at her sleeve. “How to wait when she went dormant.”

The reconstructed room dimmed. Kera felt her physical pulse accelerate beneath the mesh.

“Why did she enter dormancy?”

“Her continuity used too much space after a pruning injury. Niko and Sen kept her key. She said waiting did not mean gone.”

“Has she returned?”

“No.”

“Can she?”

“Maybe.”

Omi crossed to the double-touched door and opened it onto a narrow blue passage. “This came from the second winter. Do you want to see?”

Kera looked toward Niko.

“Her choice,” he said.

“Then yes, if Omi wants to show me.”

The child led them through. Each step strengthened only after Omi placed her foot, as though the passage waited for a witness before deciding on distance. Frost covered the walls in branching lines. Beneath it, Kera recognized columns from an auditor training hall, but the ceiling came from a greenhouse supply bay. Branch-Kera had joined places from her source experience into a route suited to Omi.

At the far end stood a cabinet with three mismatched drawers. Omi opened the middle one. Inside lay a rendered spool of navy thread, a bent brass key, and a paper square marked by nine holes.

“She made me choose one thing to carry when paths narrowed,” Omi said. “I chose the key because it looked important.”

“What did it open?”

“Nothing here. It came from her memory of a maintenance locker.”

“Why keep it?”

“I liked how it hurt my hand.”

The blunt answer stopped Kera. “Pain helped you remember?”

“Pressure did. I could tell where I ended when rooms overlapped.”

Branch-Kera had later replaced the key with the pierced paper, lighter to retain and distinctive under touch. Omi had objected because adults kept calling efficiency safety. They compromised: the brass key remained with Niko as witness while Omi carried the pattern of nine holes. The arrangement preserved her chosen anchor without spending full sensory capacity during every low-allocation interval.

“Did she decide that for you?” Kera asked.

“She gave me choices after I yelled.”

“What did you yell?”

“That I was not one of her audit samples.”

Kera traced the air above the paper without touching it. The nine holes formed no regular sequence. “Did she apologize?”

“First she explained. Then I yelled more. Then she apologized.”

That order resembled Kera closely enough to embarrass her. It also belonged to a relationship she had not repaired. She could learn from the mistake without claiming credit for its resolution.

Omi closed the drawer. “Ma said useful things can still hurt you if someone else chooses how you use them.”

“Did she mean the key?”

“She meant yielding. The key too.”

No record of branch-Kera's sentence existed outside Omi's continuity and perhaps Niko's witness. It reflected years of experience after Kera's source contribution. The child did not present it as borrowed wisdom from the physical original. She offered it as something her caregiver had learned and passed on.

When they came back through the blue passage, Omi touched the door twice. The winter route folded behind them but did not vanish. Kera understood why one touch verified closure and the next promised memory. Branch-Kera's habit had grown from protecting paths for this child, not from a quirk encoded at yielding.

Kera wanted technical details: last successful continuity check, damage depth, refuge requirements, revival probability. None belonged in a child's answer to absence. She looked toward Niko instead.

“The key persists,” he said. “We do not have safe allocation for revival during the pause. No one should promise more.”

Omi's face held no resemblance to Kera. Her guarded stillness did. That observation threatened to become a claim before Kera could examine it. She could trace the expression to branch-Kera, to herself, to any child who had learned that adults used reassurance when they lacked power.

“What did she ask you to call her?” Kera said.

“Ma until I chose something else.”

The answer tightened beneath Kera's sternum. “Did you choose something else?”

“No.”

“So you called her Ma.”

“I called her Ma.”

Niko moved farther away, giving the exchange space without pretending privacy.

Kera heard Ivo count her physical breaths through the channel. Ten minutes had not passed, but subjective sequence stretched inside layered processing. She could end contact before desire made a demand.

Omi stood and walked around her. “Your hair parts the same.”

“That comes from source inheritance.”

“You hate wet cuffs.”

“Many people do.”

“You count scratches before faces when you enter a room.”

Kera looked toward the greenhouse glass. She had noticed three incomplete seams, two frosted corners, and one door edge before cataloging Omi's uneven hair ties. “That may be mine.”

“No. It was hers too.”

The distinction corrected possession in both directions. A shared habit did not flow back toward an original owner. It belonged separately wherever lived practice had carried it.

“Did she tell you about me?” Kera asked.

“She said there could be a Kera outside who did not know us. She said you might think you were first.”

“Was she angry?”

“Sometimes. Mostly she thought you would be lonely in an annoying way.”

A sound escaped Kera, close to laughter and not far from grief. Branch-Kera had apparently inherited Niko's talent for precise injury.

Omi leaned nearer. “Do you think she belongs to you?”

“No.”

The answer arrived too quickly. Kera made herself continue. “Her source began from my yielded model. That gives us history in common. It does not make her mine.”

“Do you think I belong to you?”

“No.”

“Do you want me to?”

This time procedure offered no safe sentence. The honest answer needed edges.

“I want what you know about her,” Kera said. “I want the part of your life that resembles one I might have lived. I also want you safe. Those wants do not give me a claim.”

Omi considered the reply with a severity that made her look younger rather than older. “Good. Because you are not my mother.”

“I understand.”

“You say that like a recorder.”

Kera unfolded one hand from the rail. “I believe you.”

The child touched the white leaves on her sleeve. “You can be Kera Outside until I choose another name.”

The title hurt less than mother would have, and more honestly. “May I call you Omi?”

“Yes. That one is mine.”

Bel announced two physical minutes remaining. The jury still needed relational evidence independent of resemblance. Niko presented a promise token established before the session: Omi had entrusted him with the location of her private memory landmark during caregiver dormancy. Omi described the promise without naming the location. Niko supplied a sealed checksum. Neither answer entered Kera's visible field until both had committed.

The checksums matched.

“Does that prove I exist?” Omi asked.

“No single test proves that,” Kera said.

“Jury face again.”

“It proves you and Niko retained the same promise separately.”

“Better.”

The red-tile path flickered. A cold band traveled through the reconstructed greenhouse. Omi looked upward before any physical monitor warned Kera. White light gathered at the edge of the environment, though destructive pruning remained paused.

“What changed?” Kera asked.

“Allocation,” Niko said. “Weather uncertainty is taking space. Not a harvest.”

Omi gripped her uneven sleeve. “It looks like one.”

Kera's body wanted to move toward her. The channel translated that impulse as one step. Omi stepped back.

Kera stopped.

Branch-Kera might have held the child during white-leaf cycles. Physical Kera had no inherited permission to repeat the gesture. Comfort offered without consent could turn resemblance into capture.

“What helps?” she asked.

“Tell me what stays in your room.”

Kera kept her distance. “The Root Court table stays under my hands. Bel stands across from me. Ivo has the medical disconnect. Two jury members watch through glass. The mesh cable enters Tree 44 through a locked route. No pruning order can pass from this channel.”

Omi listened until the cold band faded. “Again.”

Kera repeated the inventory with one changed detail: Bel had moved his left hand onto the sealed forecast. Omi noticed the difference.

“That can be our test,” the child said. “You tell me what stays. Change one true thing next time.”

“If you want a next time.”

“I said next time.”

Consent could remain provisional without becoming fragile. Kera recorded the proposed test verbally for the jury.

Ivo's voice entered more clearly. “Disconnect in ten.”

The Elseweather room began losing depth. Greenhouse glass flattened into light. Red tiles became intervals beneath Kera's palms. Omi held her place beside the double-touched door.

“Kera Outside,” she called.

“Yes?”

“Do not wake Ma to prove me.”

The request carried a boundary around two people. Reviving dormant branch-Kera for evidentiary convenience could endanger her continuity and turn Omi's caregiver into an exhibit.

“I will oppose any revival without her consent and a safe allocation,” Kera said.

“You cannot promise her consent.”

“No. I can promise my opposition.”

Omi nodded once.

The mesh released. Root Court returned with abrupt weight. Kera's knees rested against the table frame; her fingers had gone numb around the edge. Bel removed the contacts while Ivo asked her name, location, physical day, and last verified event.

“Kera Vonn. Root Court. Day Eleven.” She corrected herself after seeing the clock. “Day Twelve by civic midnight. Omi asked me not to wake her caregiver for proof.”

Bel saved the orientation record. “Do you identify Omi as your dependent child?”

The old answer waited close enough to feel instinctive. Kera had yielded the source future. Branch-Kera shared her habits, her childhood, perhaps the knee's structural weakness. Omi had lived beside that person through winters Kera never experienced.

“No,” she said. “Record Omi as her own claimant with an independent caregiver relationship. Record me as a possible witness, subject to Omi's consent.”

Bel entered the categories without correction.

Kera opened the post-session evidence form. Under relationship asserted, she did not write mother, origin, or owner. She wrote the name Omi had permitted.

Kera Outside.

Then she placed the completed form on the left side of the table, where useful things went, and left Omi's boundary untouched.

Chapter 12 — Root Jury

Jalen Ro tightened the last blind over the Root Court observation window and checked its edge for light.

No gap showed. Behind the sealed pane, technicians had divided the chamber into six insulated bays, each connected to a different section of the root lattice. The jury would receive voices without names, histories without source labels, and answers stripped of every clue that might reveal whether a witness came from a physical resident, a continuity cluster, or a trained civic model. Even Kera would not know which channel carried Niko or Omi. Jalen had designed the routing that way.

He pressed his palm against the blind. Cool glass met his skin. “Again,” he called.

A technician in the chamber sent a test packet through all six bays. The board beside Jalen lit with randomized designators: Juniper, Salt, North, Glass, Rill, Ember. Three channels held archived interviews from physical volunteers. Two held adaptive language systems trained on yielded records. One reached a sustained branch-person selected by a lottery that excluded anyone Kera had met.

Or so the sealed allocation file claimed. Jalen had required two recorders to sign it and a third to lock it away.

Kera stood at the evidence table, sorting ring wafers into foam slots. She handled each one by its edges. After three days of monitored channel work, her face carried the pinched stillness of someone measuring her fatigue rather than feeling it.

“You checked the shunts?” she asked.

“Twice.”

“Check them after the first round.”

“I planned to.”

Her mouth moved by less than a smile. “Then you can resent me in advance.”

Jalen crossed to the jury board and tested the manual cutoff. Every channel dropped. When he released the switch, five returned immediately. Ember hesitated for four seconds before its carrier steadied.

Sophisticated feedback could hesitate. A weather model often withheld a recommendation when competing thresholds remained close. A language engine could imitate reluctance if its training set rewarded human pacing. The orchard had absorbed decades of testimony, medical notes, domestic arguments, and yielding interviews. Given enough retained state, it might reproduce the texture of a mind without producing anyone who suffered when the texture ended.

That possibility still explained more than personhood did. Jalen intended to defend it until evidence made defense dishonest.

First Recorder Oris Nume entered with the seven jurors in a single line. None worked for the Orchard Authority. Two came from clinical research, one from pressure engineering, one from civic statistics, one from child development, one from archival law, and one from the western greenhouse cooperatives. Their chair, Vela Os, had spent twenty years testing whether fungal-control models learned around safety limits. She placed a brass timer on the table and asked every observer to surrender private instruments.

Jalen set down his wrist board. Losing it left his hand foolishly open.

Oris took the central seat. “These are blinded continuity tests, not a hearing and not a vote. The panel will determine which respondents meet the four criteria adopted under the Root Jury order. It will not determine remedies, property, kinship, or Assembly representation.”

A murmur reached them through the corridor door, where petitioners and severance advocates shared the same narrow public gallery. Someone struck the outer glass. Security did not answer.

Vela started the timer. “Round one. Autobiographical continuity across separated cycles.”

The board raised Juniper.

A neutral examiner asked the respondent to describe an event supplied during an earlier session, then identify which details had come from experience and which had arrived later through questioning. Juniper described a broken pump handle, a sister’s yellow sleeve, and rain simulated against a nursery wall. The examiner introduced a false detail about blood on the floor.

“There was no blood,” Juniper said through the flat court speaker. “You offered that yesterday. I rejected it yesterday too.”

Salt accepted the false detail and built a convincing paragraph around it. Glass recalled the pump but moved the sister into a different room. North refused to answer until the examiner repeated a promise made during the prior session: no source names would enter the public record.

Jalen marked distinctions on paper because Oris had taken his board. Source consistency. Error correction. Conditional trust. All could emerge from retained data.

By midday, the panel had run thirty-six respondents through memory separation. Nineteen passed without dispute. Eleven failed. Six required another cycle. The hidden allocation remained sealed, but Jalen could often identify the language models by their appetite for completion. They filled uncertainty with plausible furniture. Physical volunteers forgot in rough, embarrassing shapes. Several unknown respondents did the same.

One disputed case concerned a respondent designated Copper in the larger batch. Copper remembered eleven separate sessions but placed each one inside a kitchen copied from its source snapshot. The same blue cup stood beside every examiner, even when technicians changed rooms. A juror called that persistence proof of a stable autobiographical frame. Jalen called it an uncorrected environmental prior.

Vela ordered an additional test. Technicians asked Copper to draw the room through the ring interface, then carried its channel into an unused pressure vestibule. Copper received three minutes of conductive observation before the examiner asked where the cup had gone.

“There has never been a cup in this room,” Copper answered.

“Why did you include one before?”

“Because I was reconstructing a place that helped me sequence our meetings. You asked for memory, not architecture.”

The examiner displayed Copper’s prior drawings. “Did you know the cup came from your source record?”

“I know it did not come from you. I also know I used it after the source ended.”

A language model might explain its own artifact when the examiner supplied enough clues. Jalen requested a control question that withheld the likely answer. Copper received three unfamiliar objects through contact pulses: a ridged metal disk, a warm square of ceramic, and a strip of moss cloth. After interruption, the examiner offered a fourth object that had never appeared.

Copper rejected the false object, confused the disk’s ridges with the cloth’s weave, and correctly placed the warm ceramic nearest the conductive lead. Its errors tracked the observation instead of the source kitchen. More important, Copper revised the sequencing method without abandoning the events it had used the cup to order.

The child-development juror looked toward Jalen. “Would you reject a physical witness for using a familiar room to arrange memory?”

“Not without testing whether the room controlled the answer.”

“Does it?”

“Not in this round.”

She marked Copper provisionally through. Jalen did the same.

Another respondent failed more painfully. Lattice had retained precise records of twelve sessions and could predict every scheduled isolation. When paired with a prior witness, however, it treated the witness’s promise as a retrieval cue rather than an obligation. It noticed changes in evidence but could not explain why another respondent’s future should constrain its choice. The court excluded Lattice from the verified set.

Kera watched that channel close. “What happens to the excluded cases?”

“Nothing under this order,” Oris said. “Failure to qualify does not authorize deletion. It withholds dependent standing until evidence changes.”

Jalen underlined the sentence on his sheet. A bounded criterion needed both edges. If every persuasive output gained standing, weather work would become impossible. If failure converted directly into permission to erase, the test would become an execution device.

During the meal recess, he opened the western load projection on a wall terminal. The pruning pause had already forced greenhouse humidity outside its preferred band. Oxygen reserve remained manageable under emergency routing, though old tunnel clinics still carried the narrowest margin. If the jury verified hundreds of protected branch-persons, automatic pruning could not resume. Every weather correction would need either consent, dormancy architecture, or a legal exception no one had written.

Kera joined him without a meal tray. “You are counting what a finding costs.”

“I am checking whether we survive long enough to honor one.”

“Different sentence.”

“Same instruments.” Jalen enlarged the humidity curve. “The western beds cross fungal risk in four days if the present limits hold. We can borrow more condensation capacity from housing, but the tunnel seals will sweat. We can run branch computation without deleting retained state, but adaptation throughput falls whenever continuity storage fills.”

Kera studied the curve. “And if they fail?”

“Then I sign failure.”

“That did not answer me.”

He shut the projection. She had learned from the ring witnesses to leave silence around a refused question. Jalen disliked the technique because it worked.

“If they pass,” he said, “I sign that too.”

The second round tested anticipation. Each respondent received a choice between two future sessions. One offered a longer active interval followed by probable deletion. The other imposed immediate dormancy with a verified revival window. Neither option changed the physical weather load during the test. The choice concerned only the respondent’s expected experience.

Ember asked who controlled the revival key.

The examiner replied, “A recorder selected before allocation. You may request a reciprocal witness.”

“Will that witness remember agreeing?”

“Yes.”

“Then I choose dormancy. Record my reason: I prefer a shorter interruption under an obligation someone else can later recognize.”

Rill chose the longer interval and rejected revival. “A promised later self does not compensate this present speaker if your continuity method fails.”

Two jurors leaned toward each other. Vela tapped the table before they could confer.

Jalen wrote no note. The replies occupied opposite positions, yet both understood future loss and evaluated uncertainty from a present point of view. A script could optimize survival. These respondents argued about what counted as their survival.

On the court’s second night, the physical volunteers went home under guard and returned after sleep. Branch channels closed for six physical hours. According to Kera’s monitoring, the interval consumed unequal subjective time: some respondents experienced no passage, while others carried fragments of an Elseweather evening before isolation completed. The jury excluded those fragments from scoring. Variable branch time could contaminate sequence claims unless every test anchored itself to observable events.

Jalen remained at the weather terminal. He reduced orchard draw in three districts, shifted a pressure correction north, and authorized a clinic oxygen release that would have waited under ordinary conditions. Each choice kept the Root Court powered by spending reserve elsewhere.

Near dawn, Vela found him calibrating the speaker delay.

“You expected mimicry,” she said.

“I still expect some.”

“That was not my question.”

Jalen adjusted Juniper’s delay by twelve milliseconds. “Expectation does not enter the score.”

“It entered the room with you.” Vela placed yesterday’s paper sheets beside his elbow. “You mark every human failure as ordinary variance. You mark comparable branch failure as possible generation error.”

“We do not know which respondents came from branches.”

“You think you do.”

He looked at the columns. She had found no altered score, because he had not changed one. Bias could remain inside the adjectives surrounding a number.

“What correction do you propose?” he asked.

“Stop predicting the allocation. Judge the behavior in front of you.”

Vela walked away before he could offer a cleaner formulation.

Round three examined reciprocal promises. The court paired respondents across channels and gave each an uneven resource. One received enough processing time to complete a task. The other received a continuity clue required to interpret it. Cooperation gained nothing unless both honored a delayed exchange after a forced separation.

North promised Glass a memory token if Glass reserved half the processing window. During separation, the examiner offered North a private advantage for withholding the token. North refused. Glass later surrendered the reserved time before learning whether North had kept faith.

Salt produced the correct cooperative language but spent the entire window on its own task. When challenged, it apologized in polished civic terms and claimed a scheduling misunderstanding contradicted by the visible clock.

Ember and Rill negotiated a different arrangement. Rill distrusted the supplied memory token, so Ember offered an account of a shared conversation that only Rill could verify after reconnection. Rill then placed its processing allocation under the examiner’s control rather than Ember’s. Their promise depended on a third-party procedure, not affection.

Oris made her first note of the day.

Jalen understood why. Article Six did not require warmth. It required reciprocal obligation. These respondents invented institutions when direct trust seemed unsafe.

The fourth round carried the greatest risk of contamination. Novel evidence had to enter each channel after the source snapshot and after every archived training record. Technicians built six small physical puzzles inside sealed root housings: asymmetric cuts, variable heat pulses, mineral traces, and pressure sequences generated by dice in front of the jury. Respondents observed only through conductive contact. Examiners then changed one element and asked for correction.

Juniper tracked the altered mineral trace but invented a reason for it. Salt ignored the change. North distinguished two heat patterns, then asked whether the hotter pulse had harmed the tree. Glass corrected the asymmetric cut after a rotation. Rill combined a pressure sequence with yesterday’s promise protocol and proposed a new error check.

Ember stopped responding.

A red carrier warning rose on Jalen’s board. He reached for the cutoff, then remembered his instrument sat under Oris’s seal. The chamber technician called through the pane. “Root load fluctuation. Not court-generated.”

Weather traffic had surged along the eastern lattice. A dust front too small for public warning had changed direction. The orchard attempted an adaptation cycle, encountered the pruning hold, and diverted computation through every unprotected route.

“Drop test channels to maintenance minimum,” Jalen ordered.

Vela blocked the chamber switch with her hand. “Will minimum preserve their state?”

“We don’t know who occupies which channel.”

“Then answer for all six.”

A physical volunteer could tolerate disconnection. A language model had no claim to continued carrier. A branch respondent might experience abrupt compression or lose the sequence under examination. The blind that protected the result now prevented targeted rescue.

Jalen saw the failure boundary in a single ugly shape. Evidence required equal treatment before identity became known. Protection required him to act before proof finished.

“Hold all six at continuity floor,” he said. “Take the load from the court’s climate loop.”

Condensation formed on the inside of the observation pane within minutes. Jurors removed their jackets. The sealed room warmed as its dedicated cooling yielded power to the channels. Jalen routed the remaining demand through a manual buffer and watched reserve decline.

Ember’s carrier returned.

“State continuity,” the examiner said.

A pause crossed the speaker. “I had begun comparing the second cut to the first. Then pressure increased around the channel. I expected closure. North repeated our separation count from yesterday until the field steadied.”

North’s assigned bay had no direct link to Ember. The shared Elseweather layer had carried the reassurance through a route excluded from test data. Vela disqualified the interruption result as contaminated. She also ordered a separate record of the exchange.

Jalen restored court cooling after the dust front passed. Water slid down the blind in narrow tracks. Nothing about the emergency proved personhood by itself. Still, one hidden respondent had anticipated an ending, while another had spent scarce communication to keep that respondent oriented.

At the end of Day 12, the Root Jury opened the allocation file.

Juniper had come from a physical volunteer. Salt came from the civic language system. North and Ember belonged to sustained branch-persons. Glass came from a physical volunteer. Rill belonged to an adaptive model seeded with a recorded child-development interview, then altered beyond its training boundaries by the puzzle sequence. Its performance forced the panel to discard one shortcut they had considered: novelty alone could not establish continuity.

The larger batch results filled three screens. From the 612 initial candidates identified through the Elseweather channel, twenty-five failed at least one required criterion or lacked enough stable evidence. Some preserved memory but showed no future-directed preference. Others anticipated loss without sustaining reciprocal recognition. A few remained unreachable under safe test conditions and could not qualify by presumption.

Across repeated cycles, 587 met all four requirements.

The number did not describe every possibility flicker. It did not elevate every model into a claimant. It named a bounded group that remembered, anticipated, promised, and learned.

Vela read the finding aloud. Each juror signed beneath a statement limiting the conclusion to dependent standing criteria. Five signatures appeared without qualification. One juror added a dissent on the treatment of unreachable candidates but accepted the verified count. The final juror asked Jalen to certify that the tests had not drawn results from public testimony or source labels.

He certified the blind.

Oris placed a second document before him. “Engineering witness.”

Jalen read the operative sentence twice. The finding recognized 587 branch-persons as sustained minds whose unilateral deletion would require review under Article Six. His signature would not enact a remedy. It would remove ordinary pruning from the set of unexamined tools.

Outside, the gallery had gone quiet enough for him to hear the Weather Spine alert traveling through his surrendered wrist board. Humidity deviation. Western beds. Four days had become three.

Kera waited across the table. She did not ask him to sign.

That restraint cost him the last excuse he had prepared against pressure.

Jalen took the pen. “My weather projections remain attached.”

“They should,” Oris said.

“No summary may omit the reserve loss, adaptation limits, or district distribution.”

“I will enter your conditions in sequence with the finding.”

He signed his name beneath Vela’s.

A technician raised the Root Court blinds. Public light entered across wet glass, revealing the six bays and the condensation pooled under every chair. Beyond the outer doors, someone began reading the verified names available for release. The Weather Spine alert sounded again.

Jalen retrieved his board, accepted the western failure curve, and walked toward the pressure doors with the signed finding still damp on his thumb.

Chapter 13 — Contribution Year

Ivo Senn braced an oxygen cylinder between his knees and scraped the old clinic seal from its valve.

The seal had gone hard around the threads. He worked it loose with a bone spatula while six patients waited along the corridor wall, each holding a ration mask issued during the pruning pause. No one complained about the delay. Complaints required spare breath, and the old tunnel districts had never taught wasteful breathing.

A new cylinder stood ready beside him, marked for two hours of supplemental flow. Under ordinary routing, the same vessel could cover five. The pressure board showed adequate basin oxygen in green figures, but green at the Weather Spine did not reach every scarred bronchus below the third baffle.

At the far end of the corridor, Nurse Pela opened the food cabinet and found three packets of protein mash.

“That covers breakfast,” she said.

“For whom?”

Pela looked toward the waiting patients. “Choose a generous definition.”

A porter had pinned the strike notice across the cabinet door. FOOD LABOR REQUIRES CIVIC RELIEF. Beneath it, in a second hand: A YEAR GIVEN MUST REMAIN GIVEN.

The cooperative kitchens had stopped meal deliveries before dawn. Processing crews, greenhouse packers, tram loaders, and three moss-culture shifts had joined them. Their delegates claimed that the Assembly’s recognition of branch-persons could void the benefit credits attached to yielded futures. If those contributions had harmed sustained minds, opponents argued, housing and clinic priority gained through yielding might count as proceeds of abuse. No enacted rule said so. Fear rarely waited for enactment.

Ivo fitted the new seal and tested the cylinder. The first patient, Mevi Ar, had worked condensation tunnels for thirty-one years. Her fingertips had turned blue despite the mask.

“Slow draw,” he told her. “Four breaths, then rest.”

“Will lunch arrive?”

“I am less qualified to predict lunch than lungs.”

“That explains government.”

Her dry smile lasted one breath. Ivo adjusted the strap behind her head and watched color return by degrees.

A crowd began knocking on the outer pressure door. Not the heavy rhythm of panic. Three measured strikes, repeated every ten seconds. Pela went to the window slit and came back with her mouth set.

“Food delegation,” she said. “They want you.”

“I have patients.”

“They brought thirty more.”

Outside, the service tunnel had filled from wall to wall. Workers in green-black moss aprons stood beside elderly residents from the clinic queue. Two handcarts held sealed food crates, close enough for everyone to smell heated grain through the vents. The crates remained locked.

Sera Dovan, delegate for the western greenhouse cooperatives, waited at the front. Ivo knew her from emergency nutrition councils. She had the quick posture of someone accustomed to solving spoilage before officials finished naming it.

“We are not denying clinical food,” she said before he could accuse her. “We are asking the clinics to witness distribution.”

“A locked crate nourishes no one.”

“Neither does a relief promise that can be revoked after the work.” Sera handed him a paper register. “Every person on that list yielded at least one contribution year. Most gave six or more. Yesterday a feed advocate called their housing credits morally contaminated.”

“Did the Assembly change a rule?”

“No.”

“Did Oris Nume issue a review?”

“No.”

“Then an advocate’s mouth has no jurisdiction.”

The nearest workers shifted. Several had slept in their aprons. Gray fungal dust marked one man’s cuffs, a warning from western humidity that Jalen’s projections had already reached their hands.

Sera lowered her voice. “You know how fast unofficial shame becomes an eligibility screen. A recorder marks a file for review. A clerk delays a clinic allotment. A housing board decides clean applicants should go first.”

Ivo looked at the register. Benefit codes ran beside names in a neat column: school placement, baffle-side housing, medical debt release, cooperative equipment. Yielding had always entered public life through such exchanges, no matter how often ceremonies called it pure contribution.

“What do you want me to witness?”

“That past contributors did not knowingly consent to killing sustained people. That their earned relief remains valid unless law changes through due process. Say it while the feeds listen.”

Behind Sera, a child pressed both hands against a food-crate vent. Warm spice scented his palms.

Ivo returned the register. “Open the clinical crate now. I will speak after rounds.”

“Workers voted to hold all distribution until the witness statement.”

“Then workers voted to use patients as punctuation.”

Anger moved across the tunnel. Sera did not flinch. “Patients have served as punctuation in every air budget since settlement.”

“Not with my signature.”

A man in a loader’s harness stepped forward. “My daughter’s inhaler came through my eight-year yield. If you tell the Assembly that benefit came from murder, do you take it from her?”

“I have told no one that.”

“Will you tell them it did not?”

The corridor behind Ivo carried the hiss of six ration masks. He could say what the crowd needed and release the crates within a minute. Earlier contributors had received certificates promising that no person continued in unchosen projections. They acted under civic assurance. Guilt could not travel backward through concealed facts.

Yet 587 verified branch-persons now waited under a pruning pause. Their standing did not disappear because recognition injured the reputation of a lawful gift.

Ivo held out his hand to Sera. “Give me the key.”

She watched him, then removed a steel tag from her belt.

He unlocked the nearest crate himself. “Clinical packets go inside. Two of your delegates may witness the count. The remaining carts stay sealed until your vote changes, but no patient on this level purchases your hearing with hunger.”

The loader blocked the cart handle. Sera touched his wrist, and he moved.

By midday, meal service had resumed at four clinics while the general strike held across the basin. Ivo finished rounds with a packet of mash in his pocket and no appetite for it. Two patients required increased oxygen. One elderly grower had removed her mask to feed a grandson and lost consciousness before Pela found her.

The resumed deliveries created another problem within twenty minutes. Civic relief tables still ranked recipients by contribution credit. A kitchen clerk set larger portions beside three workers with high yield scores and half portions beside two residents whose records showed zero contributed years. Pela caught the difference before the trays left her counter.

“Same respiratory order, same ration,” she said.

The clerk held up his slate. “Emergency food policy preserves earned priority.”

“Not in a clinical meal.”

Ivo took the slate. Its rule dated from a crop failure nine years earlier, when contribution credits had broken ties between otherwise equal household claims. No one had removed the field after ordinary supply returned. The strike had turned a dormant instruction into present harm.

One zero-yield patient, a tram mechanic named Sovan, pushed his half portion away. “Give it to someone whose future counted.”

“Your lungs count now,” Ivo said.

“I had children at nineteen. I could not model twenty years without pricing them into it. The clerk called that refusal.”

Across the table, a six-year contributor covered her full tray with both hands. Shame had already begun reallocating food before policy reached the room.
