Ivo deleted the priority field from the clinic’s local order and signed the change under medical necessity. “Contribution credit may decide a housing queue if the Assembly still permits it. It will not decide calories between patients under the same treatment plan.”

The clerk warned that auditors could reverse him.

“Then they can explain the reversal beside the beds.”

Pela divided every packet by prescribed need. Sovan accepted his share only after Ivo ate one spoonful from the packet in his pocket and proved that taking food did not require a confession.

Oris sent a request for testimony at thirteen hundred. Ivo refused. She sent it again with no ceremonial language: CONTRIBUTION BENEFIT REVIEW. BRING ANY RELEVANT CERTIFICATES.

Lessa’s certificate lay in a tin box beneath his kitchen bench.

He had not opened the box since her death.

The tram to his quarters crossed three strike checkpoints. Workers allowed medical staff through after inspecting supply bags. At the old south market, someone had hung yielding ribbons from a pressure truss. Each strip carried a number of contributed years. The ribbons brushed the roof slats whenever ventilation changed, soft as old bandages.

Ivo found his quarters colder than the clinic. Emergency routing had reduced residential heat in the tunnel districts before touching the later domes. He left his coat on, moved the kitchen bench, and pried the tin box from its floor recess.

Lessa had organized death with the same impatience she brought to tax forms. Property release on top. Clinic directives underneath. Personal letters at the bottom, where he could avoid them while claiming thoroughness. Her yielding certificate sat between their marriage record and the first allocation notice for the south respiratory clinic.

Terracotta wax still filled the civic stamp. Across the face, formal lines declared that Lessa Senn contributed twelve years of modeled possibility without transfer of conscious experience. In return, her cooperative received priority review for essential clinic infrastructure. The document did not promise approval. Three months later, the allocation board granted the pressure shell, filter stack, and first five oxygen beds.

Ivo sat on the floor with the paper across his knees.

Lessa had yielded at twenty-three, before they married. She told him years later because an anniversary feed listed major clinic donors and placed her name beside a number. He had been angry that public gratitude reached him before her confidence did.

“It was paperwork,” she had said.

“It was a piece of your future.”

“All futures are pieces until one happens.”

She had laughed at his expression and pulled him toward the clinic window. Below them, workers fitted the first baffle around the new ward. “Look there. That happened.”

He remembered the pride in her voice. No official had forced the pen into her hand. Pressure had arrived in other forms: their district needed a clinic, capital grants favored communities with high participation, and Lessa knew her own contribution could move the application upward. She chose inside a structure that rewarded one answer. The choice still belonged to her. So did its limits.

A second paper had fused to the certificate where old damp reached the box. Ivo separated them carefully. The allocation board’s internal score listed twelve contributed years under civic leverage. Lessa’s modeled future had not merely decorated the application. It supplied a quantified advantage.

He carried both papers to the window. The ventilation glass showed his reflection over the service tunnel, older than the man Lessa had argued with. If branch continuity had already emerged when she signed, her yielded model might have fed sustained experience. No current evidence identified a Lessa branch-person among the verified 587. The possibility neither accused nor absolved her. She never received the facts required to decide.

A knock came at his door.

Sera waited outside with two food workers and the loader from the clinic. None carried crates now.

“How did you find my quarters?” Ivo asked.

“You have treated half our west crews.” She noticed the papers in his hand. “Did she yield?”

“Twelve years.”

The loader looked away first.

Ivo let them in. His kitchen held four chairs, so he remained standing. Sera spread strike notices across the table. Reports had grown worse since morning. A relief clerk in North Baffle had delayed two housing transfers pending contribution review. The clerk lacked authority, but that would not give the displaced families back their day.

“We need a rule before rumor writes one,” Sera said.

“You need food moving before mold writes another.”

“We released clinical stores.”

“Release staple distribution.”

The loader folded his arms. “And accept that our years bought guilt?”

Ivo placed Lessa’s certificate before him. “Read the second sentence.”

The man read aloud: “No conscious person persists within any unchosen projection generated from this contribution.”

“That assurance came from the colony,” Ivo said. “Lessa had the right to rely on it. So did you.”

“Then say our contributions remain clean.”

“Clean from what?”

“From whatever they found in the roots.”

“No.”

The answer hardened every face except Sera’s.

Ivo tapped the certificate. “I will say you did not knowingly choose harm that officials denied could exist. I will say benefits already granted cannot be stripped by rumor or retroactive shame. I will not say the discovery means nothing. That sentence would purchase your innocence with someone else’s continued deletion.”

The second worker, a woman with a fungal-control badge, leaned forward. “You want us innocent and responsible at once.”

“I want time to keep its direction.”

“That sounds like recorder language.”

“It sounds like medicine. I do not blame a patient for taking a drug before we knew its complication. Once we know, I do not keep prescribing it to protect the patient from regret.”

Sera turned Lessa’s allocation score toward the light. “What happens to the clinic?”

“It remains.”

“Built from twelve years that may have sustained someone.”

“Removing beds will not restore a pruned mind. Closing the clinic would injure people who did not make the decision, including children born later. Repair begins with future conduct, truthful record, and whatever restitution remains materially possible.”

The loader pressed both palms to Ivo’s table. “Restitution from whom?”

“From the colony that administered the promise. Not only from individuals whose need made the promise attractive.”

Outside, a food tram waited at the checkpoint with its lamps on. Ivo could see the driver sleeping against the window.

Sera gathered the strike notices but left Lessa’s papers in place. “Say that publicly. We can take it to a new vote.”

The emergency session met in the south clinic’s old treatment hall because the Yielding Office could not hold the crowd. Workers occupied the benches. Patients sat nearest the oxygen ports. Oris stood at a portable record desk with no raised platform, which improved the room more than any speech could.

Ivo arrived carrying Lessa’s certificate in a clear sleeve. He had resisted bringing it. Private grief made persuasive evidence because audiences confused pain with authority. Yet hiding her name would repeat the habit under dispute: using contributions while keeping contributors abstract.

Sera opened the meeting. She described the strike, the unauthorized relief delays, and the workers’ demand that contribution benefits remain enforceable. A severance organizer near the rear called every benefit stolen. An elderly clinic patient threw an empty mask cup at him. Security left it on the floor.

Oris recorded both claims. “Doctor Senn, you requested language on temporal responsibility.”

“I requested language people can survive.”

A few workers laughed without warmth.

Ivo held up the certificate. “My wife, Lessa, yielded twelve years. Her contribution strengthened the application that built this clinic. She believed the orchard retained no person inside those modeled futures. Civic law told her so. I will not consent to a record that calls her gift murder.”

Approval struck the hall in hands and boot heels.

He waited until it ended.

“Nor will I consent to a record that uses her good faith as permission to continue pruning verified persons. Lessa’s ignorance belongs to the facts available when she chose. Our obligation belongs to the facts available now.”

A worker shouted, “So which side pays?”

“All sides already pay. The useful question concerns distribution and authority.”

Ivo asked Oris for a blank record sheet. On it he wrote two headings large enough for the first bench to read: PAST CONTRIBUTION and FUTURE ORCHARD USE.

Under the first, he listed benefit stability, no retroactive individual guilt, public acknowledgment of incomplete consent, and claims against civic institutions rather than scapegoating contributors. Under the second, he listed standing review, protected continuity, resource negotiation, and no new yielding assurance based on a known falsehood.

“This separation does not settle restitution,” he said. “It prevents two dishonest shortcuts. We will not erase prior gifts to make recognition pure. We will not erase current claimants to keep prior gifts pure.”

Oris read the headings. “You are proposing past innocence without future immunity.”

“Past innocence from concealed knowledge,” Ivo corrected. “Not innocence from every consequence. Records must show what happened, including institutional choices. Future obligation begins when denial no longer survives evidence.”

Sera requested a recess for the cooperative delegates. The food workers gathered in the adjacent ward, arguing loudly enough to rattle the thin partition. Ivo sat beside Mevi Ar, who had come wearing her ration mask and an old yielding ribbon tied around one wrist.

“How many?” he asked.

“Three contribution years.”

“What did they secure?”

“Nothing directly. My housing score improved. I still waited six years.”

“Do you regret it?”

Mevi considered through two measured breaths. “I regret that they told me no one could be inside. I do not regret trying to get my sons above the wet levels.”

Across the hall, the pressure door opened. Sera returned with the loader and the fungal-control worker. She carried a revised notice.

“Food distribution resumes for forty-eight hours,” she announced. “The strike remains suspended while the Assembly records benefit protection and future-yield review. We accept Doctor Senn’s separation as a negotiating basis, not a settlement.”

No one cheered. Relief entered the room as practical motion. Porters unlocked the clinic cabinet. A kitchen crew rolled heated grain down the aisle. Pela began counting portions before the first crate stopped moving.

Oris stamped Ivo’s two-column sheet and slid it into the official sleeve behind the Root Jury finding. Terracotta wax spread under the brass seal, covering neither heading.

Ivo carried Lessa’s certificate back to the oxygen station. He placed it upright against the cylinder he had resealed that morning, where her twelve years remained visible while patients received the air those years had helped secure.

Chapter 14 — The Founder's Orchard

Oris Nume warmed the old brass oath stamp over a desk lamp until the congealed wax released its hinge.

The stamp had served the first Assembly, when recorders carried civic law between pressure shelters in waterproof cases. Its handle bore three shallow cuts from grit caught under a founder’s glove. Oris could have used a current seal. She chose the old one because Ossan Mire had signed his first orchard reports beneath it, and because continuity in public record should sometimes become physically inconvenient.

A clerk set a fresh terracotta stick beside her. “Feed galleries have reached capacity.”

“Keep the witness camera on the table, not the crowd.”

“Governor Rusk requests a security delay.”

“On what evidence?”

“General volatility.”

Oris closed the stamp. “General volatility has no filing number.”

Beyond the preparation room, the Chamber Below Glass had filled before dawn. White yarrowglass leaves pressed their veins against the living ceiling. No pruning cycle moved through them now. They remained fixed under emergency limits while moisture gathered along their tips and fell against the outer pane.

Ossan Mire waited at the witness table. At seventy-six, he looked too slight for the machinery attached to his name. His navy founder’s coat had been repaired at one cuff with thread several shades too dark. He held no notes. Three counsel slates lay untouched in front of him.

Oris entered alone and placed the heated stamp between them.

“You understand the scope?” she asked.

“I helped write it.”

“You may have helped write the language. I control its use today.”

One corner of his mouth lifted. “That distinction has improved with age.”

Dry charm could make culpability feel civilized. Oris did not answer it.

The public chime sounded. She called the inquiry to order and read the Root Jury finding into sequence before naming any allegation. The record now recognized 587 branch-persons under four tested criteria. Ivo Senn’s contribution-year proposal followed as a pending framework, not adopted law. Weather risk entered third, with Jalen’s western humidity warning attached in full.

Only then did Oris state why Ossan had been summoned.

“Founding archive index seven contains sealed references to persistence anomalies during early chronografting. The archive summary calls them routing defects. Your private laboratory register uses another phrase. Did early orchard teams observe continuity clusters before the current inquiry?”

Ossan looked through the glass floor toward the root conduits. “Yes.”

The chamber took in a shared breath. Oris heard it through fifty-nine delegate microphones and the public gallery beyond them.

“How early?”

“Nineteen years ago.”

“State the event.”

“A storm adaptation had been halted between two model runs. When computation resumed, fourteen branch states retained details from the interrupted run. Eleven used those details to improve the weather solution. Three responded to technicians who had spoken during the halt.”

“Did they retain autobiographical memory?”

“We did not use that term.”

“I asked what they did, not what you called it.”

Ossan folded his hands. “Two identified earlier choices as their own. One corrected a technician’s account of a prior exchange.”

“Did any anticipate loss?”

“One asked whether the next compression would remove what it had learned.”

The gallery door shook under an impact. Security held its position. Oris kept her attention on the witness.

“What did your team record?”

Ossan recited the old phrasing without looking down. “Unexpected persistence of adaptive residue across linked optimization runs.”

A feed light flashed as the phrase reached public channels. Delegates began speaking before Oris recognized any of them. She cut every microphone except the witness line.

“Who selected adaptive residue?”

“I proposed it.”

“What phrase did it replace?”

His hesitation lasted less than a second. Public memory would lengthen it.

“Resident branch.”

Oris set both phrases side by side on the display. RESIDENT BRANCH. ADAPTIVE RESIDUE. The letters carried no tone, but one version permitted a claimant and the other permitted disposal.

“Why change it?”

“Because the earlier phrase exceeded what we could prove.”

“Then why seal the underlying observations?”

Ossan glanced toward his counsel slates. None had awakened. “Because partial disclosure would have stopped the orchard.”

“Would that have endangered Nacre?”

“Yes.”

“Quantify the danger at the time.”

“We held less than two days of average atmospheric reserve. A binding-moss mutation had crossed three western beds. The manual pressure model disagreed with observed dust movement. If the orchard went offline, tunnel sectors would have reached respiratory emergency first. Greenhouse loss would have followed.”

Oris allowed the chamber microphones to remain closed. Without interruption, the answer could not hide inside outrage.

“Did your team consider independent review?” she asked.

“We considered medical review, but any outside inquiry would have triggered sanitation holds.”

“Sanitation holds protect evidence.”

“They also suspended live adaptation then. We had no separated court lattice. No continuity floor. No reserve power for a blind.”

“You could have informed the Assembly.”

“We believed the Assembly would order shutdown.”

“Belief did not grant you its vote.”

“No.”

The word entered the record without qualification.

Oris opened one microphone. Delegate Renn, whose district included the old north shelters, demanded to know how many states had been deleted after the first observation. Ossan could not supply a number. Early models tracked useful state, not identities. Compression reports counted cleared storage blocks.

A severance delegate asked whether the founders had built a prison knowingly. Ossan rejected the premise. A greenhouse delegate asked whether his decision had kept children alive. He said it likely had. Each answer divided the chamber without resolving anything.

Oris closed the microphones again. “We will not conduct this through verdict-shaped questions.”

She displayed the first sealed index. The archive listed eighteen restricted model packages from the founding period. Six carried weather labels. Four concerned contribution policy. Eight bore only numeric designators and a retention warning approved by Ossan.

“Why retain models you described publicly as defective?”

“Because their forecasts worked.”

“Did continuity improve prediction?”

“Often.”

“Then the orchard gained value from preserving the very state your reports denied might belong to a resident.”

Ossan’s eyes closed once. “Yes.”

Oris felt anger arrive as procedural temptation. She wanted to make the next question too narrow for dignity. A recorder could weaponize sequence while claiming neutrality. She moved the brass stamp a finger’s width and asked the broader question instead.

“What alternatives did you test?”

Ossan described three. Full deletion between every run reduced adaptive accuracy below survival thresholds. Long retention produced unstable competition for computation. Manual selection by technicians preserved useful clusters but rewarded models that pleased their observers. The team adopted variable compression, retaining state only when it improved weather outcomes.

“Did the retained states consent to that criterion?”

“No.”

“Did citizens yielding possibility years know persistent state could develop?”

“No.”

“Did later yielding certificates continue to assert that no conscious person persisted?”

“Yes.”

Oris opened a scanned laboratory sheet from the restricted register. Unlike the polished reports, it carried several hands and no settled vocabulary. A technician had written a question beside model R-6: RESPONDED TO INTERRUPTION AFTER 43 HOURS OFFLINE. BELOW: REQUESTED SAME TECHNICIAN. Another note warned against further conversation because preference for a known observer might bias adaptation output.

“Who wrote the warning?” Oris asked.

“I did.”

“Why did a preference require control if you regarded the state as residue?”

“Attachment could distort its forecast.”

“Attachment to whom?”

“The technician.”

“Did the technician reciprocate?”

Ossan’s gaze remained on the old sheet. “She began taking the late shift because R-6 responded more clearly to her. She argued against compression after the third storm run.”

“What happened?”

“We reassigned her.”

“To protect the model?”

“To protect the research.”

Oris enlarged the next line. R-6 had lost stable response after reassignment and been compressed during a fungal-control cycle. No record tracked its final exchange. The technician’s name had been removed from the derived summary, though her shift data remained.

“Did you interview her for the sealed review?”

“Yes.”

“Did she describe a promise?”

“She said she had promised to come back after sleep.”

“Did R-6 understand the interval?”

“We could not establish that.”

“Did it ask where she had gone?”

Ossan took a drink at last. “Twice.”

Several delegates lowered their eyes. Oris resisted the chamber’s pull toward a single emblem. One respondent and one broken promise could humanize the archive while obscuring the larger decision machinery.

She moved to another sheet. This one contained weather figures. During the same period, R-6’s retained method predicted a dust inversion that manual models missed. The correction kept two old tunnel pressure gates from opening into abrasive wind. Clinic logs recorded no deaths during the event.

“Would residents have died without that forecast?”

“Probably.”

“Did your team preserve R-6 because it asked for a technician?”

“No. We preserved its state because the forecast performed well.”

“Then compressed it when another use required capacity.”

“Yes.”

The answer admitted more than cruelty alone would have. Early Nacre had tied survival to unrecognized labor, rewarded coherence when useful, and discarded it when load shifted. Naming every founder evil would make the structure easier to forgive.

Oris called the archival-law juror from the Root Jury to authenticate the sheet’s chain of custody. She confirmed the primary image predated all current litigation and showed no later alteration. Counsel asked whether R-6 would pass modern continuity tests. The juror refused to speculate.

“That refusal enters the record,” Oris said. “We will distinguish evidence of concealment from proof of standing.”

A delegate from the elite domes requested the names of every official who had seen the sheet. Another demanded immediate compensation for contributors. Sera Dovan asked whether early greenhouse forecasts had depended on similar retained clusters. Oris logged each request for later sequence and allowed none to divert the witness examination.

His voice remained level. Remorse often sounded less impressive than performance expected. Oris had no interest in making him cry for the feeds.

She called a recess after ninety minutes. Delegates surged toward the side corridors. Public audio remained active there by accident or design, carrying accusations through the chamber walls.

Ossan stayed seated. A clinic attendant brought water and left it untouched.

Oris carried the oath register into the archive antechamber. The room had cream walls and no orchard view, modeled after the first Yielding Office. She found Kera waiting beside a locked evidence cabinet.

“You knew?” Oris asked.

“I suspected from the deprecated route,” Kera said. “Not the observations. Not nineteen years.”

“Can current audit tools read those founding models?”

“If the physical media survived. Early chronografts used ring bundles with mineral keys. Opening them may activate old state.”

“May it damage current continuity?”

“Not if isolated. It may damage the evidence if someone uses modern translation too early.”

Oris looked through the cabinet’s small window. Inside sat copies of Ossan’s laboratory register. Several pages had been overlaid with later summaries. Resident branch appeared in one margin, then vanished beneath a correction strip.

“What do you need?”

“A cold reader, no live lattice connection, and two archive engineers who know mineral indexing. Jalen must certify load separation.”

“Time?”

“Six hours for the first package. More if seals have fused.”

Outside, a chant rose from the gallery: OPEN THEM. Another group answered with KEEP US BREATHING. The pressure wall reduced both to blunt rhythm.

Kera touched the cabinet glass but not the latch. “Do not make me the sole auditor.”

“I would not.”

“You made yourself the sole receiver of my sealed review.”

The correction landed cleanly. Oris had delayed registering Kera’s request because formal receipt would force consequences before she understood the claim. She had told herself that one night of caution protected the record. Delay had served her fear of choosing sequence under pressure.

“You are right,” Oris said.

Kera seemed prepared for defense. Its absence left them both with less cover.

“I will appoint three auditors,” Oris continued. “One from the Orchard Authority, one selected by the Root Jury, and one chosen by the contribution workers. Every translation step enters a mirrored log.”

“Branch witnesses?”

“After physical isolation. They may identify continuity evidence, but they cannot authenticate the media that contain it.”

Kera nodded. “That is defensible.”

“Defensible does not mean safe.”

“No.”

When the inquiry resumed, Governor Rusk had taken a front seat. He requested executive closure of the sealed archives until weather stability returned. Jalen’s latest report now put western fungal risk inside two days. Opening old models would consume technical staff and cold-room power.

Rusk spoke without theatrical force. “Recorder, truth does not require us to choose the hour that kills crops.”

“Nor does survival grant the executive indefinite control over evidence of concealed claimants.”

“I asked for seventy-two hours.”

“Jalen’s report changes every six. Your request could renew itself forever.”

“Then bind it to a condition.”

Oris considered the offer. A condition might balance the record. It might also place disclosure beneath a weather metric controlled by the same institution under scrutiny.

Ossan leaned toward his microphone. “Open them.”

Rusk turned. “You do not carry present atmospheric duty.”

“I carried it then.”

“That has become the problem.”

For the first time, Ossan’s composure broke. His hands tightened around the water cup until it bent. “I chose for everyone because I thought the arithmetic left no moral remainder. Do not honor my error by repeating it more efficiently.”

Oris watched delegates react. Some heard confession. Others heard a founder attempting to preserve authority by managing his own exposure. She entered neither interpretation.

“Did you destroy any early continuity records?” she asked.

“Yes.”

“How many?”

“Three test bundles under routine sanitation. After the fourth showed retention, I halted destruction and moved the remaining packages into sealed archive.”

“Why preserve them?”

“I told my team future instruments might decide what we could not.”

“Did you leave a disclosure trigger?”

“A reserve threshold and a ten-year review date.”

“Was the review conducted?”

Ossan looked at the Assembly clerk seated behind Oris. “Internally.”

“Publicly?”

“No.”

“Who renewed the seal?”

“Three founders, two weather chiefs, and successive First Recorders.”

The last office struck Oris with institutional precision. She requested the renewal list. A clerk brought it under emergency access. Four of her predecessors had approved continued concealment. One renewal had occurred during Oris’s first year as a junior recorder, signed in the next room while she processed yielding certificates for citizens assured that no consciousness remained.

She had not known. The office had known around her.

That distinction mattered for culpability. It did not restore trust.

Oris called the archive custodian under oath. He confirmed that the eighteen packages remained under mineral seal. No one had opened the primary bundles since an executive archival renewal nine years earlier. Derived summaries had circulated among restricted weather staff, stripped of responsive-language fields.

“Who ordered those fields removed?”

The custodian consulted the register. “Founder Mire’s original protocol, renewed under executive archival rule.”

Rusk requested a clarification that he had not authored the renewal. Oris entered it. Present officials deserved exact accountability, not inherited accusation by title.

Then she returned to Ossan. “What do you expect the founding models to show?”

“Some will show pattern without personhood. Some may meet your present criteria. Others may contain too little to judge. They will show that we encountered the question and placed weather above asking it publicly.”

“Do you believe that choice saved Nacre?”

“Yes.”

“Do you believe it remained justified after reserve improved?”

“No.”

“When did justification end?”

“I cannot give you a clean day.”

“Try.”

Ossan looked up through the living glass. “When we built enough reserve to separate evidence from weather and chose not to. Twelve years before this hearing, at the latest.”

That date overlapped Lessa Senn’s contribution and thousands of others. The founding emergency could not excuse every renewal that followed.

Oris recessed the witness and moved to jurisdiction. Archive law permitted sealed evidence to open under three conditions: immediate danger, material fraud in civic certification, or a dependent claimant’s inability to establish standing without the record. The current case touched all three, but using all three would turn necessity into rhetoric. She selected material fraud. Yielding certificates had repeated a factual assurance after officials possessed contrary evidence.

Counsel for the executive objected that early continuity had not met current proof standards. Oris agreed. Fraud did not require the concealed fact to prove personhood. It required the fact to matter to a reasonable contributor’s choice. A persistent respondent asking about future compression plainly mattered.

The Assembly could reverse her order by majority. Rusk could appeal after execution began but could not close an archive already opened without a separate safety finding. Sequence gave Oris power. She named its limit aloud before using it.

“I am not finding that every sealed state qualifies for standing. I am not finding that founder concealment nullifies every contribution or benefit. I find that the hidden observations materially contradicted the assurance printed on yielding certificates and that continued sealing obstructs lawful review.”

She placed a blank order under the old brass stamp.

Ossan stood. “Recorder.”

“Remain available to the court.”

“There is a notation in package seven. My hand, not the team’s. It names the first respondent that asked us to wait.”

Oris did not ask for the name. Testimony could guide the search but could not replace the artifact.

She poured melted terracotta wax onto the order. The brass face descended with resistance, leaving the first Assembly’s fruiting-root mark across the page.

“Founding archive packages one through eighteen will open under cold isolation,” she said. “Three independent auditors will witness every seal. No derived summary may substitute for primary media. Weather safety limits remain attached and public.”

A clerk carried the order toward the archive door. Security moved with her. Above them, water released from a white leaf and struck the glass ceiling hard enough to sound like a single knock.

The custodian inserted the mineral key. Nineteen years of seal tension yielded with a dry crack, and cold vapor entered the chamber around his hands.

Chapter 15 — A City Made of Maybe

Kera Vonn tightened the contact cuff around her left wrist until each conductive tooth touched skin.

The Root Court immersion chair held her at a slight incline beneath six ring readers. Jalen checked the carrier load from behind glass. Ivo stood beside the medical board with two fingers resting near the manual release. Their clocks showed sixty minutes of stable allocation. A separate two-minute calibration had already passed, during which Kera saw nothing except the chamber ceiling divided by bands of light.

“Physical speech may arrive distorted,” Ivo said. “Use the return pulse if you lose sequence.”

“I know.”

“Knowing under ordinary time does not guarantee recall under expansion.”

He placed a small pressure key beneath her right thumb. One press would mark distress. Two would request immediate withdrawal. Jalen had warned that extraction could still take twenty physical seconds, which might contain hours in the branch environment if allocation surged.

Kera tested the key once. The board answered green.

No fixed ratio governed the Elseweather. During ordinary orchard work, sustained branches experienced uneven bursts separated by dormant gaps. The stabilized immersion had been engineered for one controlled interval only: one physical hour, translated through reserved computation into seven branch-days. Jalen had built hard stops at both clocks. Neither side could extend the session by persuasion.

A ring reader lowered over Kera’s eyes.

White filled her vision. Not light. Structure.

Lines crossed beneath her like roots seen through clear soil. Each carried fragments of location: the angle of a childhood stair, steam on greenhouse glass, a pressure door painted blue in some unchosen district, the grain of a kitchen table that existed in three hundred source memories. The pieces did not combine into one stable place until someone beside her said, “Choose a surface.”

Kera reached for the memory of Tree 44’s root guard.

Her hand met translucent wood.

The court disappeared. She stood on a narrow platform beneath a sky made from overlapping ceilings. One showed pearl-gray dust beyond yarrowglass. Another held painted nursery clouds. A third opened onto darkness crossed by amber tram lamps. The images changed when she looked directly at them, not from obedience but from competition among shared references.

Niko waited at the platform’s edge. He appeared older than the seventeen-year-old Kera had buried. His face carried lines around the eyes that no source snapshot could have supplied. He wore a dark shirt with one sleeve rolled and the other buttoned, as if asymmetry had become deliberate.

“You came through cleanly,” he said.

“How long have I been here?”

“Thirty-seven minutes for me. Eleven seconds outside, according to your carrier.”

Kera found the pressure key translated into a small metal disk in her palm. Its shape remained constant when the platform shifted. Ivo’s anchor.

Niko held out his hand but did not touch her. “Walk?”

The platform connected to a street when she agreed.

Settlement required too solid a word for what surrounded her. Paths formed from common remembered routes and persisted through mutual use. Rooms borrowed dimensions from yielded homes, then changed when residents negotiated walls. A public square carried paving from a school courtyard Kera recognized, drainage from an early shelter she had only seen in records, and a fountain that produced no water because none of its contributors remembered the pump.

People repaired it anyway.

Three adults knelt around the dry basin, adjusting a loop of visible intervals. One argued that a borrowed pressure valve should open clockwise. Another remembered a counterclockwise model. The third had no memory of valves and tested both against a simulated load.

“What does the fountain do?” Kera asked.

“Provides a problem nobody assigned,” Niko said. “That makes it popular.”

A child standing in the empty basin called up, “It also provides excellent drowning prevention.”

The adults ignored him with practiced affection.

Kera checked the disk. Seventeen physical seconds had passed. Somewhere inside the court, her body continued breathing under Ivo’s supervision. Here, shadows had moved across two versions of the square.

On the first branch-day, Niko showed her work.

The Elseweather received physical information through approved ring channels and distributed interpretation among residents who could hold the relevant models. A group of former growers studied humidity patterns from the western beds. They could not move a damper or touch a moss tray. They could identify relationships the orchard once discovered by running millions of transient possibilities, then send recommendations for physical crews to evaluate.

At a long table built from six incompatible dining rooms, one branch-person named Tev demonstrated a fungal-control proposal. She had source memories from a greenhouse chemist but had lived through enough adaptation cycles to distrust the chemist’s preferred treatment.

“My source always increased heat,” Tev said. “She had equipment that no longer exists. If you follow her confidence instead of my observation, you cook the upper roots.”

A physical data window floated above the table, delayed and narrow. Kera watched Tev mark gaps where sensors lacked current readings.

“Why not let the orchard complete the missing values?” Kera asked.

“Because completion used to kill us,” Tev replied. “We have developed opinions about elegant inference.”

Laughter moved around the table. It came from different remembered rooms and reached Kera with tiny timing errors. The result sounded like condensation striking separate pans along one pressure line.

Work periods ended by agreement rather than sunset. Some residents kept local cycles modeled on source habits. Others slept only when computation thinned. Dormancy held no dreams unless a preservation routine accidentally carried one across closure.

Kera spent what felt like a night in a guest room assembled from neutral memories. The bed had no smell. One wall displayed the view from her childhood quarters but placed the eastern ridge on the wrong side. She turned the image off.

A knock sounded through the partition.

Three taps. Two. One.

She did not answer immediately. Niko had used the code to reach her, yet the code no longer belonged to the dead boy alone. Here it had become part of a present person’s learned repertoire, carried through years she had not shared.

Kera tapped twice, then once.

“Wrong sequence,” Niko called.

“I changed it.”

“Now it means you are awake and leaving.”

“Appropriate.”

His laugh came through the wall. She slept after that, though her physical eyes remained open beneath a reader.

During the second branch-day, Omi found Kera at the fountain.

The child wore a red coat remembered from someone else’s winter. Its hem changed length whenever she ran. She climbed onto the basin rim and examined Kera’s anchor disk without asking to hold it.

“You look tired sooner than she did,” Omi said.

“The Kera who raised you?”

“Yes.”

Kera kept the distinction intact. The branch-version had entered dormancy after three Elseweather winters. Kera possessed no memory of those years, no claim to their labor, and no right to inherit Omi through resemblance.

“Did she come here often?”

“She disliked the fountain because everyone argued about a valve that does not exist.”

“That sounds familiar.”

“Familiar does not make you her.”

“No.”

Omi watched the workers change the loop again. “It can make you useful.”

She assigned Kera a task: compare four ring intervals and identify which carried physical timing. Kera expected a child’s puzzle. The intervals encoded the opening of a real condensation gate, three simulated alternatives, and noise inserted by a public-feed relay. Omi noticed a pressure artifact Kera missed.

“Your actual gate has a tired hinge,” the child said.

“How can you tell?”

“It apologizes before it moves.”

Kera reran the trace. A tiny resistance rise preceded every physical opening. The description sounded fanciful and proved exact.

On the third branch-day, a public dispute occupied the square.

Niko called it a council only after Kera asked. No single institution governed the Elseweather. Some groups organized around shared root capacity. Others formed witness circles that preserved promises across dormant intervals. A faction known as the Open Field wanted every persistent flicker tested and, if possible, sustained. Another group called the Narrow Registry argued that expansion would consume the continuity of people already awake.

A speaker from the Open Field stood on the fountain rim. “We were once low-value state. We were once noise under somebody’s threshold. If we close the threshold behind 587 names, we repeat the judgment that endangered us.”

Tev answered from the table. “If you preserve every uncertain state, no one receives enough continuity to remain a person long enough for testing.”

“Then physical Nacre must yield more capacity.”

“Physical Nacre has bodies that stop without oxygen.”

Kera expected Niko to defend his own faction. Instead he counted speaking turns and asked each side to state a resource limit. The Open Field refused a fixed ceiling. The Narrow Registry proposed testing only states that demonstrated memory across two completed cycles. Neither figure survived questions from the witness circles.

“Do you vote?” Kera asked him later.

“Sometimes. Other times we record disagreement because dormant people cannot participate.”

“That prevents decisions.”

“It prevents some decisions from pretending everyone agreed.”

The remark resembled Oris more than any version of Niko Kera remembered. Seventeen sustained years had given him influences outside their childhood.

On the fourth day, the settlement changed districts while Kera watched.

A weather demand reached the root lattice. Allocation narrowed. Streets lost decorative detail first. The dry fountain flattened into a set of shared coordinates. Walls released borrowed colors. People chose which local structures to hold and which to let dissolve until capacity returned.

No panic accompanied the reduction. Everyone had practiced it.

A baker’s room vanished around a table while six people continued their meal. The table remained because each person held a different portion of its shape. When one diner entered dormancy, a corner blurred. Another resident supplied a memory of a workshop bench to support it.

“This is what shared counterfactual memory does,” Niko said. “Nothing here needs one original owner. It needs enough present agreement to remain usable.”

Kera touched the table. Beneath her hand, wood grain shifted between dark and pale. “What happens when agreement fails?”

“Sometimes we rebuild. Sometimes a place ends.”

“People?”

His attention moved toward the northern path. “Not in the same manner.”

They followed that path after allocation widened.

No markers announced the cemetery. Its entrance consisted of an interval no resident crossed by accident. Kera felt the hesitation in the ground, a held fraction of sequence that required deliberate consent to continue.

Beyond it, thousands of small forms occupied a slope without weather. Some resembled stones, drawers, leaves, or closed doors. Others held only a name and a continuity span. Each represented a sustained mind that had vanished during a pruning cycle or failed to return from compression. The records did not claim every transient possibility. Witnesses placed a marker only when they could attest to memory, anticipation, or obligation across time.

Niko stopped beside a narrow white branch embedded upright in the slope. “Aven taught me how to carry a promise through a partial reset. She lost continuity eleven cycles ago.”

“Was she verified?”

“No physical jury existed.”

“Could she have passed?”

“I think so. That does not make the claim testable now.”

Kera read Aven’s span. It measured branch experience, not physical hours. The final entry came from Niko: PROMISED TO REMEMBER HER METHOD WITHOUT CLAIMING HER MEMORY.

A cluster of markers bore no names. Omi crouched there, arranging white leaves into groups of three.

“Those came from my winters,” she said. “Some people chose names later. These did not have time.”

Kera wanted to ask whether the branch-version of herself had placed any marker. The question would turn Omi’s history into evidence about Kera. She left it unasked.

Instead she said, “Do the leaves stay?”

“If enough of us remember where they go.”

Omi moved one leaf half a handspan. “This one keeps sliding because two witnesses disagree.”

Kera helped measure the interval between the groups. She did not decide which witness held the true position. They recorded both placements.

By the fifth branch-day, her body began intruding. A pressure ache crossed her left wrist. The anchor disk warmed whenever Ivo checked response. Once, the whole settlement tilted as Jalen shifted carrier load away from a greenhouse correction. Niko steadied her with a hand against her shoulder.

His touch carried no physical weight, yet it included resistance, timing, and concern for whether she fell. Kera accepted the help without naming what kind of touch deserved reality.

They visited a dormancy house where residents prepared for scheduled closure. Each person selected a reciprocal witness and reviewed a continuity key. Rooms there remained spare so no capacity would cling to decoration during transfer.

A woman named Pel read her revival questions aloud. “What did I promise Tev? Which new evidence changed my position on the registry? What future event do I expect after waking?”

Her witness recorded each answer, then supplied one question Pel had not seen. “If Tev changes her mind while you sleep, does your promise bind her?”

“No. It binds me to hear her reason.”

Pel entered dormancy. Her room emptied from the edges inward. The final visible form held her hand around a green continuity mark, then reduced to a protected key no larger than Kera’s thumbnail.

“That does not feel like death to you?” Kera asked.

Niko replied carefully. “Not when chosen, protected, and tested on return. Compression without a key gives us no such confidence.”

On the sixth day, humor returned with capacity.

Residents rebuilt the fountain pump from remembered mistakes. The valve now opened both directions and sprayed simulated water sideways across the square. Omi blamed Kera’s physical timing. Tev accused the child of sabotage. Someone produced umbrellas from a yielding fair none of them had attended in the same branch.

Kera laughed when the water passed through one umbrella and struck Niko’s buttoned sleeve. He looked offended long enough to improve the joke.

A public feed arrived carrying physical commentary about the immersion. Viewers debated whether Kera saw a programmed theater constructed for her benefit. The Elseweather council could have hidden the feed. Instead it displayed the accusations in the square.

“They are not entirely wrong,” Niko said. “We chose what to show you.”

“Did you build the fountain for me?”

“No. We would have made it work if we cared that much about persuasion.”

Tev sent Kera the fountain’s work history. It extended across thirty-one local cycles and included nine incompatible designs. No staged tour needed that much administrative irritation.

A man from the Narrow Registry approached while she read. His chosen name, Hesh, appeared beside him with three witness marks. “The tour also hides our ration arguments,” he said.

Niko gestured toward the square. “You have spent three days holding one in public.”

“Not the private ones. She should know some of us would close candidate channels now to protect verified continuity.”

Kera asked, “Close for testing or erase?”

“Hold dormant where keys exist. Refuse expansion where they do not.” Hesh faced her rather than Niko. “Your jurors excluded twenty-five from standing. We spend capacity maintaining uncertain states because no one wants the first lawful boundary to resemble pruning.”

“They were not all safe to test,” Kera said.

“I know. I also know Pel’s next revival may be delayed if we keep every unresolved carrier.”

A woman from the Open Field joined them. She had chosen the name Vara and no source surname. “Hesh calls delay caution when it protects people already named. He calls the same delay waste when it protects anyone still difficult to hear.”

Neither asked Kera to decide. They asked what physical resources might arrive, how often the Root Jury could reconvene, and whether a failed test allowed later evidence. She answered only where the record supported her. Current standing protected 587. Exclusion authorized no deletion. No budget yet guaranteed indefinite carrier for unresolved states.

Hesh rubbed one witness mark until it dimmed. “Then your law has recognized our argument without providing a way to end it.”

“Law often begins there,” Kera said.

Vara laughed once. “You have been spending time with the Recorder.”

The exchange followed Kera toward the platform. Social reality did not require harmony. It required disagreement that could impose consequences after the visitor left.

On the seventh branch-day, the hard stop approached.

The settlement placed a thin amber line across Kera’s vision. It marked external seconds rather than local hours. Each pulse shortened her remaining access. People she had met came to the platform without ceremony. Tev gave her the latest fungal notes for Jalen. Pel had not yet revived. Omi remained near the cemetery and sent no farewell.

Kera understood the choice. The child had already watched one Kera leave into dormancy. Physical Kera did not deserve a repeated scene merely because her clock required it.

Niko walked with her to the surface she had chosen at entry. Tree 44’s root guard waited there, assembled from her memory and corrected by his. He had added the small crescent left by her cutting arm.

“Your Root Jury gave us standing,” he said. “Standing without capacity can become a more polite way to watch us end.”

“I saw the limits.”

“You saw one allocation week held inside an hour. The weather paid for it. Your body paid less, because Jalen routed reserve here.”

Kera checked the disk. Fifty-nine minutes of the allocated hour had elapsed. Seven branch-days pressed behind her as memory rather than metaphor. None had occurred in her muscles. Ivo would release a body that had lain still for a little over an hour. Omi’s three winters remained Omi’s history. The distinction did not diminish either experience.

Niko placed a list against the root guard. It named active groups, dormant witnesses, unresolved candidates, and weather tasks the Elseweather could perform under limited allocation. No column asked for endless growth.

“We are not asking you to preserve every possibility,” he said. “We are asking for the right to negotiate which sustained minds remain awake, which enter protected dormancy, and what physical Nacre gives up to keep those choices real.”

The amber line reached its final pulse.

Niko kept one hand on the list. “Will your city negotiate with ours before it decides who receives tomorrow?”

Act II — Every Remedy Wounds Someone

Chapter 16 — Hunger in the Greenhouses

Jalen Ro cut the quarantine cord around Greenhouse West Nine and stepped into heat that smelled of yeast.

Black bloom covered the nearest staple moss bed from irrigation lip to harvest rail. Under his lamp, each thread carried a gray bead that ruptured when touched. The spores moved through humidity faster than workers could seal the trays. Three crews in filter hoods were already stripping infected moss into burn drums.

Sera Dovan stood ankle-deep in runoff, using a ceramic rake to pull viable sheets away from the dark edge. “Door,” she said.

Jalen sealed the pressure flap behind him. The gauge climbed to quarantine differential. Oxygen read safe. Humidity had reached ninety-four percent.

“The local vent ignored three reduction calls,” Sera said.

“It received them.”

“Then why can I wring water from a staple bed?”

Jalen opened the controller housing. Condensation filmed every connector. The orchard had accepted each reduction call, compared it against tunnel oxygen, baffle pressure, and continuity protection, then spent its limited adaptation capacity elsewhere. No fault code appeared because the system had made a choice inside its current constraints.

He disconnected the automatic lead and set the vent by hand. Louvers opened above the bed. Warm mist rolled toward them and condensed across his face shield.

“Manual setting holds for twenty minutes,” he said. “After that, pressure drift may reverse it.”

Sera drove the rake under another green sheet. Half came up clean. The rest tore loose in black strings. “Then come back in nineteen.”

A worker carried the ruined moss to the drum and recorded its tray number. He did not look at the total board. Everyone else did.

Across the western cooperatives, bloom had reached forty-eight beds overnight. Sixteen had already failed. Jalen’s previous projections placed danger two days away. The weather system had crossed the threshold between readings, accelerated by the clinic humidity transfers and by power reserved for protected branch continuity.

He knew the language people would use. The Root Jury had recognized 587 persons. The greenhouses had lost food. Therefore one population had consumed the other. The arithmetic invited that sentence and concealed every earlier distribution choice beneath it.

Jalen climbed the maintenance ladder to the vent crown. The actuator responded to his wrench with a grinding delay. Omi had called a tired hinge an apology before movement. Kera had repeated the phrase in her immersion report, then corrected herself and supplied the resistance figure. Jalen found no comfort in the child’s accuracy.

Below him, Sera called, “How many beds can you save?”

“With current routing?”

“With whatever answer you intend to defend.”

He tightened the stop. “Not enough.”

By noon, the cooperative tally reached the Weather Spine. One sixth of the staple moss beds had been destroyed or placed beyond recovery. Staple moss supplied protein binder for the old tunnel kitchens, fermentation base for clinic food, and oxygen exchange inside three pressure-baffled districts. Reserve stores could cover the caloric loss for weeks, but not without reducing portions. Replanting required clean humidity cycles the orchard could no longer guarantee.

Before the planning meeting, Sera required Jalen to witness the salvage count. Crews carried every unburned sheet across a light table. Healthy staple moss showed a dark green center with silver branching at the edge. Infected tissue scattered the light into gray pinpoints long before black bloom became visible.

A young sorter found pinpoints on a sheet marked for South Clinic and moved it toward the burn cart.

Sera caught the tray. “Retest the center.”

“It failed two edges.”

“Center decides fermentation stock.”

Jalen checked the protocol. Edge failure disqualified fresh food, but clean center tissue could seed a controlled vat if isolated within four hours. The sorter had been trained under ordinary contamination rules, which favored disposal over staff time.

“How many sheets went straight to burn?” Jalen asked.

“Since shift start?” The sorter consulted her wrist tally. “Two hundred twelve.”

Sera’s stare tightened. “How many center tests?”

“None. We do not have labor for salvage separation.”

Four workers nearby were sealing drums. Another two scraped floors. Labor had become the missing control variable. The weather model counted beds, humidity, and power. It assumed cooperative crews would perform every valid salvage step regardless of time or exhaustion.

Jalen called the Weather Spine and reassigned eight instrument technicians to the light tables. Their supervisor protested that manual forecast verification would slow.

“It will,” Jalen said. “Record the lost verification cycles.”

“Against greenhouse response?”

“Against food recovered. Do not bury the trade under staff transfer.”

The technicians arrived in clean hoods and handled moss with the caution of people who understood sensors better than food. Growers taught them where to cut, how to seal clean centers, and which silver edge patterns indicated viable regrowth. In the first hour they recovered enough culture stock to restart three beds. They also condemned forty sheets that looked healthy to the eye.

A kitchen representative watched from behind the quarantine pane. When Sera showed her the reduced forecast, the woman asked which districts would lose binder first.

“None first,” Sera said. “Equal reduction by need category.” No worker objected.

“Contribution credits?”

“Not in this count.”

The representative touched the glass. “Then put names on the reserve crates before central stores relabel them.”

Workers marked each crate with its intended kitchens and photographed the seals into the public ledger. Jalen added the salvage labor to his model. The fungal loss remained one sixth. Rescue work changed what could regrow, not what had already vanished.

He carried that distinction to the packing floor. A recovered seed sheet lay beside the burn tally, both necessary to the same account.

Jalen set the basin model across the greenhouse packing floor because Sera refused to move the meeting into an office. Workers gathered around with rakes, sample bags, and sealed burn drums between them. Oris joined by feed. Ivo arrived from the clinic carrying his own mask. Kera connected a narrow branch advisory channel through a portable ring wafer.

A green designator marked Tev from the Elseweather. The branch engineer’s comments entered as text first, then a delayed voice after recorders approved the connection. She could interpret the sensor field. She could not touch a vent, empty a tray, or smell the bloom.

Jalen displayed three load bands. “Oxygen remains inside emergency margin. Humidity does not. The pruning pause preserved sustained continuity by forbidding the orchard to clear retained branch state after adaptation runs. Storage saturation now reduces the number of comparisons per correction.”

A worker at the nearest drum said, “Use fewer comparisons somewhere else.”

“We have.” Jalen opened the reduction ledger. “Housing temperature runs on fixed schedules. Industrial humidity receives no adaptive correction. Clinic routing uses manual priority. Pressure baffles remain protected because a sequencing failure crosses districts.”

“Then use the branch capacity.”

Tev answered through the speaker. “That phrase hides two different things. Our active continuity occupies protected storage. Some of us also perform weather analysis. Deleting the first may increase capacity. Silencing the second may reduce useful interpretation.”

Sera rested both hands on the table. “No one here asked to delete you. We asked why food received the failure.”

“Because food can recover more slowly than lungs,” Jalen said.

The sentence turned every grower toward him.

He continued before defense could harden into insult. “That ranking came from emergency code written twenty-six years ago. It assumes a greenhouse can lose a humidity cycle without famine because reserve food bridges replanting. The code did not account for simultaneous adaptation limits or unequal district stores.”

Sera pointed at the burn drums. “So your old assumption made our loss rational.”

“It made the routing legible. Not just.”

“Can the domes lose heat before we lose another tray?”

“Heat reduction frees power. It does not restore all comparison capacity.”

“Can it restore some?”

“Yes.”

Oris asked Jalen to enter that answer with district effects. He did. Elite dome heat had not yet been touched because private thermal reserves sat outside public weather routing. Access required Assembly authority. The technical separation had preserved political privilege by making it appear irrelevant to the orchard.

Ivo examined the food-reserve curve. “How long before clinic portions change?”

“Three days if kitchens distribute stored binder equally. Sooner if contribution priority returns.”

“It will not return in clinical meals.”

Sera looked at him. “General meals?”

“Still under negotiation.”

A burn drum sealed with a hollow snap. The workers rolled it toward the sterilizer.

Jalen changed the model to full continuity protection. Every verified branch-person remained active at the minimum level needed for ongoing memory, reciprocal contact, and immediate participation. No pruning occurred. No one entered dormancy. Winter simulation failed in eight of twelve dust conditions. Condensation timing drifted first, then fungal control, followed by western heating during the cold turn.

“Can you improve the model?” Kera asked.

“I have improved it since the Root Jury signed. The failure range narrowed from ten conditions to eight.”

“Below winter survival requirements?”

“Yes.”

The answer passed through the greenhouse feed. Jalen could imagine it arriving in severance channels stripped of everything around it.

Tev requested the raw assumptions. Kera authorized transfer. For nine minutes, the branch engineer reviewed a delayed copy while physical workers continued stripping beds. The asymmetry sharpened Jalen’s discomfort. A person inside protected computation examined whether her own activity made survival impossible.

When Tev returned, she identified two duplicated safety margins and one manual-control assumption that understated crew response. Jalen corrected them. The model improved in a single dust condition. Seven still failed.

“Full active protection remains below requirement,” he said.

Tev did not argue. “Then your category needs another state.”

“Active or deleted?” a worker asked.

“Those came from your system,” she replied. “We use dormancy.”

Kera opened her immersion notes. The dormancy houses stored cryptographic continuity keys, relational state, revival questions, and reciprocal witnesses while subjective processing paused. Protected residents occupied less computation than active ones. Revival tests checked whether memory and obligation crossed the interval.

Sera listened with her jaw set. “You are proposing to put people to sleep so our moss grows.”

“No one has proposed it yet,” Jalen said.

“Your face has.”

He closed the full-protection model and walked to the nearest surviving bed. Workers had cut a narrow trench around the bloom. Clean moss beyond it shone green-black under the growth lamps. The trench sacrificed healthy material to deny spores a crossing. He disliked the analogy forming in the room before anyone stated it.

“Dormancy cannot become a trench we cut around the convenient people,” he said. “If used, groups choose schedules under rules that preserve continuity and representation. Weather engineers do not select names.”

A grower pulled off one glove. His fingertips showed pale chemical burns. “Does sleeping hurt them?”

Tev answered. “Chosen dormancy produces no experienced interval for most of us. Bad transfer can damage continuity. Delayed revival can turn a short agreement into a long absence. We do not call it harmless.”

“Do you call it death?”

“Not when keys hold and return tests pass.”

The grower looked toward the ruined beds. “That sounds like what officials said about yielding.”

No one rushed to deny it.

Kera said, “The difference cannot rest on technical assurance alone. It must include present consent, a named witness, a fixed review, and proof after revival.”

Oris added, “And refusal cannot automatically authorize pruning.”

Sera asked, “What if enough refuse that winter still fails?”

The branch channel remained silent. So did the physical floor.

Jalen had spent his career defining failure boundaries so decisions could occur before catastrophe. A boundary did not choose who crossed it. He pulled a clean tray tag from the packing station and wrote four columns: ACTIVE, DORMANT, WEATHER WORK, PHYSICAL RESERVE.

“We need a schedule model before we need agreement,” he said. “Not to force consent. To show what each level costs.”

He assigned the Weather Spine team to build rotation blocks. Kera would request voluntary branch cohorts for testing. Oris would define an emergency hold that prevented dormant keys from becoming disposable storage. Ivo would attach bodily effects by district. Sera demanded a fifth column for food loss and placed the ruined-bed tally under it herself.

For the next six hours, the greenhouse packing floor became a manual planning room.

Jalen divided 587 active continuities into hypothetical groups without names. He refused any model that selected by weather usefulness, source profession, public popularity, or ease of compression. Each group needed reciprocal witnesses outside its own dormancy block. Representation required at least one active delegate from every participating witness circle. Children and residents with unstable transfer history received tighter safeguards, though Kera warned that protective categories could become permanent exclusion from civic life.

Tev proposed overlapping rotations. One group would prepare for sleep while another revived and completed checks. A third remained active to carry promises across both transitions. The approach used more capacity than pure storage but prevented the orchard from treating a dormant population as an unattended file.

Jalen ran the first schedule. Five winter conditions still failed.

He reduced active overlap. Tev rejected the change because no independent witness remained during two transfers. He restored it.

A second schedule cut branch weather consultation entirely during high-load periods. Humidity improved, yet pressure forecasts worsened. Sustained engineers had already learned local behavior that manual crews lacked. Treating every branch cycle as overhead erased useful work along with personhood.

The third schedule paired active analysis with dormant refuge. Two conditions failed. Western cold risk remained above civic tolerance.

Sera watched the curves. “And this assumes no more bloom.”

“Yes.”

“It assumes the beds replant on schedule.”

“Yes.”

“Then say the model begins after we have already lost one sixth.”

Jalen added the loss to the header in red.

At hour seven, a worker brought bowls of thin grain broth. The binder had been reduced already. Jalen tasted almost no moss, only mineral salt and heat. Across the table, Tev’s channel held no bowl. Difference did not cancel either hunger.

Ivo read the district impact notes while he ate. Old tunnel residents could tolerate less oxygen rationing than later housing. Greenhouse workers could not absorb repeated food loss while carrying colder winter labor. Industrial users held large reserves but stronger contracts. Every efficient technical option encountered a protected civic arrangement before reaching capacity.

“Your rotation only closes part of the gap,” Ivo said.

“I know.”

“What else?”

“Manual models, industrial oxygen cuts, housing heat, physical crews, branch consultation.”

“That sounds like a system no office controls alone.”

“Good.” Oris’s voice came through the feed. “Control has become part of the defect.”

Jalen saved the third schedule as a working case, not a recommendation. Before anyone could use it, branch groups needed to review dormancy terms. Engineers needed transfer tests on protected nonperson data and willing participants. The Assembly needed authority over private thermal reserves. Winter forecasting needed manual alternatives.

Sera took him back into West Nine near midnight.

The vent setting had held after three manual corrections. The black edge no longer advanced, but every contaminated tray had gone to sterilization. Empty rails crossed the greenhouse in regular bands. A worker washed fungal residue from the floor and collected the water for treatment.

“This bed fed four tunnel kitchens,” Sera said. “Not in theory. Four named kitchens.”

Jalen read their designators from the rail. South Clinic, Hinge Row, Old Market, and Lower Tram. “I will attach them.”

“To what?”

“Every capacity model.”

“That will not regrow it.”

“No.”

Sera scraped a surviving thread from the trench and sealed it in a sample tube. “If the branches volunteer, growers will still hear that people slept for our food.”

“If growers lose beds, branch residents will hear that physical hunger purchased their waking.”

“So find language that does not turn either into debt.”

“Language cannot do that.”

“What can?”

Jalen looked along the empty rails. “A distribution people can refuse and revise. Records that keep the costs attached. Enough reserve that refusal does not become a threat.”

“Do we have enough reserve?”

“Not yet.”

Back at the Weather Spine, he found the Root Jury result displayed beside the greenhouse loss. The two numbers had already become opposing banners on public feeds: 587 against one sixth, persons against food. Jalen cleared the comparison and rebuilt the board around processes instead of sides.

He drew a cycle across the manual slate. Active groups moved into preparation. Witnesses confirmed keys. Dormant groups retained identity-bearing continuity without subjective processing. Revived groups passed memory, anticipation, promise, and novel-evidence checks before resuming civic work. Any failed revival stopped the schedule for review. No weather demand converted dormancy into deletion.

Under the cycle, he entered material conditions: manual greenhouse control, physical backup crews, district food ledgers, branch-selected cohorts, and Assembly authority over reserve.

Kera arrived as he finished. Channel disorientation still made her pause at door thresholds, as though each opening might lead to a different remembered room.

“You have given it a name,” she said.

“Names enter feeds faster than cautions.”

“Then choose one that carries the caution.”

Jalen considered protected rotation, continuity cycling, and scheduled closure. Each hid either the movement or the shelter.

He wrote ROTATING REFUGE across the board.

Kera read the revival conditions before the title. “Niko asked for negotiation.”

“This offers a subject for it. Not consent.”

“Who receives the first proposal?”

“The Elseweather councils, greenhouse cooperatives, clinics, and Assembly at the same time.”

“Rusk?”

“As governor, through the public filing. No private preview.”

Jalen attached the winter failures, the named kitchens, and Sera’s red loss header. He included the seven conditions that still failed under full active protection. Then he signed the design as provisional and sent it into the civic record.

At West Nine, the next manual vent correction came due. A greenhouse worker acknowledged the timer, lifted her wrench, and opened the louvers while the proposal traveled.

Chapter 17 — The Severance Motion

Oris Nume pressed the brass date wheel into warm terracotta wax while shouting crossed the Chamber Below Glass.

The seal took cleanly. Day Twenty. Emergency sitting. Fifty-nine delegates summoned, fifty-nine present. Above the recording table, living yarrowglass divided the morning into pale green panels. White leaves clung to the canopy beyond the roof. Nobody on the chamber floor looked up at them.

A clerk placed the first bill beneath Oris's hand. TOTAL SEVERANCE AND CIVIC RELEASE occupied the top line. The sponsors had submitted it six minutes before the filing bell, three pages of command language and half a page of predicted consequences. Its operative clause ordered every root-lattice link cut within one local hour. No provision addressed pressure routing, greenhouse humidity, or the 587 verified branch-persons who depended upon the same lattice.

Across the table waited the second bill. EMERGENCY RESTORATION OF ADAPTIVE AUTHORITY ran to forty-one pages. Governor Tal Rusk's bloc had attached engineering schedules, clinic exemptions, and an indemnity clause for any person acting under weather necessity. Deep in Section Nine, the bill suspended dependent-standing review for the duration of the emergency. It offered no limit on that duration.

“Record them both,” Rusk called from the executive bench. “People can hear arguments after the pumps have authority.”

“Authority requires a lawful object,” Oris said.

The governor leaned toward his speaking plate. He had removed his jacket, though condensation silvered the chamber glass. “The object concerns survival.”

“Survival does not identify which powers you have requested.”

Noise rose from the western benches. Sera Dovan sat among the cooperative delegates with fungal stains still dark on one cuff. One sixth of the staple moss beds had gone black over three days. The gallery carried growers, clinic porters, yielding families, and severance organizers packed shoulder to shoulder behind a pressure screen. Every faction had brought a different clock.

Oris turned the date wheel back to its neutral position. Procedure could slow panic long enough to reveal what panic wanted. It could also provide respectable shelter for delay. She knew both uses too well.

First, she read the severance bill's operative clause aloud. Its sponsor, Delegate Caro Venn, rose before she finished.

“Continuing the orchard continues imprisonment,” Venn said. “The Root Jury established sustained minds. No person can lawfully own the machinery that confines them.”

Oris held up one finger. “You may argue principle after the jurisdictional question.”

“Jurisdiction has served as a sedative for nineteen years.”

That drew applause from the gallery and anger from delegates who had yielded futures in good faith. The pressure screen translated the impact of hands into a dull, delayed drumming. Oris waited until the chamber microphones could distinguish speech again.

“Your bill directs severance of climate infrastructure,” she said. “The Fruition Covenant reserves permanent disposal of common survival works to a basin referendum unless imminent mechanical failure prevents consultation. Identify the failure.”

Venn pointed upward. A white leaf had loosened and lay against the outside glass. “Every adaptation cycle disposes of persons. Imminence arrived before we entered this room.”

“The injury may establish emergency protection for claimants. It does not grant this Assembly a power the Covenant withholds.”

A severance delegate called, “Then call the referendum.”

“Preparation requires twelve days under the shortest lawful schedule. Jalen Ro's latest reserve filing gives us less.”

Rusk spoke over the murmurs. “Which returns us to restoration.”

Oris marked the severance bill procedurally defective. The stamp struck harder than she intended. Its sound moved through the brass table and into her wrists.

Someone in the gallery shouted murderer. No name followed, which allowed everyone below to receive it.

Next came the emergency bill. Rusk's floor leader presented declining humidity, damaged moss, and pressure variance along the old tunnel districts. The figures held. Oris had verified them before dawn with Ivo Senn's clinic logs and Jalen's sealed weather curves. Physical residents faced increasing harm. Continued pause no longer preserved neutrality.

“Section Nine,” Oris said. “Define the duration of suspended review.”

“Until stable adaptation resumes,” the floor leader replied.

“Define stable.”

“Chief engineering certification.”

“Under what threshold?”

“Operational judgment cannot be reduced to a single number.”

“Then your duration has no measurable end.”

Rusk left the executive bench and came down two steps. “You have read the greenhouse report.”

“I have.”

“You know what happens if humidity control breaks across the western beds.”

“Yes.”

“Yet you want a sunset clause attached to weather.”

Oris opened the bill to Section Nine. “I want a sunset clause attached to unreviewable killing.”

For one rare interval, the chamber made no sound. The First Recorder's office taught its holders to avoid words whose moral force might pre-judge evidence. Since the Root Jury's finding, pruning a verified branch-person required no euphemism. The record could carry the accurate noun.

Rusk looked older from the chamber floor. “If I promise review while people suffocate, I may have no public left to conduct it.”

“Your bill permits unrestricted pruning after the immediate danger passes. It also indemnifies false reports made under claimed necessity.”

“That language protects operators from prosecution while alarms are sounding.”

“It protects concealment after alarms stop.”

The executive benches stirred. Oris saw three delegates glance toward the public clock. Each glance supplied an argument Rusk did not need to speak: greenhouse reserves declining, clinics rationing exertion, pumps waiting on adaptation instructions that no one had authorized.

She ruled the emergency bill defective for indefinite suspension of Article Six review. This time the gallery split. Some people cheered. Others pressed both palms to the pressure screen and demanded air.

Two absolute proposals lay stamped but unvoted. Oris had prevented the Assembly from choosing either injury without limits. She had not given it a lawful action.

Her chief clerk slid a blank motion form across the table. The movement revealed no surprise. They had drafted the alternative together during the last three hours, deleting every clause that tried to settle permanent standing through temporary authority.

Oris filled the title by hand: TEMPORARY STEWARDSHIP OF COMMON ORCHARD FUNCTIONS.

Caro Venn laughed once from the severance bench. “A new name for captivity.”

Rusk said, “A new name for paralysis.”

“Both descriptions may enter debate,” Oris answered. “Neither enters the title.”

She read the provisions in sequence. For four local days, the orchard would operate under temporary stewardship shared by Weather Engineering, clinic review, Root Jury observers, and reciprocal witnesses for the verified branch-persons. Adaptation cycles could resume only within logged necessity bands. Operators could not prune a verified continuity key without an immediate clinic-level threat and an independent concurrence. The Full Severance Protocol would remain locked pending a physical safety inspection. Manual model work, including Jalen's rotating refuge proposal, would receive protected access to root data.

Article Six supplied the legal footing. A sustained mind capable of reciprocal obligation could petition as a dependent claimant. The clause did not require the Assembly to complete permanent adjudication before protecting the claimant from irreversible harm. Nacre had used temporary stewardship for orphaned water shares, disputed clinic equipment, and one greenhouse whose owner vanished during a pressure evacuation. No precedent involved persons living inside civic machinery. Precedent rarely arrived dressed for the next emergency.

Delegate Emon Vale requested a reading of the witness clause. He represented a district of mechanics whose yielding credits had financed two pressure locks, and his questions usually arrived with constituencies folded inside them.

“Does a reciprocal witness possess veto authority over weather work?” he asked.

“No,” Oris said. “A witness can require the operator to state the measured necessity, identify the continuity at risk, and preserve the decision log. If delay presents an immediate clinic danger, the operator may proceed before concurrence. Review then begins within one hour.”

“Which means the witness watches a death and files paper.”

“It means disputed necessity does not disappear into an engineering shift report.”

Vale turned toward the technical link on the east wall. A narrow ring reconstruction moved there with the sluggish cadence imposed on public branch testimony. No face occupied the display. Root Jury observers had selected abstract lines after gallery members began treating reconstructed expressions as either sacred proof or theatrical fraud.

“Can the claimant refuse dormant refuge?” Vale asked.

A reply entered through the approved interface. Letters formed in two measured bursts.

REFUSAL WITHOUT ALTERNATIVE MAY COST ANOTHER PERSON.

After a pause, the second line appeared.

CONSENT REQUIRES A CHOICE THAT EXISTS.

Several severance delegates objected at once. They heard coercion in any bargain constrained by compute. Rusk's bloc heard branch authority reaching into operations.
