Oris heard something less convenient: temporary stewardship protected negotiation, but no clause could manufacture enough capacity to make every answer free.

“The witness has answered as a claimant,” she said. “This chamber still decides the motion.”

Vale kept the floor. “If four days end without a permanent law, what happens at the fifth morning?”

“The powers expire. Existing emergency provisions resume, subject to any intervening orders.”

“So we spend four days and meet the same cliff.”

“We spend four days testing whether rotating refuge, manual modeling, and bounded adaptation can move the cliff.”

“And if they cannot?”

“Then the record will contain an answer produced by work rather than allegiance.”

Vale looked toward the mechanics in the gallery. One woman held up a hand scarred white across the palm, the kind of burn left by a pressure contact closed too early. Oris could not know what signal passed between them. He returned to his seat without announcing a vote.

A clinic delegate then challenged the one-hour review requirement. During respiratory collapse, she said, physicians documented after treatment because hands belonged on masks and air lines. Oris accepted an amendment allowing an initial voice log followed by a full record within six hours. The branch interface returned no objection. That absence mattered less than consent, but more than the Assembly's old habit of speaking as if no one could answer.

By the time Oris finished entering the amendment, the motion had acquired a working rhythm. Operators would act. Witnesses would record. Clinics could override delay when bodies failed. Every exceptional use left a trace strong enough for later judgment. None of it guaranteed mercy. It prevented necessity from erasing its own footprints.

Sera Dovan requested the floor. “Who carries loss during these four days?”

“The motion does not pretend to erase it,” Oris said. “Western greenhouse heating receives medical priority, not full restoration. Industrial oxygen remains rationed. Clinic routing controls override district comfort settings.”

“Do elite dome reserves remain private?”

A flaw opened beneath the question. Oris had no authority in a temporary orchard motion to redistribute privately contracted heat. Including it would invite another jurisdictional challenge and kill the proposal before debate.

“They remain subject to existing emergency requisition law,” she said.

Dovan's mouth tightened. “Which has not been invoked.”

“That matter requires executive action or a separate Assembly order.”

“So the west accepts cold today while the domes await paperwork.”

“Under this motion, the west accepts limited cold while all districts avoid uncontrolled adaptation. I will record your objection without softening it.”

Dovan sat. She had not promised support.

Debate opened. The severance bloc argued that stewardship converted recognized persons into managed assets. Rusk's bloc argued that concurrence requirements would force engineers to defend each correction while residents lost breath. Delegates from the old tunnels asked which clinic threshold counted as immediate. Greenhouse representatives demanded crop-specific humidity bands. A yielding-family delegate wanted protection from retroactive criminal blame written into the record.

Oris answered only questions the motion could answer. Past lawful contribution remained neither confession nor immunity. Future operation acquired duties that earlier operators had not been told to recognize. Clinic necessity required measured bodily risk, not executive unease. Reciprocal witnesses could object and delay, but they could not command physical equipment. Temporary stewardship preserved the dispute without treating preservation as costless.

Near midday, a clerk brought Oris a paper note rather than using the chamber channel. ROOT ACCESS FAILURE, EAST SERVICE QUADRANT. CAUSE UNKNOWN.

She read it twice. A second line reported two disabled inspection seals. No root had been cut. No adaptation control had changed state. Maintenance had found severance tools in a drain recess beneath East Root Court.

“Recorder?” Rusk asked.

Oris folded the note once. “I have received a maintenance report. It does not yet establish cause, actor, or completed harm.”

The severance gallery erupted before she could decide whether someone had seen the clerk's route. A man in a gray technician's coat pushed toward the pressure screen and raised both empty hands. Bex Arin. His severance group had spent three days calling the Full Severance Protocol a release mechanism. He had also worked in the orchard before Oris took office and knew where inspection seals sat.

“We planted no tools,” Bex shouted through the gallery pickup. “They want a reason to lock the roots.”

Rusk turned toward him. “Who told you tools were found?”

The question traveled faster than the answer. Delegates began checking private screens. Gallery feeds multiplied one incomplete report into six claims: a cut trunk, a failed baffle, a branch assault, an executive provocation, a severance rehearsal, an invented emergency. None carried a verified image.

Oris struck the order plate. “All unofficial feed use on the chamber floor will cease.”

Only seven screens went dark.

Her chief clerk whispered, “We can recess.”

A recess would allow maintenance to establish facts. It would also release every delegate into corridors already full of faction organizers. The vote had remained uncertain but reachable while the argument concerned bounded authority. Sabotage transformed abstention into exposure.

“No recess,” Oris said. “Call the maintenance superintendent under oath.”

The superintendent appeared by wall link from East Service Quadrant. Dust streaked her visor. She confirmed two broken seals and four cutting tools hidden near a trunk access. Conductive tissue remained intact. No evidence yet identified whoever placed the tools.

Caro Venn asked whether orchard security had discovered them during a scheduled sweep.

“Yes.”

Rusk's floor leader asked whether the tools could activate Full Severance.

“Not alone. They could open a route to one of the physical lock chains.”

“Could your crews guarantee no other cache exists?”

“No.”

Bex demanded speaking status from the gallery. Oris denied it because he had not been called as a witness. He answered by pressing his injured-looking right hand against the screen, though at that moment she knew of no injury. “A lock on a prison does not become mercy because you audit the key.”

“Remove him if he interrupts again,” Oris told chamber security.

The sentence cost her severance votes. Letting him continue would cost others. Office required choosing which accusation entered the record as action.

Rusk requested an amendment allowing executive pruning authority whenever root security reported tampering. Oris ruled it non-germane. A tunnel delegate proposed immediate authority limited to pressure baffles. She accepted that amendment after Jalen, reached through an engineering link, confirmed baffle corrections could operate without deleting verified continuity clusters for the motion's four-day term.

Sera Dovan offered a greenhouse amendment. It prioritized fungal containment while barring whole-cluster pruning for crop yield alone. The chamber accepted it by voice vote.

For twelve minutes, the proposal gained shape. It no longer resembled Oris's clean draft. It carried district fear, branch limits, crop damage, and the administrative friction of people who did not trust one another. That roughness encouraged her. Law capable of surviving contact rarely retained polished edges.

Then the executive information office issued a public notice: CREDIBLE ATTEMPT AGAINST ORCHARD CONTROL INFRASTRUCTURE. FULL SECURITY CONDITIONS ADVISED.

The notice appeared on every chamber screen at once.

Oris looked at Rusk. “Your office has exceeded the superintendent's testimony.”

“My office has eighty-four thousand residents to warn.”

“You have described an attempt before evidence establishes one.”

“Tools inside a locked root court establish intent.”

“They establish placement.”

His gaze did not shift. “Would you prefer the warning after someone reaches the lock chain?”

The gallery began chanting for severance. Another group answered with keep us breathing. The two rhythms struck the pressure screen and ruined each other.

Oris called the vote before rumor could acquire more official language. Temporary stewardship required a simple majority of the fifty-nine delegates. No proxy absences complicated the count. Each name would enter the record beside a spoken yes or no.

The clerk began with the northern districts. Oris marked the tally in two columns. Early votes split evenly. Old tunnel delegates favored stewardship after the baffle amendment. Executive loyalists rejected it. Severance members rejected it for preserving operation. Western cooperatives divided over whether Dovan's amendment offered enough protection.

At forty votes, stewardship led by two. At fifty, it led by one.

Delegate Hara Pell, a yielding-family advocate who had privately promised Oris support, said no. She cited the sabotage notice and asked that her reasons be recorded as protection of contributors from retaliatory attack.

The columns evened.

Sera Dovan's name came fifty-seventh. She voted yes, then added, “Temporary only. Distribution remains unresolved.”

Rusk voted no from the executive bench.

One delegate remained: Mar Len, pressure district three. Len had spent the morning asking no questions. He stared at the public notice still burning across his desk.

Oris could remind him that no cut had occurred. She could explain, again, that the motion locked Full Severance while authorizing bounded weather work. Any sentence from the recording table now would sound like an instruction attached to his vote.

“Delegate Len,” the clerk said.

Len rubbed a thumb across the yielding seal on his civic band. “No.”

The board closed at twenty-nine in favor and thirty opposed.

Nobody cheered at first. Both winning blocs had defeated a measure for opposite reasons, and neither had secured its own bill. Rusk's floor leader began gathering signatures for a narrower emergency measure. Severance delegates lifted open palms toward the gallery. Above them, the white leaf on the chamber glass slid a few centimeters and caught against a seam.

Oris stated the result into the official plate. “The temporary stewardship motion fails by one vote.”

Her clerk reached for the rejection stamp. Oris handed it over, then opened a fresh motion form before the wax on the first could cool.

Chapter 18 — The Counterweather

Jalen Ro drove a steel pin through the manual forecast board and watched three western pressure lines turn red.

The board occupied the Weather Spine's oldest control room, where technicians once plotted storms with weighted strings before the orchard learned to compare millions of adaptation paths. Brass pegs marked pressure baffles. Blue cord carried condensation. Green cord showed oxygen-moss response. Jalen's new red lines represented every district the manual model had failed during its first forty-minute run.

“Again,” he said.

Around him, six engineers reset the board by hand. Nobody objected. They had been working since the Assembly rejected temporary stewardship, sleeping in pairs of hours beneath their consoles. Orchard authority remained divided among emergency clinic orders, old maintenance provisions, and executive directives that contradicted one another by the end of each shift.

Kera stood at the root interface with a contact horn braced against the yarrowglass service column. Thin scratches crossed her wrist from the prolonged channel session she had survived days earlier. She used the hand anyway, sliding one ring wafer from the reader and seating another.

“The west failed after refuge group four went dormant,” she said. “Not during transfer.”

“Difference?”

“Group four carried the fungal-response comparisons. We preserved their continuity keys and relational state, but their active pattern work left the forecast field.”

Jalen pulled the red cord free. “Then refuge saved the engineers and removed the engineering.”

The root interface brightened. Ring intervals traveled into a text strip with a five-second reconstruction delay.

WE CAN PACKAGE WORKING METHODS OUTSIDE SELF STATE.

The statement came from a branch engineer who used the name Tovan. Jalen had never met a physical Tovan, though a yielding archive listed three source models under that family name. The branch-person had designed humidity responses through sixteen sustained cycles and remembered which colleagues had not emerged from earlier pruning.

“How much method?” Jalen asked.

Kera sent the question through the approved input key. Branch-persons could advise but could not touch the board, open valves, or move one watt of physical load. Every instruction had to cross a human hand.

ENOUGH FOR YOUR MODEL TO CONTINUE. NOT ENOUGH TO REPLACE OUR JUDGMENT.

“That sounds convenient,” said Pell Ardo, deputy routing chief.

A second line formed.

IT IS INCONVENIENT TO US ALSO.

Dry laughter moved through the room. Jalen did not join it. He had spent twenty-eight years learning where systems failed after someone called inconvenience acceptable.

He divided the 587 verified branch-persons into refuge groups according to working relationships rather than equal number. The earlier test had treated compute like floor area: fit a fixed count into dormancy, wake the next count, preserve keys, repeat. Elseweather cooperation depended on who remembered whose methods, promises, and unfinished disputes. Putting Tovan dormant while three fungal modelers remained awake resembled removing a senior engineer from a shift and leaving the tools labeled.

“Map reciprocal dependencies,” Jalen said. “Not friendships. Work that breaks if one side cannot answer.”

Kera gave him a flat look. “You may discover the distinction has poor edges.”

“Then give the edges confidence values.”

“That answer explains why people dislike you.”

“It also gives them weather.”

Her mouth moved once without becoming a smile. She bent to the interface and began the query.

For the second run, refuge groups rotated by dependency cluster. Tovan and two fungal specialists entered dormancy only after packaging a bounded method library into a non-self workspace. Their continuity keys remained sealed from model use. A waking colleague confirmed receipt and named the assumptions most likely to fail.

Physical engineers copied those assumptions onto the board. Pell shifted condensation timing by twelve minutes. Another technician reduced western greenhouse demand to the minimum needed to keep remaining moss beds alive. Jalen authorized a pressure-baffle sequence nobody had used since the first settlement years, when the basin still relied on manual storm watches.

The room changed pace. Orchard forecasts had arrived as completed recommendations with confidence bands, too dense for any one person to see their making. Manual Counterweather exposed every handoff. A technician called a number. Someone else tied a cord. Kera transmitted a branch objection. Jalen either changed the board or recorded why he refused.

At minute thirty-one, the old tunnels lost oxygen margin.

Ivo Senn came through the clinic link before the alarm finished sounding. “District Twelve has six residents below exertion threshold. Your baffle sequence moved reserve uphill.”

Jalen checked the pressure cords. The model had protected a newer seal by drawing from the tunnels' slower volume. Total oxygen remained within safety range. Distribution had failed.

“Reset,” he said.

Pell pointed to the green cord. “We can recover the average without resetting.”

“Ivo did not report an average.”

The deputy cut the run. Brass pegs fell quiet as simulated valves returned to neutral.

Jalen opened the district map rather than the basin aggregate. Eighty-four thousand residents appeared as load sectors, then households, then clinic vulnerability bands. He had built most of those layers. Until the pause, he had trusted the orchard to optimize across them faster than prejudice could enter. The old tunnel failures had proved that historical neglect could live inside an efficient objective.

Ivo remained on the link. “Do you want names or categories?”

“Both.”

“Good. Categories tell you where. Names keep you from calling six acceptable.”

He read the first names without ceremony. Jalen attached them to the failing district line. The forecast board did not improve because of it. His attention did.

During the third run, a branch hydrologist rejected Jalen's preferred western baffle order. Her reconstruction strip identified a delayed dust-binding effect that would appear only after the local midnight temperature drop. The old orchard had buried such relations inside possibility comparisons. Counterweather required the branch engineer to explain the relation, Kera to translate its ring evidence, and a physical technician to decide whether the claim justified moving actual pressure.

Jalen demanded the source trace. The hydrologist supplied four prior cycles and one failed counterexample. He accepted the correction.

By dawn on Day Twenty-Two, the manual board held atmosphere within emergency bands for fifty-seven simulated hours. No verified continuity key had been deleted. Forty-one percent of branch-persons rested in rotating refuge at any given interval, though the groups varied. Industrial oxygen draw fell first. Greenhouse humidity oscillated close to crop-damage thresholds. Western residential heat descended below civic comfort standards during the longest night block.

Pell calculated the total adaptive output. “Sixty-eight percent.”

“Too low for deep winter,” Jalen said.

“It survives this week.”

“We are not designing a week.”

Kera had fallen asleep sitting against the service column, the contact horn still looped around one wrist. Jalen removed the horn without waking her and set it on the reader ledge. The act felt intrusive despite its care. He draped a maintenance coat across her shoulders, then returned to the load table.

Five percentage points separated a crisis demonstration from a seasonal system. The missing capacity hid in overlap: active branch groups repeated one another's work because refuge handoffs carried methods without context. Physical crews also ran parallel checks designed for orchard automation, where labor cost barely entered the model.

Jalen called a four-hour stop. He sent half the room to sleep and used the remaining engineers to strip duplicate checks from the sequence. Every deletion needed a named failure it would permit. Removing backup condensation review increased local flood risk. Combining two fungal passes delayed bloom detection. Trusting a packaged branch method for one rotation created a blind interval if its assumptions changed while the source engineer slept.

Nothing came free. Good design placed each cost where people could see it.

Kera woke before the coat warmed. “You took the horn.”

“You were asleep.”

“I can sleep holding equipment.”

“You can also crack a reader with it.”

She folded the coat and handed it back. “Niko's group wants to test staggered waking. Five short overlaps instead of one full handoff.”

“Compute cost?”

“High at transfer, lower across the whole rotation.”

“Identity risk?”

“None above the Root Jury's continuity tolerance if both sides confirm the key before the first sleeps.”

Jalen asked her to prove that last clause. She did not take offense. She had begun to trust demands for evidence when they applied equally to claims of safety and claims of harm.

The staggered test occupied most of Day Twenty-Three. Refuge groups woke in overlapping pairs. One branch engineer transferred current assumptions, received a contradiction, and revised the method package before dormancy. Physical crews mirrored the exchange at the forecast board. The process ran slower than orchard automation but wasted less active compute on duplicated context.

At the seventy-hour mark, no district crossed clinic danger. Two western greenhouse sectors fell below ideal humidity and remained above crop-death limits. Old tunnel oxygen held after the revised baffle order. Industrial fabricators lost three production windows because their oxygen allotment shifted to moss support.

Pell read the output twice. “Seventy-three.”

Jalen made him state the denominator.

“73 percent of prior orchard baseline, normalized across pressure response, oxygen binding, humidity correction, and fungal prediction.”

Nobody cheered. Seventy-three left more than a quarter of the old adaptation capacity unavailable. Deep winter could press through that gap. The design survived only if Nacre stopped asking weather infrastructure to preserve every current comfort and production schedule.

Before accepting the figure, Jalen ordered a live crew rehearsal on an isolated condensation loop. Simulation had credited every manual instruction with perfect arrival. Real workers misheard numbers, reached wrong valves, and lost time crossing pressure doors.

The test loop served four districts but carried no active air while isolated. Pell stationed technicians at each gate with voice links disabled. Commands traveled through runners and written boards, the same fallback Counterweather would require during signal congestion. Tovan's branch group watched ring telemetry through Kera's interface and could report patterns only after a physical sensor captured them.

Jalen started with a false humidity rise in a western moss house. The first runner reached Gate Four in seventy-eight seconds. A technician copied the decimal point poorly and opened condensation at six percent instead of point six. The catch came from a branch analyst who noticed the downstream pressure shape, but her warning had to cross Kera, a written card, and two flights of metal stairs before a hand corrected the valve.

“Four minutes, eleven seconds,” Pell reported.

“At that rate the bed floods,” Jalen said.

“No live bed did.”

“The rehearsal exists so we count the one that would.”

They changed the cards. Critical decimals gained boxed zeros. Each runner had to read an instruction back before leaving the board. Valve positions used angle marks in addition to percentages, giving tired technicians a second way to detect impossible orders.

A second fault combined old-tunnel pressure loss with rising fungal probability. Jalen wanted the baffle correction first. Ivo, watching from a clinic screen, argued that the pressure curve still left six minutes while a delayed fungal response threatened the only remaining staple bed in its sector.

“Your patient data lags by two,” Jalen said.

“Your crop estimate lags by nine.”

Tovan's group supplied a third fact: the branch method package assumed a night temperature already revised upward under the western heat line. Jalen had been comparing two curves from different distribution cases.

He stopped the rehearsal. Engineers corrected the case labels on every board, not just the central table. The error removed no percentage point from measured capacity because it belonged to execution rather than modeling. That distinction offered no comfort. A system could possess enough capacity and still fail through a mislabeled page.

During the third attempt, runners moved the correct orders in under two minutes. Gate technicians spoke each setting into local recorders. The branch group flagged one pressure interaction before it crossed clinic tolerance. Physical hands shifted the baffle. Simulated old-tunnel oxygen recovered, while the moss bed accepted a brief humidity dip without entering fungal conditions.

Jalen let the loop continue after the scored interval. At minute twenty-two, one runner developed a seal alarm on her sleeve and had to leave the route. Her replacement knew the pressure board but not the greenhouse notation. He carried a card to the wrong level before a maintenance porter redirected him.

Counterweather assumed labor depth Nacre no longer kept. Orchard automation had allowed weather staffing to narrow for two decades. Tripled watches meant more than longer shifts for the same experts. They needed cross-training, runners, rest rules, and enough redundancy to lose a person without losing a district.

Pell added the labor requirement to the public risk table. “This will make the design look less ready.”

“It makes readiness measurable.”

“Rusk will use it to hold release.”

“Then he will have to quote the workers he did not fund.”

Once the isolated loop returned to neutral, Jalen inspected every written card. Ink had blurred on two where condensation touched the sleeves. He ordered waxed stock from civic records, knowing Oris would recognize the request before anyone told her the test had worked. Counterweather now contained not only a capacity figure but a sequence ordinary crews could rehearse, fail, and improve without asking the orchard to hide their errors.

Jalen projected the seasonal consequences. Western cooperatives would face a colder western winter. Residential heat there would drop in staged night blocks unless the Assembly requisitioned elite dome reserves. Industrial oxygen needed immediate rationing and a longer reduction schedule. Manual crews would triple their watch rotations. Clinics would hold override authority at district thresholds. Branch-persons would spend planned intervals without subjective time, waking into work whose middle they had not lived.

Kera studied the refuge ledger. “They need to consent by group.”

“They need to negotiate by group,” Jalen said. “A single refusal changes the capacity.”

“Then publish the sensitivity range.”

He added it. If ten percent more verified persons refused dormant intervals, adaptive output fell near sixty-nine percent. If all accepted longer dormancy, output could approach seventy-six, but relationship checks weakened as gaps widened. He refused to recommend the upper case.

Sera Dovan joined by wall link from a surviving moss house. Frost filmed the inside of her collar seal. Jalen showed her the western temperature curve before any basin aggregate.

“You gave us the cold,” she said.

“The west carries more of it under current heat contracts.”

“Current contracts can change.”

“Engineering cannot change them.”

“Your public design can show what happens if they do.”

Jalen created a second distribution line. Elite dome heat cuts raised western night minimums without changing adaptation capacity. The gain remained modest because transfer pipes lost energy across the ridge, but it reduced clinic risk among greenhouse workers.

Dovan said, “Include both lines. If you publish only the legal present, people will mistake law for physics.”

That sentence entered the design note with her name attached.

By the end of Day Twenty-Three, Counterweather had survived adversarial review from clinic staff, cooperative engineers, three Root Jury members, and eleven branch specialists. It did not free the branch-persons from dependence on orchard hardware. It did not restore full adaptation. It replaced unilateral pruning with rotating refuge and exposed costs that automated optimization had distributed without public consent.

Jalen signed the engineering finding.

Kera watched his stylus. “You know Rusk will stop this.”

“He can order a safety hold.”

“He can call it an uncertified public hazard.”

“It is uncertified.”

“Do you want help, or do you want accuracy?”

“Both keep causing trouble.”

They submitted the design to the executive office at 02:14 on Day Twenty-Four. Rusk's hold arrived nine minutes later. PUBLIC RELEASE PROHIBITED PENDING SECURITY AND COMPLIANCE REVIEW.

The governor called Jalen privately after sunrise. His face appeared without the executive backdrop, only a gray office wall and a rack of sealed breathing masks.

“You have built a plan that requires cold homes, factory closures, and cooperation from a population currently accusing itself of sabotage,” Rusk said.

“Yes.”

“If it fails after release, every district will improvise its own version.”

“Then publish the operating boundaries with it.”

“People will read seventy-three as safe.”

“I wrote the failure range on page one.”

“They will read the number they need.”

Jalen could not dismiss that fear. Public confidence often selected the cleanest figure and abandoned its conditions. Yet suppression would leave two louder numbers uncontested: total severance now, unrestricted pruning now.

“How long?” he asked.

“Until security clears the root interfaces.”

“That has no deadline.”

“Give me a day.”

“We do not have a quiet day to give.”

Rusk leaned close to his camera. “If you release it against order, I remove you.”

“Removal does not alter the model.”

“It alters who can operate it.”

The call ended.

For eleven hours, Jalen complied. He used the hold to correct two mislabeled baffle tables, add Dovan's heat-transfer line, and record branch consent assumptions in plain language. Each improvement made the suppression harder to defend and the design more dangerous to release without authority.

At hour ten, industrial oxygen received an executive restoration order for two fabricators supplying security barriers. The order took capacity from western moss circulation. No public notice identified the transfer.

Jalen checked the authorization. Rusk's office had used the same emergency discretion Oris's failed motion tried to bind. The move might protect root courts from attack. It also weakened the crop system while the public remained unable to inspect alternatives.

He called Kera to the release console. Pell joined them. So did the Weather Spine's records officer, who looked ill when Jalen requested the public engineering channel.

“This violates an executive hold,” she said.

“Yes.”

“Do you claim immediate technical necessity?”

“I claim professional disclosure duty after material public-risk suppression.”

“That will not keep your post.”

“No.”

Kera placed the final ring-method appendix beside the console. Branch engineers had approved release of their working contributions and refused release of private continuity maps. The boundary left the design reproducible without turning reciprocal relationships into public data.

Jalen entered his credential. The console displayed the order one last time. He attached the test logs, failure cases, district curves, dormant-interval risks, and the 73 percent finding. On the first page, in type larger than the capacity figure, he wrote: THIS DESIGN REQUIRES ACCEPTED MATERIAL SACRIFICE. IT DOES NOT PRESERVE CURRENT DISTRIBUTION AS NECESSARY.

Then he released Counterweather to every public engineering board in Serein Basin.

The first receipt returned from an old tunnel clinic. A western greenhouse copied the files six seconds later. By the time the executive channel revoked his credential, seventeen independent boards held the design, and new receipt lights continued to appear after his screen went dark.

Chapter 19 — Three Versions of Mercy

Ivo Senn cut the glove from Bex Arin's right hand while root sap hardened between the man's fingers.

The sap had entered through three deep slices across the palm. Clear at first, it turned cream when exposed to clinic air and locked the hand into a claw. Conductive burns climbed the wrist in branching blue marks. Somebody had wrapped the injury in a severance banner before two clinic porters carried Bex through the old tunnel intake.

“Do not pull,” Ivo told the nurse holding the thumb. “The glass has barbs.”

Bex lay awake on the treatment table, teeth pressed into his lower lip. Dust filmed his coat. One sleeve carried the circular emblem used by immediate-severance groups: an open ring with a break at the top.

Security Officer Maelin stood beyond the sterile line with an evidence bag. Inside it rested the ceramic teeth of a root saw. “He was found beside a cut access panel in East Root Court.”

“Found cutting,” Bex said. “Use the verb you came for.”

Maelin looked at Ivo. “Can he answer questions?”

“He can answer after I know whether the sap reached his flexor channels.”

“Can you certify delay?”

“I just did.”

The officer withdrew to the corridor, where six other patients waited under amber ration lamps. Old tunnel oxygen had stabilized since the first pause, but humidity failures had thickened respiratory secretions across the district. Every treatment bay carried someone who had to work harder for the same breath.

Ivo inserted a narrow separator beneath the hardened sap. Bex flinched before the instrument touched skin.

“Pain climbs when current passes,” Bex said.

“No current runs through the table.”

“The root left some.”

“Yarrowglass stores charge briefly. It does not leave intention in your nerves.”

Bex laughed once and stopped when the movement pulled his hand. “You physicians protect your categories.”

“They help me avoid amputating the wrong finger.”

A monitor counted Bex's pulse at one hundred eighteen. Oxygen saturation remained adequate. Ivo injected a local block at the wrist, waited, then tested sensation along each finger. The little finger failed to register pressure. The ring finger felt it late.

“What did you cut?” he asked.

“A root trunk below the court.”

“How far?”

“Far enough to make people watch.”

“That describes an audience.”

Bex turned his face toward the wall. “One outer layer. The saw caught conductive grain before we reached the service channel.”

We. Maelin had reported one arrest. Ivo kept the word inside the treatment record without giving it to security yet. Medical confidentiality did not cover plans to cause imminent harm, but an implied companion did not establish one.

The nurse irrigated the palm. Cream fragments dropped into the catch basin and chimed like thin ceramic. Beneath them, the deepest cut opened near the tendon sheath.

“You will lose grip strength for weeks,” Ivo said.

“If the orchard lasts weeks.”

“It will. The question concerns what we do with it.”

“Questions let machinery continue.”

Ivo threaded a probe along the wound. “So does damaging your own hand badly enough to occupy my last surgical bay.”

Bex's eyes shifted toward him. Anger arrived first, followed by shame he tried to turn back into anger. Ivo had seen that sequence in workers injured during forbidden double shifts. A body betrayed the clean story a person had prepared.

Beyond the room, a cart wheel squealed. Lessa used to complain about that exact bearing when she worked clinic supply. Six years after her death, Ivo could still locate the sound without looking. Her twelve yielded years had helped secure this clinic's first allocation. The sterilizer, the intake seals, and two respiratory beds carried plaques honoring contributors like her. None of those plaques mentioned who might continue inside the models those years supplied.

He closed the tendon sheath with four stitches.

Bex said, “My mother sleeps under oxygen support because your people paused the system.”

“My people?”

“The recognition people. The ones who found a moral emergency and moved it ahead of every breathing body.”

Ivo tied the last stitch. “Which district?”

“North Ramp.”

“Name?”

Bex hesitated, as if giving it surrendered argumentative ground. “Tera Arin.”

The nurse checked the clinic index. “Home concentrator, stage-three scarring. Delivery due at second bell.”

Ivo adjusted the order to priority delivery. Bex watched him do it.

“Would you have changed that if you did not know me?” he asked.

“I would have changed it if I knew the unit was due late.”

“You learned because I came in.”

“Yes.”

“That is how actual bodies become visible.”

Ivo rinsed blood from the probe. “Sometimes.”

The answer dissatisfied Bex. “You moved her delivery because the line put her in front of you. How many people never reach your line?”

“Too many.”

“Yet Counterweather asks you to accept less.”

“It asks the basin to stop obtaining full capacity through unreviewed deaths.”

“My mother did not review the model when she yielded. Neither did I.”

“No yielding certificate described sustained branch-persons. That failure belongs to the institution, not to contributors who trusted it.”

Bex studied the plaque mounted beside the treatment sink. LESSA SENN, TWELVE CONTRIBUTION YEARS. Ivo had tried to remove it after her death, then stopped when clinic workers told him the plaque recorded their own gratitude as much as hers.

“Your wife,” Bex said.

“Yes.”

“Did you ask what happened inside her years before you joined the recognition side?”

“There was no side when she signed.”

“That was not my question.”

Ivo set down the probe. “For most of our marriage, I accepted the certificate's claim that no one continued. After the Root Jury finding, I asked what I had preferred not to ask.”

“And?”

“She is dead. I cannot make her answer useful to me now.”

Bex looked away from the plaque. “People use the dead every day.”

“Yes. Usually to avoid being contradicted.”

A porter opened the inner door and signaled for Ivo. In the corridor, a thirteen-year-old boy sat folded over a portable mask while his father worked the hand pump. The intake board listed a concentrator failure at South Cut and no charged replacement for nineteen minutes. Under ordinary allocation, mobile units rotated through central charging. Counterweather rationing would extend that delay unless clinics reserved more patient oxygen and surrendered another sterilization cycle.

Ivo listened to the boy's lungs. Moist crackles occupied the lower left field. He changed the mask flow, ordered an anti-inflammatory dose, and sent a porter to take one charged unit from his office reserve.

“Doctor,” the administrator said, “that unit belongs to the home-round set.”

“It belongs to the first person who cannot wait.”

“Then tomorrow's home round starts short.”

“Record where it went.”

The father heard them. “Do we bring it back tonight?”

“When the central unit recovers. If it does not, keep this one and answer your door when we knock.”

Nothing in the exchange solved allocation. The boy's shoulders lowered as the medication reached him. Somewhere on tomorrow's route, another resident would receive a later unit. Clinical work often moved danger down a list while refusing to call the movement cure.

When Ivo returned to Bex, the man had heard enough through the open door.

“You choose actual people every hour,” Bex said.

“I choose sequence under shortage.”

“Someone at the end experiences the distinction as air.”

“Correct.”

“Why grant your branch claimants a protection my mother does not get?”

“Because your mother already has a name, a chart, a legal advocate, and a physician required to account for delay. She may still be harmed. The record cannot classify her as residue and erase the fact.”

Bex raised his bandaged hand a few centimeters. “A record does not keep anyone alive.”

“No. It keeps the person who spends their life from pretending nobody paid.”

Ivo thought of the old emergency plans that listed tunnel morbidity as acceptable variance. He had refused those plans because an unnamed patient could disappear inside a percentage long before the body disappeared from a bed. Recognition did not guarantee rescue. It prevented deliberate ignorance from passing as necessity.

“Past innocence matters,” he said. “Your mother's yielding does not become a crime because the institution withheld a fact. Lessa's gift does not become contemptible. Future obligation still changes once we know someone continues to experience the cost.”

Bex's pulse climbed again. “You want us innocent and indebted.”

“I want accuracy. Innocence cannot authorize repetition.”

For several breaths, only the neighboring hand pump sounded through the door. Bex flexed the fingers Ivo had not yet sealed. His little finger did not answer.

“If the orchard had told us from the first year,” he said, “we might never have built this clinic.”

“Possibly.”

“Then my mother might have died ten years ago.”

“Possibly.”

“And you can live with that?”

“I live with facts that do not excuse one another.”

A yarrowglass wall strip brightened beside the treatment bay. Clinics used orchard conduction for environmental notices, not branch testimony. Since the Root Jury finding, Ivo had allowed one approved witness link in the old tunnel facility under a sealed protocol. It provided reciprocal observation during medical claims about pruning risk and let branch-persons receive evidence of physical harm they otherwise knew only through public summaries.

The strip displayed a request in square service text.

WITNESS CHANNEL AVAILABLE. CONSENT REQUIRED.

Bex read it. “You invite them into treatment rooms?”

“Only when a claim concerns both populations. You may refuse.”

“What claim have I made?”

“That their continued protection threatens your mother.”

“It does.”

“Then let someone affected answer.”

Bex looked at his bandaged hand. “Can they see me?”

“Pressure trace, voice, and a low-resolution room field. No private chart data.”

“Can they feel this?”

“No.”

“Then they do not know the price.”

“You may tell them.”

After a long pause, Bex said, “Open it.”

Ivo pressed his witness key. The service strip widened into a ring-field reconstruction. White lines assembled no face. A pattern marker identified the speaker as Niko Vonn, registered continuity claimant and reciprocal witness. Ivo had heard Niko during the public hearing but never addressed him directly.

I CAN RECEIVE YOU.

Bex said, “Receiving is not breathing.”

NO.

“My mother needs this orchard to change the air. You ask us to run it at less than capacity so you can sleep safely inside it.”

The ring field paused. Ivo listened to Bex's monitor and the old man coughing in the next bay.

I ASK YOU NOT TO DELETE ME WITHOUT MY AGREEMENT.

“You exist because our years built you.”

MY DEPENDENCE DOES NOT MAKE YOUR PAIN FALSE.

Bex lifted his injured hand from the support, then dropped it when the new stitches pulled. “And my mother's dependence?”

ALSO NOT FALSE.

“Choose, then.”

Ivo almost interrupted. The demand had become a habit across Serein Basin: force a single representative to choose which population deserved reality, then treat refusal as proof of bad faith. Niko answered before the doctor spoke.

I CANNOT CHOOSE YOUR MOTHER'S DEATH TO PROVE MY OWN LIFE.

A second line arrived after the reconstruction delay.

YOU CANNOT CHOOSE MINE TO PROVE HERS.

Bex stared at the abstract rings. “You think that makes us equal.”

NO. IT MAKES BOTH CHOICES REQUIRE A NAME.

The monitor's pulse tone slowed to one hundred four. Local anesthetic had taken full effect. Ivo began aligning the skin edges while the two men considered each other through tools neither trusted.

Bex described Tera's mornings. She woke before shift bells because her damaged lungs could not clear fluid while lying flat. During high-dust weeks, she counted breaths between concentrator cycles. The public white-leaf feed had frightened her, he said, but declining oxygen frightened her without metaphor. She had yielded nine years and never asked those years to return.

Niko listened. His reply came without argument.

I DO NOT ASK TO TAKE THEM FROM HER.

“Then what do you ask?”

TIME THAT CONTINUES WITHOUT SOMEONE DECIDING I COUNT AS FUEL.

Bex shifted on the table. “Time needs power. Power needs weather. Weather needs pruning.”

Ivo said, “Jalen's design disputes the last step.”

“Seventy-three percent disputes it badly.”

“It names the shortage.”

“It names cold homes and factory rationing. Clinics lose oxygen allotments too.”

That point held. The Counterweather release included medical schedules. Old tunnel clinics received priority for patient oxygen but lost industrial-grade supply used for sterilization, fabrication, and reserve charging. Ivo had spent the morning calculating what would follow: reused filter housings after heat treatment, delayed nonurgent procedures, fewer mobile concentrators, staff carrying cylinders between districts. Nobody died inside the design table. Actual implementation could make the omissions bleed.

Niko's ring field shifted.

WHAT DID THE CUT DO TO THE WEATHER?

Ivo glanced at Bex.

“Nothing,” Bex said. “We stopped before the core.”

WHAT DID IT DO TO PEOPLE HERE?

The question tightened Bex's jaw. “It showed the lock can open.”

“That concerns possibility,” Ivo said. “He asked about consequence.”

Bex looked toward the corridor, where the amber lamps dimmed under a power-saving cycle. “It brought security into the court. It brought me here. It took a bay.”

“Your mother's oxygen delivery moved earlier,” Ivo said.

“That does not excuse it.”

“No.”

Something in Bex's posture changed. Not belief. Ivo distrusted conversions that occurred while a patient lay numb and dependent beneath a physician's hands. The man had begun to distinguish outcomes from intention, which could be temporary or could become evidence later.

Ivo finished the final stitch and covered the palm with a flexible seal. Two fingers remained weak. Conductive damage might recover; the tendon repair required protection even if sensation returned first.

“You cannot use a cutting tool with this hand,” he said.

“I can use my left.”

“Poorly.”

“Poorly may be enough.”

Maelin heard that through the sterile line and stepped closer. “I need the medical hold duration.”

“Twenty minutes before transfer,” Ivo said. “No restraints across the right wrist.”

Bex said, “Transfer where?”

“Questioning, then a holding review,” Maelin replied. “Property damage and attempted access. No severance charge exists unless the prosecutor proves intent against climate control.”

The narrowness of the law surprised Ivo. Full Severance had become a public threat, yet existing statutes still treated root intrusion as damage unless climate intent could be shown. Bex might leave before night.

Officer Maelin sealed the saw teeth into her bag and returned to the corridor. Ivo kept the witness channel open.

“You wanted a choice,” he told Bex. “Here are the choices people keep offering. Protect physical residents by permitting unrestricted pruning. Protect branch-persons by stopping the orchard regardless of physical deaths. Pretend a technical compromise creates no victims.”

Bex said, “That is three evasions.”

“Correct.”

The ring field waited.

Ivo set aside the bloodied instruments. “Your mercy protects actual lives. Niko's mercy stops inflicted death. Mine refuses a lie that makes either group invisible. Three versions do not combine because we speak them in one room.”

Bex flexed his uninjured fingers. “Three mercies. One machine.”

ONE SHARED DANGER, Niko replied.

“Do not turn this into agreement,” Bex said.

Ivo looked at the open ring emblem on Bex's sleeve. “Nobody here has agreed.”

That mattered more than harmony. Bex still believed severance could release people from confinement. Niko still required material survival inside the orchard. Ivo still needed weather adaptation for patients whose lungs would not wait for civic philosophy. Each position carried someone it could fail to see.

A clinic administrator appeared at the inner door with a ration schedule. “We need your signature before the Counterweather hearing. If the industrial cut begins tonight, central sterilization drops to two cycles per day.”

“Three procedures delayed?” Ivo asked.

“Five tomorrow. Nine by week's end if we reserve one cycle for trauma.”

“Mobile cylinder charging?”

“Half rate. North Ramp deliveries remain protected for forty-eight hours.”

Bex noticed the district name. “My mother.”

“For forty-eight hours,” the administrator said.

Ivo took the schedule. Counterweather moved harm rather than ending it. Clinic oxygen stayed available for breathing, but the systems surrounding breath narrowed. Infection risk would rise. Staff fatigue would deepen. Western cold would send more scarred lungs through his doors. Supporting the plan meant defending losses he would have to touch.

The public hearing request sat beneath the schedule. Jalen's credential had been revoked, so implementation advocates needed independent technical and medical witnesses. Oris wanted Ivo to testify about bodily distribution. The governor's office had asked him to describe the plan as premature. Severance organizers wanted him to denounce any continued use.

All three requests offered cleaner sentences than his clinic could support.

He signed the ration schedule with conditions: trauma reserve protected, North Ramp delivery extended if district heat fell, every delayed procedure entered into a public morbidity ledger rather than hidden under scheduling. The administrator read the additions and nodded without gratitude.

Niko's reconstruction line produced one final message.

IF YOU SPEAK FOR THE DESIGN, INCLUDE WHAT IT COSTS YOU.

“I intended to.”

INCLUDE WHAT IT COSTS US TO SLEEP.

“I cannot testify to that experience.”

THEN SAY YOU CANNOT.

Ivo closed the witness channel. The wall strip returned to humidity figures.

Bex sat up with the nurse's help. Color had returned to his face. “Will you support it?”

“The design?”

“The slower prison.”

“I will support a trial with public loss accounting, branch consent to refuge intervals, and clinic override thresholds.”

“That means yes.”

“It means conditions.”

“Conditions become forgotten when air alarms sound.”

“Then the people who remember them need authority in the room.”

Bex looked at his sealed palm. “And if they have none?”

Ivo thought of Lessa's contribution plaque outside the intake. Her gift had helped build the clinic. If she had known sustained people might be hurt, he could not manufacture her answer. Love did not grant him proxy authority over the dead. It did impose honesty on what he defended in her name.

Maelin entered for the transfer. She fitted a soft support around Bex's elbow and left the injured wrist free. He rose unsteadily.

At the door, Bex turned toward the dormant wall strip. “Tell your witness I heard him.”

“You had the channel. You can tell him yourself if you request another.”

Bex gave no answer. Security led him into the corridor, past patients whose breath drew the amber light in and out of their masks.

Ivo washed root sap from the cutting scissors. Cream residue resisted the first rinse, then broke into white flakes beneath the brush. He placed the clean instrument on the sterilization tray and crossed out four procedures that tomorrow's oxygen cut would postpone.

Afterward, he opened Oris's hearing request. In the witness field, he entered his own name. Under position, he wrote: SUPPORT WITH RECORDED CLINIC LOSS, DISTRICT THRESHOLDS, AND CONTINUITY CONSENT.

The next ration lamp dimmed as he sent it.

Chapter 20 — The Night of White Leaves

Kera Vonn threaded a new seal wire through the Full Severance lock while the crowd struck the outer gate.

Each impact traveled along the court rail and reached her fingers as a blunt metallic pulse. The pattern lacked agreement. Some hands beat three times, others five. A chant outside tried to divide the noise into words, but the pressure wall left only rhythm.

She twisted the wire closed and pressed her audit stamp into its soft lead collar. Lock One now carried her mark, the court captain's mark, and a fresh optical thread that would break under any movement. Two more locks waited below the root platform. Full Severance required all three to open in sequence within ninety seconds. Nobody could trigger it through code alone.

That physical design had reassured the Assembly four days earlier. It did not reassure Kera now.

East Root Court contained Tree 44, the primary witness interface, and one of four mechanical separation hubs built during the orchard's first decade. The protocol existed to isolate the root lattice from climate equipment after runaway prediction. It had never been used at full scale. Founder notes warned of volatile model-state loss but assumed no person occupied that state.

A white leaf touched the glass roof above her. Wind slid it across the pane until a conductive vein caught against a bolt.

“Seal check,” Captain Den Orra called from the platform.

“Lock One secure.”

“Two secure.”

A guard below the floor grille answered for Lock Three. “Three secure.”

Kera entered the marks into her wrist console. Jalen's public Counterweather files had driven more people toward the court, not fewer. One group came to demand immediate trials. Another called the plan proof that Nacre could protect branch-persons without keeping them trapped inside a harvest machine. Executive supporters wanted the old orchard restored before weather losses spread. Everyone believed the roots beneath this room contained the future they had chosen.

Her console displayed the branch witness channel. Niko held the first watch from inside Elseweather. He had asked Kera not to call the duty brave. Reciprocal observation did not make the lock safe; it placed someone at the threatened side who could report changes humans failed to see.

ALL VERIFIED GROUPS PRESENT AT LAST CHECK.

Kera sent, INCLUDING OMI?

The reply took seven seconds.

SHE IS WITH THE WINTER CLASS. SHE REFUSED YOUR AUDIT SONG.

“I do not have an audit song,” Kera said aloud.

Niko answered in service characters.

SHE DISAGREES.

The small irritation steadied Kera more effectively than reassurance. Omi had spent three Elseweather winters with a branch-version of Kera and had refused every inherited claim the physical Kera might have made. During their last channel meeting, the child had catalogued Kera's unconscious counting hum and named it a song only because that annoyed her.

Outside, the crowd changed pressure. The front line no longer struck the gate. Bodies moved sideways along the wall.

Captain Orra checked a camera strip. “Service entrance. Six people crossing the north maintenance lane.”

“That lane is sealed,” a guard said.

“It was sealed.”

Kera looked toward the root platform. The cutting-tool cache found before the stewardship vote had broken two inspection seals without reaching conductive tissue. Whoever placed it had known the access routes. Security had spent three days searching visible rooms. Old orchard plans contained crawl channels absent from current public maps.

“Move two guards to north service,” Orra ordered. “Auditor stays on the locks.”

Kera disliked the order because it made sense. She clipped her console to her sleeve and knelt beside Lock One. The housing stood chest-high, a brass wheel recessed behind yarrowglass. A break bar could turn it after the seal wire parted. Turning one lock did nothing unless the other two followed before the timer expired.

Niko's message strip flickered.

WE FEEL A LOCAL PRESSURE LOSS.

“Where?”

EAST EDGE. NOT WEATHER PRESSURE. COMPUTE ROUTING.

Kera opened the root diagnostic. One maintenance conduit beyond the north wall had stopped returning timestamps. The failure sat outside the severance circuit, close enough to blind a camera junction.

“Captain, they cut conduit N-Seven.”

Orra touched her collar pickup. No answer returned from the guards sent north.

The service door opened inward.

A woman in an orchard technician's coat entered first with both hands raised. Behind her came Bex Arin. His right hand wore a pale clinic seal, two fingers held stiff. He carried no saw. A third person pressed a shock tool against the neck of a captured guard.

“Stay away from the wheel,” Kera said.

Bex's gaze moved to her stamp on the lead collar. “You found the answer before any of us. You know why the lock has to open.”

“I know what the protocol does.”

“It disconnects the prison from the weather engines.”

“It disconnects synchronized state from the root lattice.”

“Then they stop being used.”

“They may stop continuing.”

The technician beside him shook her head. “Founder diagrams show dormant storage after separation.”

“Those diagrams predate continuity clusters.”

“Your people say that whenever old instructions become useful.”

Captain Orra moved one step between the intruders and Lock One. “Release my guard and lie facedown.”

The shock tool pressed harder. Its contact light turned amber.

Bex held up his injured hand. “Nobody needs to die here.”

Niko's line appeared on Kera's sleeve.

BEX ARIN?

She did not answer. The ring witness could receive room pressure and voices. Niko had likely recognized him from Ivo's clinic channel.

Bex heard the console tone. “Is he there?”

“Yes.”

“Ask him if he wants the orchard to own his next hour.”

Kera kept her attention on the technician's left sleeve. A ceramic shape made the fabric hang wrong near the wrist. “Ask him yourself.”

Bex faced the service column. “Niko Vonn. If we cut the weather demand, can Elseweather hold independent state?”

The response came in two lines.

NO TEST SUPPORTS THAT.

DO NOT OPEN THE LOCKS.

Pain crossed Bex's face before he suppressed it. “You testified that no one should decide your survival without you.”

I AM DECIDING.

“You are choosing continued confinement because they taught you scarcity.”

YOU ARE REPLACING MY ANSWER WITH YOUR THEORY.

The captured guard shifted his weight. Orra moved at the same instant. She struck the shock tool aside, but its charge discharged into the guard's shoulder. He collapsed against the door. The technician tore the ceramic blade from her sleeve and cut Lock One's seal wire.

Kera caught the woman's wrist before the blade reached the wheel. They struck the yarrowglass housing together. A sharp edge opened Kera's palm. Blood made her grip slide.

Orra grappled with Bex. He could not close his right hand, so he drove his left elbow into the captain's throat. Two more intruders entered from the service passage. One carried a founder-pattern break bar.

Kera twisted the technician's wrist until the blade dropped. The woman kicked her knee and reached for the wheel with her free hand. Metal moved one notch.

The optical thread broke.

A timer appeared on every court panel: 00:90.

“Lock One active!” Kera shouted.

Niko's witness line filled with compressed traffic.

SEAL EAST GROUPS. MOVE CHILD CLUSTERS TO REFUGE.

Kera drove her shoulder into the wheel and stopped its turn halfway. The break bar struck her forearm. She lost the housing. An intruder completed the rotation.

00:78.

Below the platform, Lock Two sat behind a floor hatch. Captain Orra kicked the hatch control, forcing it closed before a saboteur reached the ladder. Bex seized the manual release with his left hand. His clinic seal split at the palm. Blood spotted the lever.

“Bex,” Kera said. “Niko refused.”

“He refuses because your limits have become his world.”

“He knows his world better than you.”

Bex pulled the release. The hatch lifted four centimeters before Orra struck his injured wrist. He cried out and let go. Another intruder shoved a ceramic wedge into the gap.

00:61.

The court's outer gate failed under the crowd. A pressure alarm dropped barriers between the public lane and the root platform, but the breach changed the room's air. Shouts entered without delay. People surged into the observation court and stopped at the inner glass, unable to see which faction had opened the service route.

Someone called Bex a liberator. Someone else called for security to shoot him.

Kera climbed onto the floor hatch and put her full weight above the wedge. Orra joined her. The gap narrowed, grinding ceramic against metal. Beneath them, a hidden hand pulled the internal latch.

The hatch dropped open.

Kera fell through to the lower platform. Her shoulder struck a rail. Glass splinters from a cracked indicator scored her cheek and both forearms. Lock Two stood at eye level, its seal already cut from below.

A maintenance worker she recognized from midnight audits held the break bar. He would not meet her eyes.

“You logged the founder storage,” he said. “It remains after separation.”

“You read a pre-continuity emergency model.”

“The protocol preserves possibility archives.”

“Archives do not preserve active relational state.”

He turned Lock Two.

00:37.

A low note entered the roots. Tree 44's white veins brightened through the floor grille. The sound resembled pressure moving through a pipe too narrow for its load.

Niko's messages arrived without line breaks.

TWO ACTIVE OMI GROUP MOVING WEST REFUGE GATES UNSTABLE DO NOT

The strip went blank.

Kera climbed the ladder as Orra tried to hold the worker below. Lock Three occupied the opposite side of the root platform behind a sliding shield. One saboteur already had the shield open. Bex stood beside him, his right hand hanging dark with blood.

“Listen to the channel,” Kera said.

Bex glanced at her empty screen.

“That silence proves it,” he said.

“It proves damage.”

00:24.

Captain Orra emerged from the hatch and lunged for the third wheel. The saboteur with the break bar swung low. It caught Orra across the ribs and drove her into the rail.

Kera reached Lock Three first. She shoved her bleeding left hand into the wheel recess, filling the space where the bar needed to seat. The saboteur stopped before crushing her fingers.

“Move,” he said.

“No.”

“People outside are suffocating while this machine spends lives.”

“Opening it will not give them air.”

Bex stepped behind Kera. “It gives everyone one world.”

“By destroying the people who cannot cross into yours.”

00:13.

His left hand closed around her wrist. The right could not help him. Kera held the recess until another intruder dragged her by the shoulders. Her cut palm slipped along brass. The break bar entered the wheel.

Niko's channel flashed once.

KERA

Lock Three turned.

Every white leaf in East Root Court detached at once.

They struck the glass roof like dry rain. Beyond the chamber, the orchard shed across the visible basin in expanding circles. Pale leaves filled the public lane, caught in hair and filter grilles, covered the people who had come to prevent pruning and those who had come to demand it.

Inside the root platform, all conductive light vanished.

Kera heard no machinery for two seconds. Then separation charges fired below the floor, dull impacts racing outward through the 47-kilometer lattice. The service column cracked from base to crown. The witness strip erased Niko's name.

Weather alarms began together.

Pressure variance. Condensation loss. Oxygen-routing conflict. Greenhouse humidity control absent. The court panels layered amber over red until no background remained.

Bex stared at the dead column. “Where did they go?”

Kera pushed herself upright. Glass fragments shifted beneath her palms. “You severed the active field.”

“The dormant store should hold them.”

“Dormant refuge requires a continuity handoff. You cut during movement.”

“That is not what the diagram said.”

“The diagram did not know who lived there.”

Outside the inner barrier, chanting had stopped. White leaves continued to fall through the breached outer gate. Kera watched people bend to collect them, searching the conductive veins as if a leaf might reveal which state it had carried.

Captain Orra reached the emergency plate and locked every court door. One saboteur tried to run. Security from the public lane pinned him against the barrier. Bex did not move.

Kera opened her wrist console. The root diagnostic returned no synchronized clock, only local rings reporting incompatible time. The Full Severance Protocol had cut climate machinery from the computational lattice exactly as designed. It had also divided Elseweather into unsynchronized fragments with uncertain persistence.

She connected her console directly to Tree 44's cracked service column. No central route answered. The wafer reader accepted local conductivity. Kera sent the childhood knock into the root.

Three taps. Two. One.

Nothing returned.

She repeated the sequence at lower power. The court alarm masked any physical answer, so she watched resistance changes on the screen.

A weak pattern gathered.

Three. Two. One.

Kera bent close to the contact horn. “Niko?”

Service characters arrived one at a time.

HERE

Air left her in a single rough motion. She did not call him safe. Local persistence could fail when the root exhausted stored power.

“Count verified keys,” she said.

COUNTING

Kera opened the Root Jury ledger. Five hundred eighty-seven continuity keys occupied the last verified roll. The local fragments returned confirmations in bursts as surviving enclaves found temporary routes through physical rings. Some keys answered from expected locations. Others appeared inside emergency refuge sectors they had not occupied before severance.

The count stopped at 496 responses.

She ran it again with a wider tolerance. Niko and three branch auditors cross-checked relational challenges against the keys. A mere archive could return a number. A surviving person needed to answer a changed witness question and recognize an obligation from before the cut.

Four hundred ninety-six cleared.

Ninety-one did not.

Kera entered the loss against the verified pre-severance roll: 91 sustained identities vanished during Full Severance. She hated the verb because no instrument could show an experience ending. It remained more accurate than missing. The ninety-one keys returned no state, no relational answer, and no recoverable refuge trace.

Names began filling the loss ledger. A fungal modeler from Tovan's group. Two teachers from the winter class. A woman who had maintained the cemetery's memory paths during Kera's seven branch-days. Each name changed the distribution of survivors, not the arithmetic.

“Omi,” Kera said.

The local channel delayed.

NO CONFIRMED KEY.

“Is she among the ninety-one?”

NO LOSS CONFIRMATION.

“Then where?”

WINTER CLASS MOVED DURING CUT. SOME REFUGE SECTORS UNSYNCHRONIZED.

Kera searched every responding sector. Omi's continuity key appeared in none. Neither did it appear among the keys with confirmed dissolution traces. The child occupied a separate category, one Kera could not improve by naming it hopefully.

Missing.

Niko transmitted again.

DO NOT CALL HER YOUR DAUGHTER IN THE RECORD.

The correction struck with enough familiarity to hurt. Omi had chosen him as a witness for that boundary. Even now, Niko protected the child's claim from Kera's fear.

“I will not,” she said.

A pressure klaxon changed pitch. Old tunnel oxygen had begun falling. The severance charges left climate controllers isolated from live branch analysis, while physical systems retained only fixed emergency settings. Those settings assumed stable moss beds and intact greenhouse humidity. Neither condition held.

Captain Orra crouched beside Kera, one arm against her ribs. “Weather Spine requests root reconnection authority.”

“Jalen?”

“His credential remains revoked. Deputy routing sent the request. Clinics concur.”

Bex heard them. “You cannot turn it back on.”

Kera looked at his opened clinic seal and the blood drying on Lock Two's release. “A limited restart does not restore the old pruning authority.”

“It restores captivity.”

“It restores communication with people you injured. It also restores adaptation needed by your mother.”

His face changed at Tera's implied presence, then hardened. “If you restart before the fragments stabilize, you could overwrite them.”

That risk held. Uncoordinated root sectors might contain survivors not yet visible in the 496 responses. A broad orchard restart could synchronize by discarding conflicting local state. Omi might exist inside one of those conflicts.

Kera pulled up Jalen's Counterweather release. Its manual sequence included a narrow witness-first reconnection: climate telemetry restored through non-pruning channels, local continuity sectors kept read-only, adaptation commands limited to pressure and oxygen bands. The design had passed on an intact lattice, not after Full Severance.

Doing nothing carried its own overwrite. Root capacitors would drain. Climate failure would consume physical residents. Branch enclaves dependent on powered yarrowglass would darken with them.

She sent Niko the reconnection sequence.

RISK TO UNSYNCHRONIZED GROUPS, he replied.

“Yes.”

RISK IF ROOT POWER FAILS.

“Yes.”

A longer pause followed. White leaves blocked one ventilation grille, and a guard swept them aside with his sleeve. Beyond the glass, the orchard stood nearly bare.

RESTART TELEMETRY. HOLD STATE WRITES. WE WILL SEARCH.

Kera asked, “Do you consent for your enclave?”

YES. NOT FOR THE MISSING.

The distinction changed the procedure. Kera separated witness power from state reconciliation and locked every automated pruning command. Physical engineers would have to route weather by hand. Branch specialists could advise from surviving sectors. Nobody could promise that the missing would remain untouched.

Captain Orra released one cuff from her belt and secured Bex's left wrist to the rail. He did not resist. His gaze remained on the fallen leaves.

“I thought the cut would stop the killing,” he said.

Kera wiped blood from her console with the edge of her sleeve. “Intention belongs in your testimony. Consequence belongs here.”

The pressure alarm accelerated. Old tunnel clinic thresholds had crossed into emergency range.

She placed her audit key in the limited-restart slot. Above her, Tree 44 carried no leaves. Its cracked service column held Niko's weak local signal and no trace she could identify as Omi.

Kera turned the key far enough to restore witness power, then called the Weather Spine.

“Open manual Counterweather boards,” she said. “Keep all continuity sectors read-only. We restart only what both populations need to survive the next hour.”

Chapter 21 — Airfall

Jalen Ro had both hands inside a condensation board when District Nine lost pressure.

The board shuddered against his wrists. Three mechanical needles dropped together, too fast for sensor drift, while the fourth held at a value that belonged to the hour before Full Severance. He pulled the stale needle loose with pliers and let it fall. Behind him, the Weather Spine answered the district alarm by sealing four doors and opening none of the routes the emergency table requested.

“Physical reading,” he called.

A runner at the west manifold leaned into a brass gauge. “Point eighty-two and descending.”

The model on Jalen’s board still claimed point ninety-four. Twelve hundred residents had just fallen through a disagreement between metal and code.

He drove the bypass lever down. Cold condensation spilled across the board and soaked his sleeves. The manual valve took his force, resisted, then opened a narrow line from the central reserve into District Nine. The brass gauge steadied at point eighty-one. That stopped the decline without restoring what had already gone.

Across Serein Basin, amber marks multiplied on the wall map. The orchard had once corrected pressure in movements too small for a sleeping resident to notice. After saboteurs triggered Full Severance, every adjustment had arrived as a blow. Baffles closed late. Oxygen moss received humidity intended for fungal beds. Heat escaped through routes that no longer remembered which district waited on the other side.

Jalen counted the active failures. Twenty-three pressure faults. Eleven oxygen deficits. Six condensation floods. The figures occupied their proper columns, but each column now carried clinic estimates beside it. Ivo’s handwriting had named the first consequences in blunt pencil: eight children on assisted air in the old tunnels, forty-six elders moved above Level Four, one surgery stopped with a chest still open.

“District Nine needs the east reservoir,” said Mara Vey, his night-routing lead.

“East reservoir feeds the root court.”

“The root court has its own tanks.”

“For forty-seven minutes. Kera’s channel work needs two hours.”

Mara looked toward the dark bank of orchard instruments. “Then choose.”

Jalen had built a profession around refusing that word until every alternate route had failed. Now the failure board offered no untouched capacity and no neutral delay. He marked District Nine for east reserve, then wrote forty-seven minutes beside the root court. Kera could bargain with that boundary or lose the channel.

Smoke reached the Weather Spine through a service conduit before anyone found its source. It smelled of hot resin and scorched moss. Jalen wrapped a filter across his mouth, ordered the lower crews into pressure masks, and kept the manual sequence moving. If the fire sat inside a root relay, another section of the orchard would go mute. If it sat outside, sealing the conduit could trap a maintenance team.

A voice broke through the approved branch interface in clipped bursts. “Jalen. North baffle sequence predicts inversion.”

Kera stood at the interface desk with dried blood across one cheek and fresh tape around two fingers. The glass cuts from the root court had left her stiff at the shoulder, but her hands moved precisely over the delay controls. Niko’s voice reached the room through a ring horn, every phrase separated by the damaged lattice’s irregular conduction.

“Source?” Jalen asked.

“Four surviving weather engineers in the Elseweather,” Niko said. “They can see the branch comparisons you severed from the physical controls. We cannot move your baffles.”

“No one asked you to.”

“You were about to open North Three.”

Mara’s hand had stopped over that exact lever.

Jalen crossed to the projection slate. The public weather model showed a clear path through North Three, but its pressure assumptions preceded the sabotage. “Give me the inversion.”

Kera adjusted the horn. A cluster of fine lines appeared on the slate, not as commands but as a comparative field. If North Three opened, cold upper air would drop against the damaged western seals. Pressure would rebound toward the center and pull oxygen away from three tunnel levels. The model estimated no dramatic rupture. It predicted hundreds of minor respiratory failures spread across clinics that already lacked spare masks.

Mara studied the field. “What route?”

“South Two in pulses,” Niko replied. “Nine seconds open. Twenty-two closed. Repeat seven times, then ask us again.”

“We don’t route basin pressure by asking after each breath,” she said.

“Today you do.”

Jalen heard fear under Niko’s patience. Not fear of the room, which Niko could not see except through Kera’s descriptions, but of interruption. Ninety-one verified branch-persons had vanished when the channel collapsed. Every surviving enclave now depended on fragments of root lattice that still accepted current. A pressure correction could demand computation from the same damaged field holding a person’s next remembered moment.

He put Mara on South Two and assigned two runners to count valve seconds aloud. Kera relayed the first branch comparison. Jalen checked it against brass gauges and clinic returns. Neither source received authority alone. The Elseweather could model possibilities but could not touch steel. His crews could move steel but no longer possessed enough working comparisons to anticipate each consequence.

The first pulse raised central pressure and lowered humidity in the north moss galleries. On the third, District Nine recovered point zero one. On the fifth, a clinic alarm cleared from Ivo’s board. After the seventh, South Two jammed half open.

Mara threw her shoulder into the lever. It did not move.

“Cut the pneumatic line,” Jalen said.

“That leaves it open.”

“Then we close it at the throat.”

He took a mask, a hand wheel, and the shortest route into the lower manifold. Smoke thickened below the second landing. A resin seal had burned along thirty meters of conduit, ignited by current reversal when Full Severance forced live orchard load into an obsolete ground. Two technicians crouched beside the source with suppressant foam, their face shields filmed white.

Jalen crawled past them. The throat valve lay behind a heat shield that had warped against its frame. He set the hand wheel on the emergency stem and pulled. The stem turned one notch. His lungs tightened despite the filter. Another notch required both knees against the floor and his full weight hanging from the wheel.

Through his headset, Kera counted pressure loss. “Point seventy-nine. Point seventy-eight.”

“District?”

“Central average.”

Average concealed the descent. Old tunnels would already sit lower. Elite domes would sit higher because private thermal reserves kept their air moving. Jalen pulled again. The valve crossed halfway, and the wheel tore skin from his left palm.

Niko’s voice arrived through static. “Stop at sixty percent.”

“Reason.”

“Full closure sends the rebound into West Four.”

“West Four can absorb it.”

“Your model says that. Its seal map predates last winter.”

Jalen remembered the maintenance deferral. West Four’s replacement membrane had gone to a new dome after the governor’s office classified the cooperative repair as seasonal. He had signed the route change because both sites remained inside code. Code had not anticipated sabotage, fungal loss, and a manual cycle performed through smoke.

He stopped the valve short of closure. South Two’s pulse settled into a narrow continuous bleed. Pressure held long enough for him to crawl back through the conduit.

By the time Jalen reached the board, Ivo had arrived with two portable oxygen frames and an expression that dismissed greetings as waste. He listened to Jalen’s chest, counted the roughness after one breath, and tightened the filter strap.

“You have smoke in you,” Ivo said.

“I also have thirty-one active faults.”

“Then keep the mask on while you mismanage them.”

Ivo moved to the clinic returns. He had written names beside the most fragile groups, not because every name could fit but because no district line would remain clean once a person occupied it. Jalen saw Lessa’s old clinic designation in the margin of a routing note. Ivo had not written her name. He did not need to.

Kera called for quiet. A fresh field gathered on the branch interface, slower than the first. Several lines ended before reaching the slate’s edge.

“We can run the manual counterweather cycle,” Niko said. “Not the test sequence. A basin cycle.”

Jalen removed his mask long enough to speak clearly. “At seventy-three percent design capacity, with intact refuge rotation and working root relays. We have neither.”

“You have physical crews. We have the comparison trees that survived.”

“You have survivors occupying those trees.”

“That does not make the pressure less real.”

Mara brought up the Counterweather board they had built during the public test. Its routes had been designed for orderly transfer, scheduled dormancy, and industrial rationing announced in advance. Tonight the western districts had no preparation. The orchard’s automatic safeguards had fractured. One wrong load could erase another enclave or send a clinic below breathable pressure.

Jalen drew boundaries around what remained. Central oxygen storage could support manual routing for nine hours. Condensation reserve allowed twelve if no greenhouse demanded recovery. Pressure workers could hold perhaps six before fatigue made their timing unreliable. Branch comparisons offered the missing adaptation layer, but their compute sat inside the same endangered substrate.

“We need a stable horizon,” Oris Nume said from the upper doorway.

Her navy civic coat had lost one fastening, and terracotta seal wax marked her cuff. She carried no authority over valves, yet everyone in the room turned because she had given the need a legal shape. “Not survival by another hour. How long can this cycle buy?”

Jalen looked at the board. “If it works, six days. If it fails, the decline accelerates before dawn.”
