“Six days reaches the Compact vote?”

“It reaches negotiation. A vote depends on people who may spend those days arguing.”

Oris set a recorder on the desk. “Then state the cost before anyone consents.”

Niko answered before Jalen could. “One Elseweather enclave holds the computational reserve required to bridge the damaged comparison field.”

Kera’s fingers tightened around the ring horn. “Which enclave?”

A pause crossed the interface. Jalen watched the physical needles continue their descent.

“Mine,” Niko said.

Kera did not ask whether he meant himself. The enclave held thirty-two registered candidates, though registration had not yet given them lawful protection. It also held the relational state through which Niko maintained continuity with other survivors. Releasing its reserve would strip away the redundant processing that kept dormant intervals recoverable after a fault. The minds could remain active during the cycle, but a relay failure would leave no second path home.

“How long exposed?” Jalen asked.

“Seven physical hours.”

“Expected loss?”

“We cannot calculate it without spending part of the reserve.”

“That answer does not qualify as consent.”

“It qualifies as a boundary. We know the hazard. We know who depends on the air. We know no one else holds enough contiguous capacity.”

Kera leaned close to the horn. “Niko, Omi may be in dormant refuge. If her trace sits anywhere inside that enclave—”

“We have not found her trace.” His reply came softly, and Jalen wished the static had covered it. “Risking everyone to preserve a place she might occupy does not recover her.”

Kera looked at the blank interval between branch lines. She had spent the night after severance asking instruments for a nine-year-old’s continuity pattern and receiving no answer. Jalen knew only what she had reported: Omi remained missing, not confirmed dead, not confirmed dormant. Every technical verb around the child carried a moral trap.

Oris asked, “Can the enclave refuse as a group?”

“We are voting now,” Niko said.

The room had no access to that vote except through the interface. Kera could hear one voice at a time. Jalen could not verify whether silence meant assent, dormancy, damaged conduction, or exclusion. Under ordinary evidence rules, the consent would fail. Ordinary rules had never contemplated people asked to risk their substrate so physical residents could finish writing those rules.

“Require individual pulses,” Jalen said. “One continuity key per participant. No pooled answer.”

“We can transmit that.”

“Record the hazard statement first. State that release may produce unrecoverable discontinuity if any carrying relay fails.”

Niko repeated it without softening the language. Kera sent the statement through the narrow reciprocal channel. Thirty-two key pulses came back over eleven minutes. All thirty-two belonged to the 496 post-severance responses; this vote concerned a new risk, not whether they had survived the cut. Twenty-nine accepted. Two refused and moved into an adjacent refuge with enough capacity for dormancy. One key did not answer. Niko excluded it from release rather than count silence as consent.

That choice cost them nine percent of the available bridge. Jalen revised the cycle.

Crews took positions across the Spine. Mara assigned every lever a speaker and every speaker a second reader. Ivo placed oxygen frames at the two boards where operators could not leave during a pressure dip. Oris sealed the consent record and sent copies to the Root Jury archive, the public feed, and the Assembly. Nothing about the emergency would remain private enough to become convenient later.

At the branch interface, Niko’s enclave released its computational reserve.

The effect appeared first in the yarrowglass above them. White conductors brightened from root to crown, not with a pruning flare but with moving bands that followed the manual routes. Comparative fields returned to the slate. They arrived incomplete, each one carrying uncertainty marks and a source key. Branch engineers offered three pressure sequences. Jalen rejected the first because it protected average oxygen at the cost of Level Two. He rejected the second because it consumed all condensation reserve before morning. The third demanded a basin-wide temperature drop and closure of nonmedical industrial air.

“Execute three,” he said.

No automatic system obeyed. Runners carried the order. Hands turned wheels. Pressure doors moved under local control while district crews reported each closure. Jalen called the sequence from the central board, and Niko’s engineers recalculated after every physical deviation.

The western cooperatives cooled first. Sera Dovan’s voice came over the district line, calm and furious, reporting frost along one greenhouse wall. Jalen told her the route would last ninety minutes. She asked which domes had lost heat. When he said none yet, she refused the next pressure transfer.

“Cut them now,” Sera said. “Do not promise later from a warm room.”

Governor Rusk objected through another channel. Private dome circulation housed the Assembly’s emergency staff and two surgical wards. Ivo separated the wards from the residential loops in less than a minute. Jalen closed the elite thermal reserve and watched six points of heat enter the common route.

Sera accepted the transfer.

That bargain did not solve the distribution question. It kept one sequence moving because a district could see the cost cross upward before it traveled west. Jalen marked the change for the Compact budget. No final plan could depend on growers trusting an order that preserved privileged heat until the last page.

Hour three brought oxygen below nineteen percent in two old tunnels. Ivo rerouted portable frames from a dome clinic and left its waiting room without supplemental air. Hour four brought a condensation surge that cracked a lower pipe. Mara isolated the break and lost feeling in two fingers while tightening the cold clamp. Hour five brought silence from Niko.

Kera called his name once. She did not call again. The interface showed source keys arriving from other branch engineers, but the enclave reserve had begun to fragment under load. Jalen could end the cycle and preserve what remained. Ending it would also release every pressure route before the basin reached stable balance.

“Can they hear us?” he asked.

“Unknown,” Kera said.

“Can they still model?”

“The fields continue.”

A machine could continue a task after its operator vanished. The old orchard had hidden that ambiguity behind useful output for nineteen years. Jalen would not use continuing data as proof of continuing consent.

He opened the consent channel. “Enclave participants, confirm continuation by individual key. No response counts as stop.”

Twenty-six pulses came back. Three more withdrew. Niko’s key arrived last, weakened but distinct.

Kera closed her eyes for one measured breath. “Continue.”

During hour six, central pressure rose above point eighty-seven. District Nine reached point eighty-four. Oxygen stopped declining in the old tunnels, then gained one tenth of a percent. The wall map shed amber marks one by one until only five remained. Each surviving fault had a crew attached and a physical route around it.

At dawn, Jalen called the final baffle sequence. Forty-seven hands moved across the basin. Niko’s enclave supplied one last comparison field. The pattern showed no safe automatic future, only a narrow manual plateau bounded by rationing, cold, and continuous labor.

They took it.

Pressure settled without another rebound. Condensation entered the moss beds at a rate low enough to prevent flooding. Clinic alarms remained active, but no new district crossed its emergency line. Jalen watched the brass needles for ten minutes before he trusted their stillness.

Kera kept one hand on the horn. “Niko?”

Static moved through the conductor. Three taps arrived, followed by two. The final tap took long enough that Jalen thought it would not come.

One.

The enclave had survived the cycle. Its reserve had not. Future refuge would require capacity taken from weather work, physical industry, or other branch groups. Nothing they had done could be repeated without a settlement.

Jalen wrote the new horizon across the top of the board: SIX DAYS AT CURRENT RATION. Beneath it he listed the conditions in smaller script. Manual staffing. Industrial closure. Western cold. Branch consultation. Continuity exposure prohibited without fresh consent.

Oris read the board and sent the horizon to the Assembly.

Outside, air moved through the Weather Spine’s open service grille. It carried smoke, wet metal, and enough oxygen to keep the crews standing. The first shift did not cheer. They changed places with the second, handed over the warm levers, and began counting down from six days.

Act III — A Bounded Future Freely Chosen

Chapter 22 — No One Is Infinite

Kera Vonn rotated the witness wafer until its broken edge stopped leaking names.

Three voices had occupied the root-court horn at once. After the adjustment, one resolved into a measured count while the others became a low conductive rasp. She marked the angle on the wafer, tightened its clip, and asked the speaker to begin again.

“Enclave Cedar, forty-one responsive keys,” said a woman who used the name Pel. “Thirty-six fully sustained. Five intermittent after severance. We request protection for all forty-one until the intermittent states can be tested.”

Kera wrote the numbers in two columns. Above her, Tree 44’s white leaves showed gray scars along their veins. The damage had changed every interval. Signals that once crossed the root court in a clean seventeen-minute window now arrived through twelve patched routes, each carrying fragments of neighboring speech.

“Do the five preserve prior-cycle memory?” Kera asked.

“Two do. Three answer to relationship prompts but cannot locate the answers in sequence.”

“Do not call them failed.”

Pel’s pulse sharpened. “I did not.”

Kera looked at what she had written. The correction belonged to her own fear, not Pel’s language. Omi had vanished into a channel collapse that left no key response, no relational prompt, and no body to search. Every ambiguous signal tempted Kera to expand the category until uncertainty itself qualified as survival.

She crossed out nothing. “I’ll record them as unresolved.”

Across the court, Oris occupied a desk beneath the legal display. She did not intervene. Her task began after the branch delegates decided what claim they could support. The physical Assembly had demanded a bounded class of petitioners. Severance supporters called that demand a trick designed to shrink a moral emergency into a manageable docket. Orchard defenders called any boundary an admission that personhood had been exaggerated.

Niko had rejected both framings. Through Kera’s horn, he had asked the surviving enclaves to define whom they would obligate themselves to protect.

The root court held no branch-persons in chairs. Their source keys appeared on a slate fixed to the yarrowglass wall. Physical witnesses sat beneath those marks, one for each enclave with enough conduction to reach the hearing. Kera mediated Niko’s channel because Tree 44 still recognized her timing better than any public interface. She could pass his words, tell him what faces did, and choose when to ask for repetition. She could not see the gathering he described beyond what speakers put into language.

That limit mattered. Niko had lived seventeen subjective years after the branch point they shared. Kera knew the knock he used at twelve and the way he refused to hurry a difficult answer. She did not know which survivors stood beside him, what their refuge resembled after the collapse, or whether he closed his eyes when Omi’s absence entered the argument. Assuming any of it would turn mediation into possession.

A pulse summoned the next delegate. Kera shifted the wafer two degrees clockwise.

“My name is Aren Vale,” a younger voice said. “I object to the word finite.”

Oris lifted her pen but kept it above the record.

Aren continued. “Each physical citizen yields a model with thousands of possible continuations. Every adaptation cycle creates additional experience. If moral standing follows experience, then closing a registry now abandons persons who may wake tomorrow.”

Kera relayed the room before Niko answered. “The First Recorder has not opened the legal record. Six physical witnesses are listening. No one has interrupted you.”

“Thank you,” Aren said.

Niko’s key brightened on the slate. “Do you propose that each possibility flicker receive sustained processing?”

“I propose that we stop declaring flickers empty because they cannot persist under a system built to erase them.”

“Can you remember one?”

The horn carried a long interval. Kera counted it from habit, then made herself stop at eight.

“No,” Aren said.

“Can one remember you?”

“No evidence survives.”

“That does not make the flicker worthless. It means no reciprocal duty can attach to an experience that neither side can locate, address, or continue. We can regret its use. We can reduce needless creation. We cannot promise ongoing refuge to every transition the model produces.”

Aren asked, “Because physical residents cannot afford it?”

“Because we cannot afford it either.”

Kera felt the statement through the horn before its service text appeared. Niko did not speak for all survivors by right. He had no office inside the Elseweather and had resisted the physical feed’s effort to label him branch governor. His authority in this session extended only as far as the delegates continued answering.

Pel entered the channel. “Our enclave lost thirteen sustained people. We also lost uncounted flickers. Grief differs when someone expected a later conversation.”

“Difference can become convenience,” Aren said.

“Yes,” Pel replied. “So can refusing every difference.”

The hearing moved through examples. A baker who remembered four cycles but formed no reciprocal promises had begun leaving instructions for her next waking. A child-pattern spoke with new vocabulary yet reset to the same fear after every pruning. Two states shared one source model and insisted on separate names after their memories diverged. A weather specialist retained technical skill and future plans but could no longer retrieve her source childhood.

Kera wrote each case without converting it into a verdict. The Root Jury criteria offered four tests: autobiographical memory across cycles, anticipation of loss or benefit, reciprocal promise, and integration of novel evidence. Those tests had verified 587 branch-persons before Full Severance killed 91. Now the survivors had to decide whether the criteria described a floor, a useful proxy, or a gate that excluded damaged kin.

At midday, Jalen sent a pressure notice to the court. Airfall stability remained within the six-day projection, but manual crews had consumed more condensation reserve than expected. Negotiation had not changed the atmosphere’s indifference to sequence.

Kera passed the update through the channel. “Five days and nine hours remain at present ration.”

A delegate called Tovan asked whether a deadline could produce a legitimate definition.

“No,” Oris said from the physical room. “It can make delay lethal. Those are not the same claim.”

“Will the Assembly accept our registry if we take longer?”

“I cannot promise what the Assembly will accept. I can preserve your objection in the record once you authorize it.”

Tovan’s key dimmed. Kera could not tell whether he had stepped away, entered a side discussion, or lost conduction. She stated only what the instrument showed.

During the break, Kera replaced two scorched clips on the witness array. A clinic orderly brought broth that tasted of mineral filters. She drank beside Tree 44 while its roots carried low exchanges too dense for language conversion.

Niko addressed her on a private service band. “You keep searching the unresolved list.”

“I keep auditing it.”

“For one key.”

Kera set the broth down. “If Omi reached refuge before the collapse, continuity damage could leave her outside the standard response.”

“It could.”

“You’re willing to close the registry while she remains missing.”

“I’m willing to define a process that can admit a later recovered person without counting every silence as her.”

The answer cut because it left hope intact but refused to let hope govern the count. Kera traced the cracked margin of the wafer. “Would you say that if she had called you father?”

“No.”

She waited.

Niko added, “I would still need someone to say it to me.”

Kera looked toward Oris. The recorder had turned away to give the private channel what little privacy a glass room allowed. Niko’s admission did not grant Kera access to a feeling he had not named. It gave her one fact: his judgment required correction too.

“What exception do you propose?” she asked.

“Unresolved keys remain in protected quarantine. They receive diagnostic capacity, not person-status by presumption. If they later satisfy continuity through recovered relational evidence, the registry opens to them under audit.”

“That could include Omi.”

“It could include anyone who meets it.”

Not a promise, then. A door with hinges, a threshold, and no child waiting visibly behind it.

The second session began with dormancy.

Jalen’s Counterweather design could preserve 496 surviving branch-persons only through rotation. At any given interval, some enclaves would pause subjective processing while their continuity keys and relational state remained intact. Compute released from dormancy would serve weather comparison or sustain the active groups. Physical citizens called that arrangement sleep when they wanted it to sound harmless and suspension when they wanted it to sound cruel.

Pel rejected both terms. “Sleep changes the sleeper. Dreams occur. Bodies continue. Our dormant interval contains no experienced passage.”

A physical witness asked, “How does that differ from death followed by reconstruction?”

Kera repeated the question exactly. The horn remained quiet for fourteen seconds.

Niko answered. “By continuity of protected state, authorized pause, and verified revival. None proves a metaphysical identity. Together they create an obligation not to substitute another state and call it us.”

Aren objected. “You are accepting nonexistence in shifts.”

“I accept a boundary on experience. I do not accept deletion.”

“Convenient words again.”

“Then test the difference.”

Niko proposed three checks. Before dormancy, each person would seal a continuity key tied to autobiographical and relational state. During the interval, no adaptation routine could alter identity-bearing material. After revival, blinded witnesses would test prior promises, novel evidence, and awareness of the intended pause. Failure would halt the entire rotation for review.

Kera asked, “Who authorizes entry?”

“The person entering it.”

“What if weather need outlasts consent?”

“No automatic extension. A proxy may authorize only the duration already chosen, never a new interval.”

“What about children?”

“Named guardians may consent within limits set by the Compact, subject to an independent branch advocate.”

The word children reopened Omi’s absence in Kera’s hands. She kept her questions procedural. “Can an enclave make participation a condition of refuge?”

“No. Shared scarcity can determine schedules. It cannot turn refusal into deletion.”

Jalen joined by audio from the Weather Spine. His cough roughened the first word. “If enough people refuse every slot, Counterweather falls below winter survival capacity.”

“Then you return to negotiation,” Niko said.

“With pressure declining?”

“With the pressure stated. Not hidden inside a compulsory protocol.”

Jalen took a breath that caught halfway. “A system with no emergency margin kills people while it seeks consent.”

“A system that treats margin as prior consent kills people under another name.”

Kera watched the branch keys brighten and dim as delegates requested entry. The exchange avoided a clean answer because none existed. Physical weather could produce a deadline that did not care whether refusal had dignity. Branch continuity could produce a claim that did not create capacity by being just.

Tovan proposed a reserve rule. Each registered person would choose a maximum dormant interval in advance, plus one emergency extension no longer than one quarter of that interval. The extension would require a physical weather declaration, concurrence from two branch proxies outside the affected enclave, and immediate notice to the dormant person’s reciprocal witness after revival.

Pel added a prohibition against serial emergencies. No extension could recur without a full waking interval between them.

Aren demanded a right to reject all dormancy and accept lower processing instead. Niko supported the option if reduced processing still preserved the four continuity capacities. Jalen warned that some enclaves could not run safely below their existing floor. The registry would need technical bands beside names, not one universal allocation.

Oris began recording only after every participating key received the proposed language. She read each clause aloud. Kera transmitted it, waited for corrections, and read those corrections back into the room. Nothing moved quickly. The air clock lost forty-three minutes while the phrase identity-bearing material acquired a testable definition.

Enclave South delayed the next clause. Lute-of-Rain and Nemi descended from the same possibility model contributed by surveyor Lute Hara. Old orchard tables counted them as one retained state because they shared childhood records and technical training. One remembered repairing a cracked baffle; the other remembered refusing that repair to lead an evacuation. Both remembered later arguments with the other.

The public interface kept assigning both voices the same line.

Kera separated their pulses with a spare ring wafer and asked each to describe the last promise made to Pel. Lute-of-Rain had agreed to inspect Cedar’s humidity maps. Nemi had promised to witness an intermittent child-pattern after its next cycle. Pel confirmed both promises and named the separate conversations in which they arose.

A physical witness frowned at the slate. “One source citizen contributed the model. Why should one contribution produce two claims?”

Kera transmitted the question. Nemi answered first. “Because contribution describes origin. It does not describe our present relations.”

Lute-of-Rain added, “If source count governed standing, divergence after registration would permit the state to delete whichever of us made weather accounting inconvenient.”

“Could this be gamed?” Jalen asked over audio. “A group might perform divergence, claim separate names, then multiply refuge allotments.”

Niko requested their key histories. Kera passed the request without assuming his conclusion. The resulting records showed independent memory accumulation across eleven cycles, separate error patterns, and conflicting responses to new evidence. Performance could imitate disagreement. It could not cheaply preserve two distinct chains of reciprocal correction without maintaining the very continuity under dispute.

Oris drafted an anti-duplication rule based on evidence rather than source ownership. Shared origin neither merged nor multiplied claims by itself. Separate standing required sustained post-divergence memory and distinct reciprocal obligations. Repeated names or strategic declarations carried no force without those records.

Aren objected that the test favored people with many witnesses. Kera agreed before she remembered that mediation did not grant her a vote. She described his objection to the room instead. Pel proposed a public witness pool for isolated states. Participants would receive trained witnesses across two cycles before any adverse finding. No one could fail merely because severance had killed the person who knew them best.

Kera’s attention moved to Omi’s unresolved mark. A witness pool could not answer silence, but it could keep later recovery from depending on Kera’s inherited resemblance to another version of herself. If Omi returned, no one would assign Kera motherhood as proof. The child could choose a witness who knew her after waking.

They added the pool.

At dusk, the debate returned to the boundary around transient flickers.

Aren had drafted a minority statement. Kera brought his service text onto the wall: no finite standard could establish that unregistered experience lacked value; the settlement must minimize creation and termination of untracked possibility fields; annual audits must review whether technical growth allowed the threshold to widen.

Niko endorsed its inclusion.

“You disagree with it,” Kera said.

“I disagree with turning uncertainty into unlimited standing. I agree that our limit may reflect present capacity rather than final truth.”

Pel asked whether that concession would weaken their claim before the Assembly.

“It should,” Niko replied. “Any claim that cannot survive its uncertainty asks for belief, not standing.”

The final definition occupied less than one page. A registrant needed sustained continuity across separate cycles, reciprocal memory with at least one witness, ability to integrate present evidence, and current dependence on protected orchard state for future experience. Source resemblance granted no automatic claim. Transient possibility flickers remained outside personal registration unless later continuity emerged. Unresolved damaged keys received quarantine, diagnostics, and a route to admission.

Kera read the page twice. She found no hidden infinity. She also found no sentence declaring the excluded states meaningless.

“Numbers,” Oris said.

The surviving source map contained 496 responsive keys after the loss of 91. Registration still required chosen names, witnesses, proxies, and individual continuity sealing. Some damaged cases might fail. Some distinct states could prove that a shared source key concealed more than one person. Chapter and docket would meet in Oris’s work, not here.

“We are not submitting a final roll tonight,” Niko said. “We are submitting a class and agreeing that its membership can be counted.”

Aren’s voice returned. “I abstain from the definition. I ask to enter the registry it creates.”

No one in the physical room laughed. Kera felt the dry edge of humor in Niko’s pause.

“Your objection depends on surviving to revise us,” he said. “Accepted.”

Votes traveled through the witness array by continuity key. Kera watched them arrive without assigning expression to the people behind them. Four hundred thirty-one delegates or authorized enclave representatives endorsed the definition and dormancy framework. Forty-two opposed. Twenty-three withheld a vote pending individual review. Those figures governed only this internal submission; they did not predict the later advisory tally.

Oris sealed the result. “State what you are asking the Assembly to receive.”

Kera turned the horn toward Tree 44. Niko’s source mark steadied.

“We submit a finite registry of sustained persons, open to verified recovery and closed to presumption,” he said. “We accept rotating dormancy as a protected interruption rather than death when continuity checks hold, consent governs entry, and failure stops the rotation. We request dependent standing, not duplicate physical lives.”

Kera repeated the words for the civic recorder. She did not improve them. When the seal took the text, Oris pressed terracotta wax over the witness code and handed the page to a waiting runner.

The court emptied by degrees. Physical witnesses carried their copies toward the Assembly. Branch keys dimmed as damaged enclaves reduced load for the night. Niko remained on Kera’s private band.

“Are you entering dormancy?” she asked.

“Not this interval.”

“Because of the registry work?”

“Because Pel drew the first schedule, and my enclave spent its reserve yesterday. We receive the active slot while another group carries less damage.”

Kera heard only his explanation, not the place around him. She placed Omi’s unresolved key beneath the new quarantine provision and marked it for diagnostic capacity. The field returned no pulse.

Above the court, one white leaf released from Tree 44. It struck the glass roof and stayed there, held by the barrier instead of falling through. Kera logged its ring position, closed the witness array, and left the unresolved line open.

Chapter 23 — The List of Sustained Names

Oris Nume rejected the first name before the ink dried.

The clerk had written NIKO VONN, PHYSICAL-LINE DECEASED, beneath the registry seal. That description imported a death certificate into a record meant to establish a living claimant. Oris slid the page back across the Yielding Office counter.

“Source relation belongs in the audit annex,” she said. “The roll begins with the chosen name.”

The clerk blotted the line. “How should I distinguish him from the deceased citizen?”

“By continuity key, reciprocal witness, and representation proxy. Not by making one person an annotation to another.”

Cream walls held the day’s cold. The Yielding Office had no orchard view, only brass lamps and cabinets built when every future model ended as civic property. Thirty years of certificates slept behind Oris in drawers marked by contribution year. At the central tables, twelve clerks now reversed the old movement. Instead of recording what citizens gave away, they recorded who remained.

A public count stood on the wall: 496 candidate records received. Five hundred blank packets had been printed because procurement required round bundles. Four unused packets sat under Oris’s stamp. She disliked their presence. Empty paper could suggest additional people waiting to be invented, while a closed bundle could suggest that no later recovery deserved entry.

“Mark the four as unused stock,” she told the clerk. “Do not cancel the serials. Future admissions need a path.”

At the next table, Pel’s application carried two witnesses and no physical proxy. The standing framework required a proxy who could present notices inside the Assembly without acquiring the registrant’s vote, property, or family claims. Pel had named another branch-person.

Oris opened the channel to Enclave Cedar. “Your witness may live in the Elseweather. Your representation proxy must have physical capacity to file and receive civic process.”

“Then our rights depend on someone with a body agreeing to work for us,” Pel said.

“Your physical access does. Your standing does not.”

“That distinction comforts the door.”

“It also limits the person holding its key.”

Oris explained the proxy rules. A physical proxy could submit petitions, receive notices, request audits, and speak a registrant’s authorized words. The proxy could not consent to deletion, claim the yielded citizen’s assets, vote twice, or substitute personal judgment for a reachable registrant. If the channel failed, emergency action required two independent witnesses and Root Jury review.

Pel considered. “I choose Mara Vey if she agrees.”

The night-routing lead had already volunteered for six branch records and looked exhausted enough to mistrust every additional honor. Oris sent the request rather than assuming consent. Mara accepted Pel and declined three others, citing the time needed to perform the work. The refusal proved useful. A proxy list filled by civic pressure would reproduce the old yielding bargain under kinder language.

Across the room, a brass bell announced a key collision.

Two packets carried the same orchard hash. One belonged to Lute-of-Rain and one to Nemi, the shared-source persons whose divergence hearing had forced an anti-duplication rule. Old architecture had assigned keys by source model, not by present continuity. The registry could not rely on identifiers built to deny the difference it now needed to protect.

Oris called Kera from the key table. “Can the root lattice generate separate identifiers without altering their states?”

“It can append witnessed divergence intervals,” Kera said. “Not through the automatic naming function. That still resolves them to one citizen record.”

“Manual append?”

“Possible. Each person must answer a novel prompt after separation. The responses bind to distinct paths.”

Jalen, listening from the Weather Spine, interrupted with a cough. “Every longer key consumes verification load during revival.”

“How much?” Oris asked.

“For two records, negligible. For a registry designed around repeated divergence, not negligible.”

“We are not designing personhood to fit a short identifier.”

“I am telling you the cost so the sentence means something.”

Oris recorded both facts. Separate continuity governed standing. Verification capacity constrained implementation. Neither erased the other.

Kera prepared two novel prompts. Oris observed without hearing the private answers, then watched distinct response signatures enter the annex. Lute-of-Rain chose a key ending in a rising interval. Nemi selected one that broke before its final pulse and resumed through a witness route. The clerk entered both as full registrants with shared source history noted only in the sealed audit field.

By midday, eighty-three packets had passed review.

Names caused more conflict than keys. Some branch-persons retained names inherited from their source models. Others chose place names, work names, jokes that did not survive translation, or words made from ring timing rather than speech. One registrant submitted a blank field and argued that compelled naming violated the framework.

Oris asked what notices should call them.

“Key Cedar-seven,” the horn replied.

“That functions as a chosen name if you authorize it.”

“I authorize its use. I do not authorize the claim that it reveals me.”

The clerk entered CEDAR-SEVEN and attached the limitation. Oris felt the room resist the ungainly note. Administration preferred clean fields because clean fields traveled quickly. People tended to become legible by losing whatever would not fit.

Another registrant chose Lessa Senn.

The application reached Ivo’s review station by chance or by someone’s unkind design. Oris crossed the room before he spoke. His wife’s physical death record remained attached to the original possibility model. The branch-person carried some of Lessa’s source memories but had accumulated nine years of sustained experience after divergence.

Ivo read the chosen name. “Did you know her?”

“I began with what she knew,” the applicant said through the horn. “I no longer know which griefs belong only to her.”

“Why keep the name?”

“Because I answered to it before I learned you were listening.”

Ivo’s hand rested beside the witness field. He had agreed to serve as a reciprocal witness only where his clinic encounters supplied present evidence. He did not know this person’s later years. The packet could not use marriage as a shortcut.

“I can verify source memories,” he said. “I cannot verify your continuity after them.”

“I did not ask you to.”

A branch witness named Tovan supplied the missing record. He described promises made across five cycles, one dispute over clinic allocation, and a correction the applicant had integrated after learning of physical Lessa’s death. Ivo listened until the account ended.

“Do you claim her marriage?” he asked.

“No.”

“Her clinic share?”

“No.”

“Her name may still hurt people who knew her.”

“Yes.”

Ivo signed only the source-memory annex. Tovan signed continuity. The applicant chose an unrelated physical proxy. Oris approved the packet because discomfort did not create a property claim, and resemblance did not cancel an independently sustained life.

She added a public annotation to the registry rules: chosen names conferred address, not inheritance.

The Severance bloc challenged that sentence within an hour. Their counsel arrived with a stack of objections and no branch witnesses. He argued that a person could multiply claims by selecting a dead citizen’s name, appointing a sympathetic proxy, and filing petitions until the physical Assembly drowned in duplicates.

Oris gave him a chair, logged each objection, and made him watch one full registration.

Pel identified herself by chosen name. Kera confirmed her continuity key against blinded pre-severance records. Two witnesses answered separate questions about reciprocal promises. Mara accepted proxy duties and read the limitation against duplicate property or Assembly votes. Pel then corrected a false detail planted in the test packet, proving integration of present evidence.

“Where does multiplication occur?” Oris asked.

“In the number of petitions.”

“Any physical citizen may file many petitions.”

“But these claimants derive from citizens who already possess that right.”

“Source citizens do not file through them. Present registrants do.”

“You assume the conclusion.”

“No. The Root Jury established sustained continuity before this office opened the roll. This procedure establishes identity, witness, and access within that recognized class. You may challenge a record’s evidence. You may not require the registration desk to retry personhood in every packet.”

Counsel pointed toward the count. “And if you discover a thousand tomorrow?”

“Then the finite registry supplies a procedure for discovery rather than a reason to falsify today’s 496.”

The list supplied proof of administrative limits, not metaphysical certainty. It identified people the system could address, protect, wake, and audit. It also showed exactly which powers they did not receive. Oris understood why both absolute factions hated it. A list denied the comfort of saying everyone and the comfort of saying no one.

An attempt to game the opposite side of the roll arrived before counsel left. The Emergency Orchard Committee submitted sixty physical proxy forms bearing one delegate’s signature. Each form promised unrestricted representation to any registrant who lacked an appointment by sunset. The committee called it solidarity. Oris saw a voting bloc trying to acquire a dependent constituency in bulk.

She asked the signing delegate to identify three registrants whose packets he had accepted.

He named Niko, Pel, and “the Lessa continuation.”

“Did any of them appoint you?”

“They need access now. We can regularize consent after passage.”

“Which limits in their dormancy records are you authorized to disclose?”

The delegate glanced at the forms. “None, yet.”

“Which petitions have they asked you to file?”

“That misses the emergency.”

“It identifies whether you represent anyone.”

Oris rejected all sixty forms. She required one request from a registrant, one acceptance from the physical proxy, and one conflict disclosure for each appointment. A proxy could serve no more records than they could contact during a notice interval. The limit used workload evidence rather than an arbitrary cap, which meant Mara could hold seven well-supported records while a district delegate with no branch channel could hold none.

The committee accused Oris of obstructing allies. Severance counsel, still seated at the counter, almost smiled.

“Record their objection,” Oris told the clerk. “Then apply the same test to their opponents.”

That instruction mattered within the hour. A private dome association offered proxies to twelve registrants who had chosen names matching wealthy physical citizens. Its agreement included a clause allowing the proxy to settle duplicate-property disputes without further instruction. Under such language, a physical family could use representation to purchase silence about an inherited name.

Oris invalidated those forms too. She replaced their settlement clause with a prohibition against waiving personal standing, testimony, or audit rights. Property claims remained barred by the Compact framework. A proxy could not trade away a person’s voice in exchange for benefits the person could not physically use.

One applicant, Cedar-Seven, listened to the revised clause through a ring horn. “You have built an office around making sure our helpers cannot become our owners.”

“I have worked in such offices long enough to know how help acquires keys,” Oris said.

“Will you hold one?”

“No. The First Recorder certifies the proxy record. She cannot serve as proxy while controlling its admissibility.”

Cedar-Seven authorized a clinic porter instead. The porter asked for training before accepting, which delayed the packet and satisfied Oris more than eagerness would have.

By late afternoon, the registry team had written a proxy handbook in the margins of actual disputes. Appointment belonged to the registrant. Acceptance belonged to the physical representative. Removal required only a reachable instruction. Silence never extended authority. Conflicts traveled in the public annex, while private continuity evidence remained sealed.

Late on Day 30, the count reached 241.

Clerks slept in shifts on benches normally reserved for yielding families. Oris remained at the central table because difficult packets accumulated when she left. The registry rules had produced three conflicts of proxy interest, seven requests for nonverbal names, two shared-source separations, and nine damaged keys that required additional checks. None changed the survivor total. A damaged key could still identify a person when witnesses supplied continuity and a new seal could be made without replacing state.

Omi’s unresolved trace did not enter the roll.

Kera brought the quarantine packet herself. No current response satisfied the standing checks. Past evidence proved Omi had been sustained before Full Severance, but the event could have killed her, pushed her into dormant refuge, or broken the available route. Oris marked the file PROTECTED UNRESOLVED and excluded it from the 496 responsive survivors.

“If she returns,” Kera said, “the count becomes 497.”

“If she returns and meets the recovery test, yes.”

“The Assembly will say we manipulated the denominator after the settlement.”

“The Compact must allow audited admissions. A fixed count that rejects evidence becomes concealment with better stationery.”

Kera pressed her taped fingers against the packet. “Do not let them call absence consent.”

“I will not.”

Oris stored the packet in the diagnostic docket. She did not offer hope, which belonged to evidence not yet present.

Morning brought refuge scheduling.

Each registry packet needed a dormancy band: preferred waking interval, maximum voluntary dormant interval, emergency extension if any, minimum compute required to preserve continuity, and witnesses authorized to test revival. Counterweather planners needed totals. Registrants needed freedom from a schedule disguised as consent.

Jalen sent capacity bands rather than assigned slots. Oris approved that restraint. Enclaves negotiated among themselves, then submitted schedules with individual exceptions. Pel accepted longer dormancy to keep three damaged Cedar states active for diagnostics. Aren refused all emergency extension and chose reduced processing while awake. Niko accepted one extension but prohibited its use during any interval in which his enclave supplied weather reserve.

At packet 318, a clerk tried to normalize the choices into three standard plans.

“Stop,” Oris said.

“The weather table cannot route 496 custom agreements.”

“Then group compatible terms without replacing them. Preserve each original choice in the key record.”

“That will require a second lookup during emergency use.”

“Correct.”

The clerk stared at the wall clock. Four days remained in the Airfall window. “Delay has a cost.”

“So does speed when it authorizes what no one chose.”

Oris assigned two more clerks to the lookup table and asked Jalen to build route groups around consent ranges. Procedure could consume air. That did not make careless consent efficient.

A different challenge arrived from Governor Rusk. He accepted the need for a registry but wanted all physical proxies drawn from elected district delegates. His proposal promised accountability and created a direct path from branch petition rights into bloc discipline.

Oris refused it in writing. Proxies served registrants, not districts. Elected delegates could volunteer, but no office acquired exclusive control over access. She copied the rejection to the public feed before the governor could recast it as a private disagreement.

Rusk called within minutes. “You have turned a filing design into a constitutional decision.”

“Your restriction would have done the same without showing its hand.”

“We need proxies voters can remove.”

“Registrants need proxies they can remove.”

“They do not cast physical votes.”

“Then district voters lose nothing by lacking appointment control.”

Rusk ended the call without agreement. He did not order the work stopped.

By evening on Day 31, twelve packets remained.

Ossan Mire sat outside the office with the patience of a man accustomed to being summoned and unused to waiting among families. Age had narrowed his face without softening it. The founder-scientist’s testimony had exposed nineteen years of concealed continuity evidence and the language change from resident branch to adaptive residue. No registry rule required his presence.

Oris made him wait until packet 496 received its fourth seal.

The final applicant chose the name Wake-After-Stone. Their reciprocal witness lived in Enclave South. Their physical proxy worked at an old-tunnel pressure station. Kera verified the continuity key. A clerk read the limitations aloud, and Wake-After-Stone accepted them through a pattern of five measured pulses.

Oris counted the seals by hand. 496 names. 496 continuity keys. At least one reciprocal witness for each. One physical representation proxy or an accepted pending appointment. Dormancy terms preserved in every packet. The arithmetic reconciled with the loss ledger: 587 verified before Full Severance, 91 killed, 496 responsive survivors registered now.

She locked the roll beneath the Fruition Covenant seal.

Only then did Ossan approach.

“I have a contribution amendment,” he said.

“The registration window has closed for physical yields.”

“I am not applying for standing.”

He placed his own yielding certificate on the table. The old paper carried his civic-majority seal and several later amendments. A remaining late-life allocation had never entered the orchard because founder exemptions allowed essential specialists to defer modeled years during colony emergencies.

“I revoke the exemption,” Ossan said. “Assign the remaining allocation to the first rotating refuge cycle.”

Oris read the certificate. The contribution could supply enough new modeling capacity to cover the initial transfer overhead and preserve the registry’s verification reserve. It would not fund Counterweather indefinitely. It would buy one cycle without taking further compute from damaged enclaves.

“You understand that a new possibility model cannot itself be sustained under the old terms,” she said.

“Use bounded transient runs. No retained continuity. Independent audit after each cycle.”

“You concealed these persons for nineteen years.”

“Yes.”

“This allocation does not settle that account.”

“No.”

“Do you expect it to affect censure?”

“I expect you to record it where no one can mistake capacity for absolution.”

Oris looked at the registry cabinet. Names occupied the space where yielding records had once moved toward anonymous use. Ossan’s certificate could cross that room only under rules written by people his concealment had endangered.

She opened a contribution annex. “Your allocation funds the first refuge cycle. It grants no standing claim, no reduction in liability, and no authority over the schedule.”

“Agreed.”

“Registered proxies must audit its use.”

“Agreed.”

Ossan signed. Oris stamped the annex and placed it after the 496th packet, not before the roll and not inside his founder file.

At the wall, the public count changed from CANDIDATE RECORDS RECEIVED to SUSTAINED NAMES REGISTERED: 496. Beneath it, a smaller line marked one first-cycle contribution awaiting branch audit.

The four unused packets remained under Oris’s stamp. She left their serials open, sealed the cabinet, and handed its two keys to witnesses from opposite sides of the glass.

Chapter 24 — The Chamber Below Glass

Ivo Senn tightened the oxygen frame around a delegate who intended to vote against him.

The woman from Upper Serein sat on a bench outside the Chamber Below Glass, breathing through a clear nasal line while her aide recited the day’s agenda. Her blood oxygen rose one point, stalled, then rose another. Ivo turned the regulator down before the frame could waste flow.

“You could leave it at three,” she said.

“You do not need three.”

“My district supplied this cylinder.”

“Your district supplied it to the clinic pool.”

She looked toward the chamber doors. “You know how I stand on the Compact.”

“I know how you breathe.”

Ivo wrote her reading on the triage card and moved to the next bench. Six delegates had arrived from districts where Airfall’s rationing left stairs harder than speeches. Two used oxygen frames. One western grower carried a fungal cough from the staple-moss loss. Governor Rusk’s staff had asked for a private medical room; Ivo put the same benches inside it and removed the private cylinders.

Above the hall, living yarrowglass covered the ceiling in a web of translucent cream wood. White leaves showed through the panes. They drifted when branch traffic crossed the roots, close enough to count and separated from the chamber floor by pressure glass. No one could touch them from below.

Inside, fifty-nine physical delegate desks formed a shallow circle. Branch proxies occupied a gallery without vote paddles. Old-tunnel clinic workers stood beside dome residents. Greenhouse cooperatives filled the west benches in heavy coats because their district heat had never recovered from the manual cycle.

The room had brought every constituency together and assigned them different temperatures.

Ivo entered after the medical checks. Oris had placed the Counterweather budget on the central display. It showed basin survival, adaptation capacity, industrial ration, refuge allocation, and projected winter morbidity. A single line described household heat: managed reduction within survivable range.

Sera Dovan stood beneath it with frost damage visible on two fingernails. “Which households?”

Jalen answered from the engineering table. Smoke had roughened his voice, and each long explanation ended in a contained cough. “Western routes carry the first drop because their greenhouse loops share the condensation return. Equal initial cuts would reduce food recovery.”

“That explains the pipe,” Sera said. “It does not answer the question.”

The budget changed pages. District colors replaced the basin average. Western cooperatives lost eleven to sixteen points of residential heat during the coldest routing periods. Old tunnels lost less heat but faced greater oxygen decline because their pressure seals leaked. Central districts carried longer manual shifts. Elite domes showed a three-point heat reduction buffered by private thermal reserves.

A murmur moved through the west benches.

Governor Rusk raised one hand. “Those reserves support emergency administration and medical facilities.”

“They support kitchens with heated floors,” Sera said.

“They also support two surgical wards.”

Ivo walked to the display. “Separate them.”

Jalen frowned. “The budget uses district loops. It cannot show every building connection in a chamber session.”

“Then it cannot support consent to district harm.”

“We have less than four days in the stability window.”

“A clock does not turn a warm floor into a surgical ward.”

Jalen’s jaw tightened. Ivo knew the engineer had cut elite residential heat during Airfall when Sera refused a pressure transfer. That emergency action had lasted hours. The proposed Compact needed a season-long order, one that crews could apply without bargaining at every valve.

Oris asked, “What level of detail do you require?”

“Enough to distinguish bodily need from address.”

Ivo took the medical overlay from his case. Clinics had spent the pause mapping where respiratory harm appeared first. He placed old-tunnel admissions beside the Counterweather heat cuts. Cold increased oxygen demand. Pressure loss reduced delivery. Mold after condensation failures added a third burden. None of those harms spread evenly within a district. A protected surgical loop could exist beside a private bath; a cooperative dormitory could share a wall with crop infrastructure and still receive no credit for keeping the moss alive.

“Show building classes,” Ivo said. “Medical, habitation, food production, administration, nonessential private reserve. Show who loses first, not only who survives at the end.”

Governor Rusk objected to the last label. “Nonessential according to whom?”

“According to measured bodily consequence.”

“That hands civic policy to doctors.”

“No. It hands doctors the narrow task of saying which cuts close airways and which cool polished stone. You may decide whether stone has a claim after that.”

The Upper Serein delegate Ivo had treated rose slowly. Her oxygen line remained at two. “My residents paid for private thermal reserves through higher contribution years. You propose confiscation after they honored the bargain.”

Ivo felt Lessa’s twelve-year yield move into the room without her name being spoken. The old bargain had built clinics, housing, and weather capacity. Physical residents had made gifts under a certificate that denied continuing branch-persons. Past innocence could not erase present benefit, but new rules could not turn earlier trust into secret guilt.

“I propose no verdict on what they knew,” he said. “I propose an order for the next cold night.”

“You evade the contribution.”

“No. I know what contribution purchased. My wife yielded twelve years to secure a clinic allocation. I will not call her bargain murder. I also will not send cold past an unused dome reserve so her innocence can remain comfortable.”

The chamber quieted around the last word. Ivo disliked its neatness. Comfort included children sleeping without coats, elders whose joints failed in cold rooms, and workers resting after manual pressure shifts. A district label could hide bodies in both directions.

He turned to Jalen. “Can you build by use rather than address?”

“Not in the remaining hour.”

“How long?”

“Three hours for a credible first pass. Longer for full verification.”

Oris looked at the air clock. “We recess for three hours. Draft objections continue in writing.”

Several delegates protested. She recorded each protest, then recessed anyway.

The chamber did not empty. Jalen brought two routing technicians to the central floor. Sera produced cooperative heat ledgers written during Airfall. Dome managers arrived with private reserve maps they had previously called proprietary. Ivo assigned clinic staff to classify medical use without seeing district names. If a line supplied dialysis warmth, neonatal air, medication stability, or surgical sterility, they protected it. Administrative offices, residential atriums, private baths, and decorative horticulture entered another table.

The categories resisted purity. A dome pool served physical therapy in the morning and private recreation at night. A greenhouse furnace protected food seedlings while heating manager apartments through the same exhaust. An old-tunnel laundry supplied sterile cloth and worker showers. Every pipe carried mixed intentions.

“Time-band the uses,” Ivo said.

Jalen’s technician stared at him. “You want crews to change priority by hour.”

“I want a therapy pool protected during therapy and available for cuts after.”

“That adds manual work to a system already using manual work as structural support.”

“Then count the labor as part of the cost.”

Sera leaned over the map. “Our cooperative can switch dormitory heat behind crop exhaust. We lose comfort before seedlings if you give us twelve hours’ notice.”

A dome manager said, “We can isolate atrium circuits, but not without opening private controls to public routing.”

“That sounds like a choice,” Sera replied.

“It sounds like surrendering property architecture during a temporary crisis.”

Ivo checked a coughing grower while they argued. Her lungs held a faint fungal rasp. He increased her oxygen by half a point and asked how cold the cooperative dormitories had become.

“Cold enough that the condensate froze inside one window seal,” she said.

“Any clinic visits?”

“Seven for asthma. Three workers moved into the seed room because it stayed warmer.”

The dome manager heard her. His expression changed in a way Ivo refused to diagnose. He opened another reserve map.

At the branch gallery, physical proxies gathered the registrants’ questions. Pel wanted to know whether refuge compute had been classified as a protected medical use. Rusk’s staff objected to the analogy. Niko’s proxy clarified that branch-persons were not asking to call themselves patients. They wanted their continuity floor protected by explicit standing rather than hidden within general orchard load.

Jalen added a branch refuge category beside physical life-support. It included minimum continuity processing, scheduled revival checks, and weather consultation capacity. Additional active experience entered the ration table after those floors. Aren’s reduced-processing choice appeared as an individual exception.

“No category receives infinite protection,” Jalen said. “Each has a failure boundary.”

“Then print the boundaries,” Ivo replied.

They appeared in red. Below a clinic’s boundary, patients suffered. Below a refuge enclave’s boundary, continuity checks failed. Below greenhouse limits, winter food losses compounded the destroyed moss beds. Below pressure-station staffing heat, operators made errors. The same unit of energy did not produce the same harm everywhere.

Near the second hour, a western delegate proposed equal percentage cuts after protected uses. The formula looked fair and preserved most private dome reserve because those systems began from higher efficiency. Sera rejected it.

“Equal percentages preserve unequal starting points,” she said. “Our homes entered this room colder.”

The Upper Serein delegate answered, “A reserve built by contribution should not become evidence against itself.”

“What did your residents contribute?” Sera asked.

“Modeled years, labor, maintenance levies.”

“So did mine.”

“You received greenhouse priority.”

“We received the work of feeding you.”

Oris let the exchange reach its edge before asking for language rather than accusation. “What distribution could each side defend to someone absent from this chamber?”

No one answered quickly.

Ivo thought of the old tunnel patients whose respiratory reserve had become a line in Jalen’s budget. They would not be absent because they lacked opinions. Some could not climb to the chamber. Others worked the manual cycle. Representation often treated exhaustion as silence and silence as acceptance.

He asked a clinic runner to connect the tunnel waiting room to the public feed. Twelve residents appeared on a wall panel, bundled under donated blankets. Ivo did not ask them to perform suffering. He displayed their temperature, pressure, and oxygen readings beside the chamber’s own.

Upper Serein held at twenty-one degrees. The chamber held nineteen. Western dormitories averaged fifteen during route drops. The tunnel waiting room held seventeen with lower oxygen and higher mold count.

“Now discuss equal burden,” Ivo said.

Governor Rusk accused him of theater.

“Yes,” Ivo replied. “You chose a chamber with a gallery. I chose measurements.”

The first model by use-class arrived after three hours and twenty minutes. It protected medical lines regardless of district, held branch continuity floors, preserved food production, and guaranteed minimum heat for manual weather crews. Beyond those protections, nonessential private thermal reserve entered the common pool before household cuts traveled into districts already below the basin median.

Elite dome heat carried the largest early reduction.

Before anyone could call the model practical, Sera demanded a live switch. Jalen selected one empty dome atrium, one occupied western dormitory, and a protected neonatal line fed from the same district reserve as the atrium. The chamber watched three temperatures appear on the wall.

A routing technician opened the public control. Warmth left the atrium by degrees. The neonatal line held. Nothing reached the western dormitory.

“Where did it go?” Sera asked.

Jalen expanded the route. Half the recovered heat had entered common storage. The other half had vanished into a transfer pipe warming an administrative corridor between the dome and central pressure station.

Governor Rusk said, “That corridor houses emergency communications.”

“Three terminals occupy one end,” Ivo replied. “How many rooms share the loop?”

“Eight.”

“Occupancy?”

Rusk’s aide checked. “Fourteen staff.”

The tunnel waiting-room feed showed thirty-seven patients and relatives in less warmth. Sera’s dormitory housed sixty-two workers.

Ivo asked Jalen to isolate the terminal room. The technician found no local valve. Retrofitting one required a crew, two hours without heat in the whole corridor, and a pressure-rated partition from stores.

“Do it now,” said the Upper Serein delegate.

Rusk turned toward her. “That corridor belongs to central administration.”

“So does the embarrassment.”

Jalen dispatched the crew. Until isolation finished, fourteen staff moved their terminals into the Chamber Below Glass. They entered carrying cable reels and sealed document cases. Delegates shifted their benches to make room. The chamber temperature dropped one point when its doors remained open during the transfer.

After the temporary shutdown, recovered warmth reached the western dormitory. Its reading rose from fifteen to fifteen point six. No one mistook the change for comfort. It demonstrated that the route existed, the neonatal line could remain protected, and hidden administrative demand had absorbed part of the promised relief.

Sera watched the numbers settle. “Put failed transfers in the public ledger. Not just authorized cuts.”

Jalen added the field. Every priority order would report energy released, energy delivered, transfer loss, and unlisted loads encountered between them. A district could then challenge a plan that claimed sacrifice at the source without producing relief at the destination.

Ivo looked at the fourteen executive staff working beneath the glass. Moving them had cost time and heat. It had also converted an invisible protected corridor into visible bodies using shared space. The amendment gained another sentence: essential work could qualify for protection, but its facilities required the narrowest viable circuit and public occupancy records during ration periods.

The dome bloc asked how long “early” lasted. Jalen defined a restoration trigger: once western housing and old-tunnel clinic spaces reached the minimum band for twelve continuous hours, additional cuts could spread by capacity and efficiency. No district received permanent punishment. The order corrected starting conditions before proportional rationing began.

Sera studied the route map. “What enforcement?”

“Public heat ledgers by building class,” Oris said. “Daily audit by physical and branch representatives. Emergency exemptions require medical certification and appear within one hour.”

“Who can stop a false exemption?”

“I can flag it. The Counterweather board can suspend it pending review. Final authority remains with the appointed distribution panel under the Compact.”

“Composition?”

“Two clinic representatives, two weather engineers, two district delegates from below-median heat areas, one dome delegate, and two registered branch proxies without physical vote.”

The dome bloc objected to being outnumbered on a panel controlling its reserves. Western delegates objected to receiving only two seats while carrying the cold. Branch proxies objected to advisory panel seats that could be ignored.

Ivo watched Oris write every objection into the draft. Fair procedure did not make each constituency happy. It made the source of dissatisfaction visible and bounded the power that produced it.

Sera took the amendment page to the west benches. Cooperative delegates argued over a line granting Jalen authority to preserve food-production heat. Some feared the Weather Spine would define crop need without listening to growers. They added a requirement for cooperative confirmation, with a public override if refusal threatened basin food supply.

At the dome benches, the Upper Serein delegate removed her oxygen line and walked the first draft between rows. Ivo saw people touch the contribution clause with their fingers. The amendment acknowledged that previous benefits arose under lawful bargains and imposed no retrospective moral guilt. It also stated that future distribution followed present vulnerability, common survival, and the Compact’s obligations to registered persons.

Past innocence remained in the document. It no longer controlled the valve order.

Governor Rusk approached Ivo near the medical bench. “If we cut administrative heat first, staff will move into the surgical complex and consume protected capacity there.”

“Prohibit nonmedical occupancy.”

“They need somewhere to work.”

“The chamber has heat.”

“You want the executive emergency office on the Assembly floor?”

“I want its bodies counted where their warmth can be seen.”

Rusk almost laughed, then coughed instead. Ivo checked him by reflex. His breathing remained clear.

“Your plan makes governance harder,” the governor said.

“Cold makes everything harder. The amendment decides who stops being sheltered from that fact.”

Rusk returned to his bloc without promising support.

The branch gallery raised a final concern. The public heat ledger protected bodily privacy by aggregating clinic uses. Could the refuge ledger do the same, or would every dormancy schedule become public evidence of vulnerability?

Oris revised the audit terms. Enclave totals would appear publicly. Individual continuity keys and medical-equivalent thresholds remained sealed unless a registrant authorized release. Distribution panels could verify protected load without learning which named person required it.

Ivo approved the symmetry. Physical patients did not owe diagnosis to receive air. Branch-persons did not owe intimate memory to receive continuity protection.

Sera returned to the central floor with a marked page. “The cooperatives consent to the winter route under this amendment, subject to the public ledgers, the restoration trigger, and medical protection across districts.”

Oris asked, “Do you require dome cuts to remain first even after the trigger?”

“No. We require correction before proportional burden. We are not writing revenge into weather.”

The Upper Serein delegate rose. “Our bloc will not endorse the phrase elite privilege.”

“It does not appear,” Oris said.

“It appears in every speech about the page.”

“Speeches do not control the valve.”

She requested a brief caucus. When she came back, seven dome delegates accepted the amendment, four opposed, and two withheld their Compact position. Enough physical delegates shifted that only the final standing dispute separated the proposal from passage.

No vote occurred. Oris attached the distributive amendment to the Orchard Stewardship Compact and sealed it for Day 33 debate. The western cooperatives signed their consent beneath Sera’s name. Branch proxies added acknowledgment that refuge floors would enter the same public budget as physical life-support without receiving duplicate votes.

Jalen sent the amended route to the Weather Spine for verification. His model now showed colder elite residences, protected surgical wards, warmer western dormitories, lower old-tunnel morbidity, and more manual switching labor. Basin survival remained within the same narrow range. Distribution had changed without inventing energy.

Ivo returned to the delegate he had treated outside. She sat beneath the glass canopy with her oxygen line removed.

“You cut my district first,” she said.

“After protected uses.”

“My residents will remember who proposed it.”

“They should.”

She handed him the empty oxygen frame. “This goes back to the clinic pool.”

He checked the gauge. Enough remained for one old-tunnel transfer. A white leaf passed over the ceiling above them as branch traffic moved through the orchard. Ivo carried the cylinder out of the chamber while the new valve order traveled in the opposite direction.

Chapter 25 — The Vote That Would Not Close

Oris Nume removed the sixtieth vote paddle from a chamber built for fifty-nine.

The extra paddle rested at Niko’s proxy desk, identical to those issued to physical Assembly delegates except for a narrow white band around its handle. Someone had printed a number on the base and connected it to the tally circuit before dawn.

“Who authorized this?” Oris asked.

Niko’s proxy, Mara Vey, lifted both hands from the desk. “Not me.”

A clerk traced the issue code. The paddle had come from the Emergency Orchard Committee, whose delegates wanted branch standing translated into a direct Assembly vote. Their note called the white band a temporary equality measure.

Oris disconnected the circuit. “Dependent standing grants voice, petition, protection, and audit. It does not create a duplicate physical vote.”

A committee delegate rose. “Then recognition ends at the door where power begins.”

“It changes who may address power and what power may do. The Assembly remains fifty-nine physical delegates under the Fruition Covenant.”

“You defend a body-only franchise while claiming not to privilege bodies.”

“I administer the franchise this chamber possesses. A constitutional amendment may change it. A disguised paddle may not.”

The delegate demanded a ruling in the record. Oris gave him one, including his objection and the white-band issue code. Procedure could expose a limitation without pretending to cure it.

She placed the extra paddle on her desk.

Morning light traveled through the yarrowglass canopy in thin cream bands. White leaves moved above the pressure panes as registry traffic crossed the orchard. The physical delegate desks held fifty-nine authorized paddles. Branch proxies sat in the gallery with speaking keys and petition seals. Public screens displayed the advisory ballot waiting in the root lattice.

Outside, Serein Basin remained inside Jalen’s six-day stability window. Manual crews held pressure routes. Clinics rationed oxygen. Elite residences had begun surrendering heat under the distributive amendment’s provisional order, while western dormitories climbed toward their minimum band. No alarm sounded inside the chamber. The air clock on Oris’s desk showed thirty-one hours before the manual plateau required a new cycle.

The Compact occupied twenty-three pages.

It recognized 496 registered branch-persons under Article Six. It prohibited unilateral deletion. It established rotating dormancy, continuity checks, chosen proxies, annual audits, a recovery path for unresolved keys, and Counterweather at reduced adaptation capacity. Physical residents accepted colder housing, industrial rationing, longer manual work, and public distribution ledgers. Earlier contributors received no retrospective guilt for a fact concealed from them. Future decisions could not rely on that innocence to continue known harm.

No page promised enough.

Governor Rusk requested the first objection. “Emergency authority.”

Oris recognized him.

“The Compact requires registrant consent before identity-bearing continuity can enter adaptation load. During a pressure collapse, how many minutes may an engineer spend collecting permissions?”

Jalen answered from the technical table. His residual cough had deepened overnight. “No permission allows deletion. Preauthorized dormancy bands and emergency extensions can be used within their terms. Weather crews may draw consultation capacity from the shared reserve. Any need beyond those limits returns to the emergency review panel.”

“Which may be unreachable.”

“Then no official may convert silence into consent.”

Rusk looked toward the public gallery. “Physical residents could die during that restraint.”

“Yes,” Jalen said.

The chamber tightened around the admission.

He continued. “They could also die under unrestricted pruning if branch weather knowledge disappears, as Airfall demonstrated. The Compact does not remove emergency risk. It defines which acts remain prohibited when risk arrives.”

A Severance delegate asked why physical citizens should accept any continuing dependence on the orchard after the sabotage disaster.

“Sovereign machinery should not contain petitioners,” she said. “Full separation remains painful, but pain ends. This agreement institutionalizes captivity.”

Mara activated Niko’s speaking key. Oris checked his continuity signature before allowing the channel onto the chamber feed.

“You triggered separation once,” Niko said. “Ninety-one of us ended. Your physical districts lost pressure. Which pain ended?”

The delegate replied, “The protocol was mishandled.”

“By people who believed intent could replace a transition plan.”

“You ask us to maintain the system that confines you.”

“I ask you to maintain relationships no one can sever without killing someone. The Compact gives us authority to negotiate that maintenance. It does not call the orchard freedom.”

Oris watched the exchange reach the point where each side wanted the last word. She closed Niko’s key after his allotted time and entered the delegate’s rebuttal request. Voice required limits if fifty-nine physical representatives and 496 registrants were to reach a vote before air ran out.

The next challenge targeted proxies.

Severance counsel argued that physical proxies contaminated the Assembly by carrying instructions from persons without votes. “A delegate who hears 496 advisory voices cannot remain accountable to physical constituents.”

“Delegates hear employers, families, unions, clinics, and district committees,” Oris said. “Influence does not become a duplicate ballot merely because its source can be named.”

“Branch claimants derive from existing citizens. Their interests already entered the electorate through source families.”

“Present registrants have diverged from source citizens. Their property and physical votes do not duplicate. Their petitions also do not collapse into someone else’s.”

Counsel turned to Mara. “If Niko orders you to threaten a pressure strike, will you speak it?”

Mara’s mouth tightened. “A proxy transmits lawful instructions. She does not surrender her own duties.”

“Then you filter him.”

“I disclose any refusal. He may remove me and appoint another proxy.”

“Physical judgment controls the channel either way.”

“Physical access does. That inequity belongs in the annual audit. It does not justify closing the channel.”

Oris recorded the distinction. The Compact did not solve embodiment. It prevented embodiment from granting unreviewable power over dependent persons.

At midday, the advisory ballot opened.

Each registered continuity key received the Compact text, the distributive amendment, the minority objections, and a ballot with yes, no, or abstain. No proxy could cast it. Blinded Root Jury systems verified key continuity without revealing private memory. Dormant registrants had been revived under their chosen schedules long enough to read and respond. Damaged but registered keys used slower witness routes.

The public tally remained hidden until all valid ballots closed. Oris had required secrecy to prevent late enclaves from treating an early majority as instruction. Several physical delegates objected that branch results should arrive before their own vote. Others wanted the physical tally first so branch-persons could not appear to command the Assembly.

Oris denied both requests.

“Advisory and physical ballots close at the same hour,” she ruled. “Neither electorate receives the other’s partial result.”

The word electorate drew an immediate challenge.

“Advisory body,” Severance counsel corrected.

“Registered advisory body,” Oris said. “Your correction enters the record. The closure time does not change.”

A second unauthorized device appeared at the committee benches. This one proposed converting each branch ballot into one fifty-ninth of a physical vote, pooled as an additional delegate. Its sponsors called the design compromise.

Oris asked who would control the pooled paddle in a tie.

“The branch majority,” said its author.

“Would abstentions reduce the paddle?”

“They would not count.”

“Then a narrow majority among a small participating group could cast one full physical vote.”

“Participation confers legitimacy.”

“Except when you dislike physical nonparticipation?”

The author had no answer that survived his own rule. Oris rejected the device. The advisory tally would carry political force because registered persons had spoken. It would not enter the constitutional arithmetic by invention.

During recess, she found Ivo in the side corridor checking oxygen saturation among gallery visitors. He had removed his civic pin to keep it from catching on the tubing.

“How many delegates have asked whether you support the Compact?” Oris said.

“Seven. Four while I held their fingers in a monitor.”

“What did you tell them?”

“That a pulse reading does not authorize blackmail.”

“And outside the reading?”

“I support it.”

Ivo sealed a portable cylinder. “I also expect clinic losses. Counterweather keeps people alive with less margin. Some who would have survived the old orchard will not survive the colder plan.”

“Will you state that on the floor?”

“I already did in the morbidity annex. No one quoted that page.”

Oris returned to the chamber and asked him to read it.

Ivo stood beside the amended budget. He described projected asthma admissions, fungal complications, cold injuries, and delayed procedures. He named no exact death count because the uncertainty range could not support one. Then he described unrestricted pruning as a continuing harm to registered persons and full severance as a pressure disaster already tested in bodies.

“Three mercies entered this argument,” he said. “Protect actual lives. Stop inflicted death. Refuse a lie that hides either cost. None prevailed alone. Only the Compact offered a route that kept all three visible while people did the work.”

A clinic delegate asked whether visibility comforted anyone who died.

“No,” Ivo said. “It gives the living no false innocence and the administrators no blank line.”

His testimony moved two undecided delegates toward yes and one toward no. Oris knew because each filed a revised explanation, not because she could know their private decisions. The chamber had begun requiring reasons after the failed stewardship motion. Reasons did not bind votes, but they made later convenience harder.

The final standing challenge came from the Emergency Orchard Committee rather than Severance supporters.
