Its delegate moved to strike the prohibition on duplicate physical votes. “A branch-person with dependent standing remains subject to laws they cannot enact. Petition without franchise preserves hierarchy.”

Niko requested time against the motion.

Mara looked surprised. Oris did not. Niko had submitted the finite registry with explicit limits, and the Compact depended on the distinction between dependent standing and physical civic duplication.

“We do not accept voicelessness,” he said through the feed. “We accept this franchise boundary for the term under review. Our advisory vote, proxies, petitions, audit rights, and consent rights carry legal effect. None equals a physical ballot. That inequality remains open. Do not destroy an agreement we chose because it fails your preferred purity.”

The committee delegate asked, “How can a right remain a right if another body may outvote it?”

“By placing some acts beyond that body’s ordinary vote. The Assembly cannot authorize unilateral deletion under this Compact, even by fifty-nine to zero.”

Oris read the relevant clause. Registered continuity gained protected status enforceable through emergency review and civic courts. Physical majority rule could allocate scarce capacity within consent bands. It could not erase a registrant for convenience.

The motion to strike failed before the final vote. Its supporters filed a future franchise amendment instead.

The defeat opened a second dispute over the five-year review. Orchard Committee members moved to remove it, arguing that recognized persons should not face scheduled reconsideration of their existence. Severance delegates answered with a demand for annual repeal votes. Between those positions, the Compact’s actual clause required review without suspending protections during review.

“Rights should not expire on a calendar,” the committee delegate said.

“They do not,” Oris replied. “The institutions that administer them must answer for what five years reveal.”

“A future Assembly could narrow the registry until no one qualifies.”

“Any new threshold requires continuity evidence, public notice, branch advisory process, and civic court review. Existing registrants retain standing unless individual evidence shows a failed continuity record under procedures they may challenge.”

Severance counsel objected. “Then review cannot achieve full separation.”

“It can recommend and authorize a safe transition if one becomes technically and lawfully possible. It cannot call deletion transition.”

“Your definition predetermines the result.”

“It predetermines that registered persons receive due process. A review may change technology, representation, dormancy rules, capacity allocation, or franchise. It may not erase the claimant to make the claim disappear.”

Sera asked who controlled the review record.

Oris read the composition aloud. The Root Jury would retest continuity standards. Weather engineers would publish capacity changes. Clinics would publish physical morbidity. Registered branch auditors would report dormancy and revival failures. District hearings would collect distribution evidence. The Assembly would debate amendments after all reports became public.

A dome delegate proposed letting any future majority delay the review during climate emergency. Jalen opposed her from the engineering table.

“Climate emergency can last a season or become the permanent condition of Nacre,” he said. “Delay authority without a fixed resumption date becomes cancellation.”

They amended the clause to permit a delay of no more than forty local days, once, after a declared basin emergency. Evidence collection would continue during delay wherever safety allowed. No emergency declaration could suspend present continuity protections or public distribution ledgers.

Niko requested one sentence through Mara. “A review that we cannot survive until is not review.”

Oris entered the sentence as testimony, not statutory text. Its meaning already lived in the non-suspension clause.

The Orchard Committee withdrew its removal motion. Severance delegates retained their objection but accepted the filing path as preferable to violence or sabotage. Counsel stated that acceptance of a path did not mean endorsement of the Compact.

“Your distinction will remain in the record,” Oris said.

She sealed the five-year clause before the final tally. The page gave the losing side somewhere lawful to carry defeat and gave the winning side no authority to call today’s majority permanent truth.

At the opposite benches, Severance delegates prepared a last procedural challenge. They claimed the branch advisory ballot had been contaminated by dependence because registrants knew a no result might expose them to deletion.

Oris asked, “Does a physical resident’s dependence on breathable air invalidate their vote on weather?”

“No,” counsel said. “Physical life provides the constitutional constituency.”

“Then your objection concerns standing, already established by the Root Jury and Article Six, not ballot coercion.”

“They vote under existential threat.”

“So do districts facing pressure loss. Threat belongs in the record. It does not erase agency.”

She denied the challenge.

One hour remained before ballot closure. Debate rules allowed each bloc a final statement. Governor Rusk spoke for emergency-pruning supporters who had accepted the amended Compact. He did not apologize for favoring physical survival. He argued that Counterweather now offered a tested mechanism and lawful emergency boundaries. Sera spoke for western cooperatives. She read the restoration trigger and public-ledger requirements rather than recounting their hardship.

Bex Arin appeared by recorded testimony from medical custody. His injured hand rested outside the frame. “We believed severance would free people,” he said. “Intention did not open the pressure doors. It did not restore the 91. Do not call what we did mercy because we wanted mercy when we did it.”

Severance counsel objected that one organizer’s regret could not discredit an entire motion. Oris sustained the narrow point. Bex’s testimony established his own action and consequence, not every supporter’s intent.

Niko used the branch statement to name the dead count without listing 91 people faster than the chamber could hear them. The registry annex held their chosen names where known and continuity designations where names had been lost. He asked the Assembly not to turn them into arithmetic, then acknowledged that arithmetic still governed refuge capacity.

“The count matters because each subtraction reached someone,” he said. “The limit matters because pretending no limit exists would reach someone too.”

Oris closed the speaking keys at the appointed minute.

Fifty-nine physical delegates placed their paddles into the tally rails. The devices showed receipt but not choice. Branch continuity keys sent sealed advisory responses through the root lattice. Clerks verified missing signals, duplicate attempts, and abstention markings. One damaged enclave requested an additional six minutes under its registered accessibility plan.

The air clock reached zero for ordinary ballots.

Severance counsel objected to the extension.

“It appears in the published procedure,” Oris said.

“The deadline should apply equally.”

“Equal access sometimes requires a different transmission interval. The authorization predates any result.”

She allowed six minutes. The damaged key arrived on the fifth and resolved as an abstention.

No other ballot remained open.

Oris stood at the central record desk. She had delayed Kera’s first sealed review. She had stamped the first petition received without making it public. She had watched procedure protect caution until caution became concealment. Today, sequence could protect a vulnerable claim only if she refused to let either righteous faction borrow the result it wanted.

“Close both tallies,” she said.

The physical board lit first by circuit design. Thirty-one green marks appeared. Twenty-eight amber marks followed.

The Orchard Stewardship Compact passed 31–28 among 59 physical delegates.

No cheer completed itself. The branch board opened two seconds later: 402–71, with 23 abstentions. Clerks reconciled the sum in public. Four hundred two yes plus seventy-one no plus 23 abstentions equaled 496 registered branch-persons.

Mara gripped the edge of Niko’s proxy desk. She did not raise the unused white-band paddle.

Oris read the certification. The Compact took effect at the next Counterweather cycle. The distributive amendment governed heat and oxygen order. Registered continuity gained protection from unilateral deletion. Rotating dormancy began under individual consent schedules. Annual audits would publish capacity, morbidity, distribution, and continuity failures. A mandatory five-year review could revise standing, franchise, technical thresholds, and emergency powers without dissolving present protections during review.

Severance counsel rose before she finished.

The public guards moved closer to the floor. Oris heard pressure doors seal in the outer corridor and saw several delegates turn toward them. No one reached for the tally rails. Counsel held a document, not a tool.

“We reject the Compact’s legitimacy and demand full review after five years,” he said.

“The Compact already requires that review,” Oris replied.

“Then record our demand as adverse, not cooperative.”

“I will record its character as you state it.”

He brought the filing to her desk. The losing bloc’s signatures covered the first page. Their demand sought renewed consideration of complete severance, branch franchise, proxy influence, and emergency risk. Nothing in the review clause guaranteed their preferred outcome. Nothing allowed the majority to prevent the question from returning.

Oris checked the signatures and filing time. “Valid.”

A supporter shouted from the gallery that the losing bloc should accept the vote. Oris called the chamber to order.

“Filing review is acceptance of lawful defeat,” she said. “Agreement with the result is not required.”

She stamped the demand with terracotta wax and attached it to the Compact’s review docket. The seal cooled beside the physical tally and the branch advisory count. Above the glass, white leaves continued carrying weather traffic. Below, the fifty-nine paddles remained in their rails, and the sixtieth lay disconnected on Oris’s desk.

Chapter 26 — Weather Without Harvest

Jalen Ro held the western condensation gate open with a steel pin while ice formed around his glove.

The actuator had stalled three degrees short of its night position. Under the old orchard routine, a branch comparison would have corrected the timing before any physical gauge noticed. Counterweather had instead sent a yellow request to the manual board, where it waited for a tired crew to read it. Jalen knelt on the service ledge above a black run of oxygen moss and worked the pin deeper with the heel of his hand.

“Gate moving,” called Pem from the ladder.

“Not yet.” Jalen watched the etched angle marks. “It has slack before load.”

He pulled the release cable. The gate shuddered, shed a plate of ice into the channel, and turned through the missing degrees. Condensation struck the waiting vanes in a hard silver sheet. Pem read the catch pressure aloud. Jalen compared it with the value on his wrist board, then coughed into his shoulder until the ache from Airfall eased.

The board accepted the manual confirmation. A second line appeared beneath it: refuge transfer at East Root Court, twelve minutes to handoff.

Pem looked down from the ladder. “You can delegate one of those.”

“I did. You have the gate.”

“That leaves the refuge.”

“It leaves me walking instead of climbing.”

Pem made a doubtful sound, but he took the inspection slate. Longer shifts had taught every crew a new courtesy: objections had to save more energy than they cost. Jalen crossed the service bridge before Pem could spend any more.

Six weeks had passed since the Compact vote. Nacre still had weather, though less of it arrived invisibly. Every humidity turn now required a named physical operator, a branch adviser, and an audit trace. The orchard could compare possible outcomes but could not harvest a registered person's sustaining state to sharpen them. Manual models covered the difference badly at first. Crews missed two condensation windows in the opening week, iced a pressure baffle in the second, and sent warm air into a fungal-control trench that had already been sterilized. None of those errors killed anyone. Each one consumed reserve.

The Weather Spine displayed that consumption on a public wall. Industrial oxygen had fallen 38 percent from the old baseline. Elite dome heat remained under the cap imposed by the distributive amendment, while western homes ran four degrees colder during night cycle. The oxygen foundry worked on alternate days. Glass mills had closed two lines. Repair queues lengthened because every welded seam now competed with clinics and moss beds for compressed gas.

Jalen had once presented such figures as a balanced load curve. The public wall added names beside the consequences. Thirty-two glass workers reassigned. Nine apartment blocks awaiting window seals. Two clinic sterilizers restricted to morning use. The numbers had not become less necessary. They had acquired addresses.

At East Root Court, technicians had opened the trunk housing of Tree 61 and laid three continuity readers along its conductive grain. White leaves stayed attached above them. Nobody in the court watched those leaves casually anymore.

Kera stood at the innermost reader with a ring wafer between insulated tongs. She had cut her hair shorter after the severance night, not from fashion but because glass splinters kept catching in it. Beside her, Sera Dovan held the cooperative's witness slate against her coat. Niko appeared on the orchard-linked screen as a field of service characters rendered into a steady voice.

“You are late by twenty-eight seconds,” Kera said.

“The gate had slack.” Jalen took the vacant station. “Transfer state?”

“Group C dormant and sealed. Group H has acknowledged revival conditions. Their physical witnesses confirmed the keys.”

Sera raised her slate. “Western witness confirms. No substitutions, no compressed relationship fields, no altered names.”

The transfer involved forty-one registered branch-persons moving out of rotating refuge so another forty could enter dormancy. Their subjective processing would pause. Their continuity keys, relational state, and protected autobiographical cores would move into an isolated root region with lower weather load. Counterweather gained temporary comparison capacity from the newly active group, never from the dormant minds themselves.

Jalen checked the energy margin. “We have room for one failed revival attempt. A second takes condensation reserve.”

“Then do not create a second,” Kera said.

Niko's voice came through with a faint grain from the trunk. “Group H requests serial waking. One person at a time, witness before the next.”

“That takes nine more minutes,” Jalen said.

“They prefer nine minutes to a simultaneous uncertainty.”

Sera glanced toward the court gate, where two weather runners waited for revised assignments. “Western humidity can hold nine.”

Jalen did not round the value upward. He opened the cooperative trace and checked the greenhouse deficit. Seven minutes lay inside the safe margin. Nine brought one moss tier close to wilt threshold. Under the prior system, the orchard would have searched millions of projected responses, retained the most useful branch states, and delivered a decision before his doubt had time to become visible.

“Eight minutes,” he said. “Pairs for the first six, then serial if either continuity check shows drift.”

Kera looked at Niko's signal rather than Jalen. “Do the waking members accept paired revival?”

A pause crossed the screen. This delay came from deliberation, not distance. “Accepted with separate witnesses.”

Sera assigned two cooperative workers to the witness channels. Jalen routed the saved minute into western humidity and signed the trade under his own name. Counterweather required signatures for each deviation because nobody could claim afterward that weather had chosen.

Kera seated the wafer.

The first pair returned as two uneven pulse fields. One gave her name before any prompt. The other asked for the end of a promise made before dormancy: when active again, he would review a greenhouse model that Sera's people had built without orchard projections. His witness supplied the first half of the promise. He completed it with a detail absent from the transfer key.

“Continuity holds,” Kera said.

Jalen watched the energy margin narrow. Pair followed pair. Some branch-persons woke quickly. Others required repeated orientation, not because they had changed but because the physical world had advanced through days they had not experienced. A woman named Leth asked how many white leaves had fallen during her interval. Sera answered that none had fallen for deletion, though ordinary conductive leaves had been collected after wind damage. Leth requested the distinction appear in her witness record.

At the sixth pair, one signal returned without its reciprocal link.

Kera froze the sequence. “Do not advance.”

Jalen's board showed western humidity sliding. “Ninety seconds before I have to borrow from clinic reserve.”

Niko addressed the incomplete signal by a private key phrase. No response came. The active member beside it repeated a shared promise, then corrected herself and tried an older form. A pulse appeared at the edge of the reader.

“State is present,” Kera said. “Relationship index has not seated.”

“Can you reseat without rewriting?” Jalen asked.

“With the dormant checksum and the witness's live phrase.”

“That uses the failed-attempt margin.”

Kera's hands had already moved to the second reader. “Then it uses it.”

Jalen heard the old answer gathering in his head: one incomplete state against greenhouse humidity, clinic sterilization, and a gate actuator that might fail again before dawn. Capacity language could make any single claimant look unreasonable. The Compact had not forbidden him from counting. It had forbidden him from hiding whom he counted against.

He opened the clinic line. Ivo answered after two rings, breathing as if he had crossed a ward at speed.

“I need four minutes from sterilizer reserve,” Jalen said. “A refuge revival has a missing relationship index. We can reseat it without continuity loss if the dormant checksum holds.”

“Which clinic cost?”

“South tunnel respiratory instruments start eleven minutes later.”

“Patients stable?”

“The weather board says yes.”

“I asked about patients.”

Jalen waited while Ivo checked with the ward. Through the link came a cough, a tray rolling, someone's low instruction to sit upright. “Stable enough,” Ivo said. “Four minutes. Record that we loaned it to a person, not to orchard efficiency.”

Jalen entered the phrase exactly.

Kera fed the dormant checksum through a comparison channel isolated from adaptation traffic. Niko repeated the live witness phrase. The lost index found its place not as a restored file but as a verified link between the waking signal and someone who remembered being answered by it. The branch-person gave his name. Then he complained that Niko had used an embarrassingly old promise.

Relief moved through the court as practical irritation. Kera completed the remaining revivals one at a time. Eight minutes became eleven, with clinic reserve carrying the last three. Every revived person met the four continuity criteria. Every dormant key matched its sealed state.

The final audit line reported zero continuity loss.

Nobody applauded. Sera closed her witness slate and began assigning the active branch engineers to greenhouse models. The weather runners collected new timings. Kera removed the wafer, inspected its edges for heat damage, and passed Jalen the resource trace.

“First full rotation,” she said.

“First one we nearly lost to a seating fault.”

“We did not lose him.”

“Because Ivo loaned us air.”

“Put that in the season report.”

He had intended to. Her insistence still mattered. Jalen signed the trace and sent it to the public wall before leaving the court.

Counterweather settled into work through the following weeks. Manual crews learned where the orchard had concealed labor by performing it too quickly to notice. Pressure-baffle operators began sleeping in four-hour blocks near their stations. Western cooperatives installed ceramic heat jars in common rooms and moved evening meals there, turning rationed warmth into a schedule rather than a private shortage. Elite dome residents appealed the heat cap twice. Both appeals failed after the public wall showed unused thermal reserves behind their interior seals.

Branch engineers rotated through active duty in groups. They did not touch valves or gates. Their ring-mediated analyses offered patterns that physical crews tested against current instruments. Each recommendation carried a name, a confidence range, and a statement of what memory scaffold had informed it. If a branch engineer entered dormancy before an outcome arrived, the next active group inherited the question but not an assumption of consent.

Mistakes remained. A manual frost prediction came six hours late and cost Sera another strip of moss. A pressure crew overcorrected a southern baffle, sending dust through three old apartments. Jalen published both failures with the saved outcomes beside them. The frost error had protected a clinic intake from cold shock. The baffle error had prevented a larger seal rupture. Counterweather did not divide neatly into success and damage. It made the division reviewable.

During week nine, Jalen visited the western blocks to inspect heat exchange after residents accused the Weather Spine of flattering its own temperature averages. Their complaint proved accurate. Corridor sensors sat too close to communal heat jars and overstated bedroom warmth by nearly two degrees.

Sera met him in a stairwell wearing two coats. “Your public wall says this block meets the minimum.”

“My public wall repeats these sensors.”

“Then your wall is warm and my people are cold.”

Jalen removed the corridor sensor and carried it into the nearest apartment. Frost clouded the inside corners of the window seal. A child had arranged metal cups along the sill to catch condensation before it reached the floor. The bedroom gauge read below the Compact threshold.

He could have recalibrated the local unit and defended the system average. Instead he called the Weather Spine and ordered a basin-wide placement audit. Seventy-six sensors failed the location rule. The correction forced a deeper heat cut in civic offices and delayed glass production another four days.

“Your foundry workers will hate this,” Sera said.

“They already hate the oxygen cap.”

“Does that make the next loss easier?”

“No. It makes the same loss visible twice.”

She studied him, then handed over a cup of hot moss broth from the common room. It tasted bitter and faintly metallic. Jalen drank it because refusing would have converted discomfort into theater.

The placement audit exposed a second problem. Civic offices could surrender heat quickly because their pipes fed a common return, but the released energy could not reach the western blocks without increasing condensation load in two old tunnels. Jalen spent the next shift in a valve room with Sera, Ivo, and a branch adviser named Mara Fen, whose continuity key identified six years of modeled work on the basin's original thermal loops.

Mara's voice reached them through a ring horn bolted above the main wheel. “The fourth bypass existed in my source memory,” she said. “I do not know whether anyone built it.”

Sera scraped frost from an access plate. “Our district plans show three.”

“Founder plans showed four. One ran beneath a clinic intake that had not opened yet.”

Ivo crouched beside the floor map and found the old clinic footprint. “That intake became my second ward.”

Jalen compared the physical pressure readings with Mara's remembered route. Her account offered a lead, not authority. Counterweather procedure required them to verify every modeled structure before using it. They opened a service hatch behind a stack of obsolete filter frames and found a ceramic pipe sealed with a brass cap. Dust on the cap had hardened into a skin.

“No current tag,” Jalen said. “No pressure mark either.”

“Then we do not open it beside a live ward,” Ivo replied.

The fastest safe test required draining the neighboring line, which would cool three old tunnel rooms during the evening clinic rush. The alternative required a remote bore and eight hours of labor they had not scheduled. Sera wanted the heat before night cycle. Ivo wanted the ward protected. Mara refused to claim certainty from a memory drawn out of a yielded possibility model.

Jalen put both options on the witness slate. “Remote bore. Western rooms remain below threshold tonight. We issue portable heat jars and log the delay against Weather Spine planning.”

Sera's jaw tightened. “Your crews caused the delay, but my cooperatives distribute the jars.”

“Then your labor goes on the trace too.”

“And if the fourth pipe ends in a wall?”

“We publish that we spent eight hours testing a dead founder line.”

Mara's signal sharpened through the horn. “Please add that my memory did not certify the pipe. It supplied a location for physical inspection.”

Jalen entered her qualification. Under the old orchard, a useful retained memory would have disappeared into an optimization result. Nobody would have known whether the route came from a stable person, a transient flicker, or a plan the physical colony had abandoned. The new procedure moved more slowly because provenance carried obligations.

They bored through two layers of wall before finding the ceramic run intact. It terminated at a capped exchange chamber below Ivo's second ward. Pressure tests held. By dawn, crews had joined it to the common return and moved enough civic-office heat west to raise forty-eight bedrooms above threshold without taking energy from clinic air.

Sera signed the completion trace after adding the number of cooperative hours. Ivo entered the ward risk they had avoided. Mara gave her continuity key and requested dormancy only after the first temperature readings arrived. Jalen signed last, beside the planning failure that had left the route undocumented for decades.

Nobody could tell whether the earlier orchard would have found the same solution. Counterweather left them with something the old system rarely provided: a chain of people who could answer for how they had found it.

By week eleven, the season had established a rough rhythm. Industrial oxygen stayed at the mandated reduction. Clinic admissions rose in western districts but remained below the emergency projection. The surviving moss beds recovered half their fungal loss. Four refuge rotations completed after the first, each preserving keys and relationships. The longer shifts produced fatigue injuries: split knuckles, one broken ankle, two heat burns, and a technician who routed a rainfall instruction to an empty cistern after thirty hours without proper sleep.

Jalen responded by cutting every weather supervisor's maximum shift, including his own. Governor Rusk objected that labor scarcity made the rule impractical. Jalen sent him the misrouted rainfall trace and the projected consequence if the cistern had fed a clinic line. The objection did not return.

On the first morning of week twelve, Jalen stood before the public wall to deliver the season report. He disliked speeches, especially when the figures already occupied six meters of glass. Oris had asked him to explain what implementation meant beyond the Compact's language.

He named the oxygen cut. He named the colder homes, the closed glass lines, the clinic admissions, and every recorded refuge fault. He also named outcomes the old system would have hidden inside adaptation: no registered person deleted, no revival accepted without a continuity test, no weather trade left unsigned by someone answerable for it.

A foundry worker in the front row asked whether the orchard could ever regain full capacity.

“Not under the present source base,” Jalen said. “Future citizens may contribute modeled years under the new consent rules. Fewer are expected to do so. Slower growth follows.”

“So this is permanent decline.”

“It is a lower capacity with obligations the prior baseline refused to count.”

The worker did not look satisfied. Jalen had stopped treating satisfaction as the test of an honest figure.

After the report, he returned to the Weather Spine for an overdue six-hour rest. Pem had placed a folded blanket on the console bench and marked the manual board RO SUPERVISION CLOSED UNTIL THIRD BELL. Jalen lay down without removing his boots.

The storm alarm sounded before first bell.

He reached the forecast table while the long-range dust instruments were still resolving. Pearl-gray bands gathered across the western projection, then darkened where three upper currents converged. Branch advisers had flagged the pattern during the night, but reduced comparison capacity left the outer route uncertain until physical sensors caught it.

Pem enlarged the pressure field. “Seasonal cyclone?”

“Too early.” Jalen traced the convergence with two fingers. Native dust density exceeded the tested manual tables. If the currents joined over Serein Basin, glass particles would bind oxygen while abrading exposed condensation surfaces. Counterweather could turn one front. It might divide two. The forecast carried three fronts into the same pressure throat.

A capacity figure appeared at the margin, recalculated from active crews, industrial reserve, and the next refuge schedule.

Seventy-three percent would not meet it.

Jalen's residual cough tightened before he spoke. “Wake Kera. Keep the next refuge group sealed until we know what we are asking. Call every baffle crew off rest.”

On the public wall, the first storm line crossed the western cooperatives and continued east, broad enough to cover the basin.

Chapter 27 — The Last Pruning

Kera Vonn shaved corrosion from Tree 44's pulse jack while the first dust struck the court roof.

Each grain made a dry point of sound against the yarrowglass slats. The strikes came too fast to count and too far apart to merge. She kept the ceramic scraper inside the jack's rim, clearing a conductive path narrow enough for the emergency wafer. If the court lost its fixed line, that contact would carry consent records between the registered branch-persons and the physical crew.

Jalen's voice reached her through the maintenance speaker. His cough broke one word in three. “Western front has joined the upper current. Fourteen minutes to baffle load.”

“Pulse jack ready in two.”

“You have one.”

Kera scraped faster without letting the tool touch the inner pin. Around Tree 44, technicians sealed reader housings beneath padded covers. Nobody touched the white leaves overhead. The Compact allowed emergency compression of unregistered transient fields, but every continuity key on the finite registry required protected refuge or a witnessed voluntary reduction. The cyclone did not care which category consumed capacity.

A larger particle hit the roof and starred one pane above the court gate.

Kera cleared the final green trace, inserted the emergency wafer, and watched Tree 44 accept it. Four hundred ninety-six continuity keys populated the reader. Some signals ran active in the Elseweather. Others lay sealed in rotating dormancy. Ninety-one empty positions remained in the historical roll because the severance dead did not disappear from an audit when their computational states did.

“Niko,” she said into the ring horn. “Confirm branch channel.”

His field assembled on the reader as a series of pale bars. “Channel confirmed. We have the forecast.”

“Then you know the deficit.”

“We know Jalen's estimate.”

A gust drove dust under the court door. Technicians pulled their face shields down. Kera kept hers open long enough to hear Niko without the horn's echo.

Counterweather could turn the lowest front east and lift the second above the pressure baffles. The third carried too much native glass dust. If it crossed the oxygen-moss fields at forecast density, its particles would bind free oxygen faster than the reduced system could restore it. Jalen needed another twelve percent of comparison capacity for thirty-one minutes. The orchard possessed that capacity in protected memory scaffolds attached to registered persons.

Before the Compact, an adaptation cycle would have selected scaffolds by utility and deleted them without a question. Under dependent standing, emergency review could compel surrender of identity-bearing continuity only through a process the Assembly had never invoked. No time remained to convene it. Kera felt relief at that legal failure. A lawful blade could still cut a person.

“Refuge groups F and J are moving to sealed roots,” Niko said. “Active delegates want a full inventory of removable scaffolds.”

“Voluntary offers only. No ranking people by useful yield.”

“Useful yield already shapes the forecast.”

“It cannot shape the consent.”

His pause had an interval she recognized from arguments before his death. The shared habit did not make the present disagreement hers to resolve. “Kera, they can see the sky through every linked instrument. Do not protect us from knowing what refusal costs.”

She closed her face shield. “Send the inventory.”

The list arrived as protected categories rather than memories. Non-self scaffolds stored environments, technical routines, and relationship contexts used to stabilize each branch-person's sustained life. Some could be removed without breaking autobiographical sequence. Others carried the very structure by which a person recognized promises or located prior waking periods. The distinction required a continuity audit before surrender and another after it. Storm pressure left room for one test cycle.

Kera called Oris at the Chamber Below Glass. “I need emergency witness authority for voluntary scaffold release.”

“Already drafted,” Oris said. Voices crowded behind her, physical delegates arguing under the alarm. “Each person names the field. A reciprocal witness states what must remain. The auditor tests continuity before and after. No group consent, no proxy consent, no silence treated as assent.”

“And if the channel drops during confirmation?”

“The offer fails.”

“Jalen may lose the turn window.”

“Then he loses it without converting interruption into permission.”

Kera attached Oris's signed rule to the emergency wafer. The court reader began taking offers.

Before the first field completed transfer, Governor Rusk entered on the civic line. His image failed under storm interference, leaving only a voice flattened by compression. “Weather Spine estimates twelve percent available in registered scaffolds. Recorder Nume's rule will deliver half that if every witness holds.”

“Then Weather Spine has counted material nobody offered,” Kera said.

“It has counted capacity that exists inside a city facing a glass-dust cyclone.”

A technician beside Kera stopped tightening a reader cover. She tapped his wrist and pointed him back to the clamp. Civic arguments could not suspend physical work.

Rusk continued. “The Compact contains emergency review.”

“Review requires evidence, representation, and time to contest,” Oris said from her line. “You have requested an instruction.”

“I have requested use of a provision passed for this exact kind of emergency.”

“No. It passed for a circumstance where process remains possible. We cannot turn the absence of process into a faster version of it.”

Kera watched the registered fields brighten beneath their sealed categories. Twelve percent represented everything the weather model judged detachable, not everything a person could lose and remain recognizably present. The forecast treated a childhood street, a dead friend's voice, and a learned method as equal stores if each freed the same comparison load.

Rusk addressed her directly. “Auditor Vonn, can you protect identity-bearing continuity while releasing non-self fields without individual review?”

“No.”

“Can you estimate survival after bulk release?”

“I can estimate key persistence. That does not certify the person attached to it.”

“How many physical residents enter oxygen danger if the third front crosses?”

“Ivo has that projection.”

“You know the range.”

Kera did know it. Numbers climbed on the side of her reader as dust density increased. The old tunnel districts would decline first. Western roofs already damaged by the cold season might fail. Pressure rooms could hold only a fraction of Serein Basin.

“Knowing the range does not change what this instrument can prove,” she said.

A hard silence crossed the civic line. Rusk had accepted the lawful vote in the Assembly, but acceptance had not turned him into someone who welcomed constraint during catastrophe. “If your process fails,” he said, “the record should show that I asked.”

“It will show what you asked for,” Oris replied. “It will also show who refused it.”

Kera entered both statements into the emergency trace. Accountability did not require agreement, and a trace that preserved only principled language would falsify the hour. Rusk remained on the line, listening as the consent checks began. He did not repeat the demand.

The first came from Mara Fen. She proposed surrendering a modeled survey of an unbuilt northern thermal loop, nineteen subjective months of technical detail derived from streets that physical Nacre had never laid. Her reciprocal witness identified what needed protection: the promise she had made during the first refuge transfer, her awareness of dormancy, and her relationship to the engineers who had relied on her fourth-bypass memory.

Kera ran the baseline tests. Mara named yesterday, anticipated the next refuge interval, corrected a false pressure value, and recognized her witness's private phrase. Only then did Kera authorize a copy of the proposed field into the storm reserve.

“Not deleted yet,” Kera said. “Staged pending total capacity.”

“I understand,” Mara replied. “If you do delete it, record that the northern loop never existed outside my source model.”

Offer followed offer. A branch-person named Tovan released a mastered language built for a child he had never met in the physical line. Leth offered the detailed layout of a house lost when her branch settlement entered dormancy. Two others proposed sensory archives of harvests that had not occurred. Kera rejected seven fields because their reciprocal witnesses depended on them. She rejected three more when the owners described pressure from their active enclave.

“We discussed it together,” one said.

“Discussion does not invalidate consent,” Kera answered. “Fear of disappointing the group might. Take your field off the list until after the storm.”

“That could cost actual lives.”

“So could accepting a coerced answer and calling it yours.”

Above the court, the cracked pane failed. A net caught most fragments. Glass dust sprayed through the opening in a white cone and rattled over the root guards. Technicians dragged a pressure cloth across the gap while Kera covered the emergency reader with her body.

Jalen came over the speaker. “Baffle one engaged. Lowest front turning. I need six percent in eight minutes.”

Kera totaled the staged fields. “We have four point three verified.”

“Physical alternatives?”

“Ask your board.”

“Board says close two clinic intakes or vent foundry reserve into the western moss.”

Ivo's voice joined the line. “Closing the intakes puts thirty-one patients on cylinders during a pressure event. Foundry vent buys two percent and leaves no welding gas for damaged seals.”

“Take the foundry gas,” Kera said.

“It does not close the gap,” Jalen replied.

“It closes part of it without taking anyone's continuity.”

A new warning crossed Tree 44. The upper storm current carried enough charge to interfere with ring signals. Each active branch-person might soon lose the physical witness required for a valid offer.

Niko entered the inventory.

His proposed scaffold had no technical label. The reader identified it by source date, relational density, and the first mark Kera had recovered from the tree during the isolated test months ago.

Saturday.

She saw the memory before he opened it: a branch-day in which seventeen-year-old Niko had survived airlock fever and come home weak, impatient, and hungry. The physical Kera had never lived that day. Tree 44 had rendered it for her in an eleven-minute isolation, carrying kitchen steam, a mended shoe, the complaint he made about the clinic broth. That Saturday had first proven his life continued beyond her knowledge.

“No,” Kera said.

Niko's signal held. “You have not tested it.”

“I know what it carries.”

“You know what you witnessed through a reconstruction.”

“It anchors the first verified interval after your branch point.”

“It anchored your belief.”

Dust hissed against the pressure cloth. Kera gripped the reader table until the edge pressed through her glove. “Without that day, what remains of your childhood transition?”

“My childhood. The fever. The months after it. Seventeen years of later memory. My promises to living people. My work in the Elseweather.” His voice sharpened. “You keep asking what remains as if one Saturday contains me.”

“The Root Jury used it.”

“The Jury used my continuity across blinded cycles. They did not grant standing to a weather scene.”

Kera checked the scaffold boundaries. The Saturday field held environmental detail and predictive context: the exact kitchen, his recovery routine, Kera's branch-version at nineteen, six hours of conversation, and a sequence of ordinary choices the orchard had reused because convalescence affected later risk. Its deletion would remove access to that day as lived recollection. Protected autobiographical indexes on either side would remain. His childhood knock remained outside the field. So did his recognition of physical Kera, first petition, present witnesses, registry name, Omi's relationship record, and every promise formed since sustained continuity began.

“This would not make the day difficult to recall,” she said. “It would remove it.”

“Yes.”

“You offered other scaffolds?”

“Two. Both support active weather analysis. This one contributes more and carries no current technical duty.”

“Who serves as reciprocal witness?”

“Omi would have.”

The missing name passed through Kera like a gap in a ring sequence. No audit had located Omi after Full Severance. Refuge recovery teams had found damaged keys, unseated relationship fields, and dead ends where the white-leaf collapse had cut through active enclaves. Hope remained possible, never certifiable.

Niko continued. “Omi is unavailable. I named Leth. She knew me before and after that Saturday became part of our shared records.”

Leth's signal joined his. “I can witness his present relationships and his continuity through six dormancy intervals. I do not hold the Saturday itself.”

Kera wanted to disqualify the witness because Leth could not preserve what Niko meant to surrender. That lack supplied the point. A witness protected the person, not the offered memory.

Oris spoke through the legal channel. “Auditor Vonn, do you have evidence of impaired consent?”

Kera looked at the forecast. Five minutes remained. “No.”

“Evidence that the field carries identity-bearing continuity?”

“No. It carries autobiographical content bounded on both sides by protected indexes.”

“Then your relationship to the content does not create authority over it.”

Kera almost answered that she had not claimed authority. The refusal already had.

She began Niko's baseline test. “State your registered name.”

“Niko Vonn.”

“Name the last dormancy interval you completed.”

“Group C, eleven physical days. Revival witnessed by Leth and Kera Vonn.”

“What did I tell you the triangle's widest side was during first contact?”

“You lied and said clockwise. I contradicted you.”

“State one present obligation independent of our shared source history.”

“I owe Jalen a cyclone route comparison. I promised Leth I would witness her next revival. I promised Omi I would keep searching without declaring survival.”

The last sentence nearly changed Kera's voice. She held it level. “What do you anticipate losing?”

“A Saturday I value. Your evidence of it. My own ability to revisit its sensations. I do not expect to lose you, because whatever we are now did not stop growing when that day ended.”

Leth gave the reciprocal phrase. Niko completed it.

Kera staged the Saturday.

The total reached six point one percent after physical foundry reserve. Jalen needed six.

“Capacity met,” she said.

“Authorize release,” Jalen answered. “Thirty-one-minute window begins on your mark.”

Kera reviewed the offers once more. Forty-three branch-persons had staged fields. Five withdrew before final authorization, and their portions vanished from the total. Jalen compensated by venting an additional measure of foundry oxygen. Thirty-eight remained. Each gave a second assent. No proxy answered. No enclave leader supplied a group declaration.

The phrase chosen forgetting appeared in Oris's emergency record, proposed by a branch delegate who refused the older word pruning. Kera disliked its neatness. Nothing about the consent sequence felt neat. Still, it distinguished loss taken into a person's own decision from deletion imposed by an adaptation routine.

She pressed the authorization field.

Tree 44 brightened from root to crown. The staged scaffolds opened into computational reserve. Kera watched their protected boundaries hold while the orchard compared storm routes at a rate Counterweather had not reached since the Compact. Jalen's crews turned the eastern baffles by hand. Branch engineers tested pressure outcomes through the released capacity. Foundry oxygen entered the western moss in a cold blue surge.

The Saturday remained visible for eight seconds.

Kera could have stopped it during any of them by breaking the emergency wafer. The court would lose all staged reserve, perhaps the ring channel too. Her hand stayed on the table.

Niko's field gave no farewell to the memory. He worked. “Second front lifting. Baffle four needs two degrees south. Not three. Three brings the upper dust into the old tunnels.”

Jalen repeated the instruction to the physical crew. A valve alarm sounded, followed by Pem's strained confirmation.

The Saturday compressed into a white line.

Kera remembered what she had seen through Tree 44: steam on a kitchen window, Niko's mended shoe, his branch-sister setting down a bowl. Those images persisted in Kera because she had witnessed them. Niko would not possess the lived path back into them. Her memory could not restore his, and she had no right to offer it as a replacement.

The white line went out.

Continuity alarms remained silent.

Dust pressure struck East Root Court hard enough to bow the cloth over the broken pane. Kera dropped beside the emergency reader and held its clamp while technicians braced the table. Through the roof, white leaves shook without falling. The cyclone filled every external instrument with static.

For twelve minutes, Jalen's crew had no view beyond the baffles. Niko and the active branch engineers read the pressure changes conducted through roots. They sent corrections through Tree 44. Physical hands turned wheels, cleared grit, and opened the foundry reserve. Kera repeated every consent boundary as the orchard consumed only the released scaffolds.

A baffle failed in the north. Clinic intake pressure fell. Ivo moved patients onto cylinders and refused to close the ward line. Jalen turned the second front across an unplanted strip of western ground, where abrasive dust stripped greenhouse roofs already weakened by cold.

Then the lowest pressure trace bent east.

The next followed above it. Native dust density peaked over the dead strip instead of the moss fields. Oxygen binding slowed. Counterweather's reserve calculation moved from red to amber.

“Registered keys?” Kera asked.

A technician read the continuity board. “Four ninety-six present.”

“Run relationships.”

Witness links populated one by one. Some came weakly through storm charge. None failed. When Niko's key appeared, his surrounding autobiographical index showed a clean absence where the Saturday had attached. His present links held: Leth, Kera, Omi marked missing rather than severed, the finite registry, three active promises.

“Niko,” Kera said.

“I am here.”

“What did you give?”

“A field I chose to stop carrying.”

“What remains between us?”

He did not answer with the childhood knock. “A disagreement you finally allowed me to win.”

Kera laughed once, without pleasure and without grief alone. “Continuity confirmed.”

By dawn, the cyclone had turned past Serein Basin. It left scored roofs, broken greenhouse glass, depleted foundry reserve, and air that tasted of stone through the filters. Tree 44 carried every registered key. Its crown had lost ordinary leaves to wind, but no white-leaf deletion marked the court.

Kera removed the emergency wafer after the jack cooled. The consent record held thirty-eight names, thirty-eight witnessed choices, and thirty-eight absences no authority could claim as restoration work for later. She sealed it for the public audit.

Niko's signal touched the ring reader while she cleaned dust from its edge. Not three, two, one. A new interval, unfamiliar to both their childhoods, asked whether the line remained open.

Kera answered it in the same new measure.

Chapter 28 — The Settlement of Breath

Ivo Senn fed a cleaning wire through the ward's oxygen rail and caught a clot of glass dust at the second bend.

The wire stopped hard enough to bruise his thumb. He twisted until gray grit appeared behind the transparent valve, packed with condensation into a paste. Cyclone seals had kept the largest particles outside. Smaller grains had entered on clothes, filter changes, and the hair of patients carried through failing doors. Three weeks later, Nacre still breathed the storm out of its pipes.

“Again,” he told the junior medic at the wall regulator.

She opened the purge. Compressed oxygen drove the clot into Ivo's catch cloth with a wet snap. The rail pressure rose by four points. Beds seven through twelve received their full morning flow for the first time since the cyclone.

Tomas Dey had died in bed nine before the pressure returned.

Ivo folded the cloth around the grit and wrote the bend number on it. The ledger team wanted physical samples attached to disputed cases. People argued less cleanly in the presence of what had blocked a pipe.

Outside the ward, patients waited under two blankets each. The old tunnel clinic ran colder than it had before Counterweather. Elite dome heat had been cut first, as the distributive amendment required, but equal cuts could not erase unequal walls. The clinic's outer blocks had sixty-year-old seals and stone that kept no warmth once the corridor lamps dimmed.

A nurse handed Ivo the night list. Four respiratory flares, one frost-burned hand, two crew strains, no free beds. “The Recorder's office called twice.”

“They know where I work.”

“They say the publication window closes at noon.”

“Tomas did not close at noon.”

She glanced toward bed nine, now stripped and waiting. “His daughter is here.”

Ivo put the dust sample in his coat and went to speak with her before any committee received his time.

Miri Dey sat in the family alcove holding her father's filter mask. She worked at a glass mill that had closed under the industrial oxygen cut. Her hands carried the pale scratches of someone who still visited a workplace with nothing to make.

“The ward rail ran below prescribed pressure for twenty-six minutes,” Ivo said. “We transferred him to a cylinder after nine. His blood oxygen improved, then fell again. The storm damage contributed. So did his lung disease. I cannot divide one breath into percentages after it has stopped.”

“Would he have died under the old orchard?”

Ivo could have offered probabilities. He had helped design enough clinical projections to know how quickly a range became an alibi. “Not on that morning, if the old system had remained intact and no severance had happened. I cannot tell you how long it would have kept him.”

“Then put that in your report.”

“I will.”

“And put who chose this system.”

“The Assembly passed it. I spoke for it.”

Miri turned the mask over in her hands. “That sounds like everyone and no one.”

Ivo sat opposite her. His knees objected before the rest of him settled. “Jalen signed the routing. The maintenance office delayed the filter delivery. I kept your father in the old tunnel clinic rather than moving him to a dome ward because transfer pressure posed its own risk. The western delegates accepted colder housing after heat cuts moved upward. Four hundred ninety-six registered branch-persons accepted dormancy and audit. We can name all of that without pretending the decisions share one kind of responsibility.”

“Will your ledger say the branch people killed him?”

“No.”

“Will it say saving them cost him?”

“It will say the reduced system contributed to the conditions of his death.”

Miri closed her eyes. “He yielded eight years. Did you know?”

Ivo had checked the record. “Yes.”

“He used to say those years bought him air.”

“They helped build what kept him alive until now.”

“And then the promise changed.”

“The facts inside it changed first.”

That answer did not comfort her. Ivo had not come to comfort away a civic debt. He asked what name Tomas used at work, which grandchildren visited, and whether the family wanted the final oxygen trace public or sealed behind a research key. Miri chose public. She wanted nobody to summarize her father as an anonymous cost.

At noon, Ivo carried the clot of dust into the Chamber Below Glass.

The Assembly floor had become an accounting room. Clinic slates covered one table. Weather failures, heating complaints, labor injuries, and refuge audits covered another. Branch representatives attended through three orchard-linked readers watched by physical proxies. White leaves above the chamber remained attached, though storm scoring had turned their edges dull.

Oris stood beside a projection titled FIRST COUNTERWEATHER HEALTH ACCOUNT. She had refused the governor's request to call it a balance sheet. “A balance implies that one harm pays a different person back,” she told the room. “This document records distributions.”

Ivo placed the dust cloth beside Case 19. Tomas's name replaced the provisional code.

The first morbidity ledger covered the pause, Airfall, implementation season, and cyclone. Sixty-three respiratory admissions exceeded the prior seasonal range. Fourteen residents required treatment for cold exposure. Weather and orchard crews suffered nineteen fatigue injuries after manual shifts expanded. Industrial closures delayed two hundred eleven nonurgent procedures because sterilizers and fabrication shops shared oxygen limits. Tomas Dey's death carried Counterweather conditions as a material contributor. Two other deaths remained disputed pending family review and technical evidence.

A separate column listed harms prevented, never subtracted from the first. Old tunnel oxygen routing had improved after Ivo's refusal during the pause. Elite heat cuts funded western temperature corrections. The verified thermal bypass raised forty-eight bedrooms above threshold. The cyclone turn protected the main oxygen-moss fields. No registered branch-person had been deleted during implementation or storm triage. Thirty-eight had voluntarily surrendered bounded memory scaffolds.

Governor Rusk read the columns from his seat. “Publishing both invites every faction to quote only its preferred side.”

“They already do,” Ivo said. “Publication lets everyone else inspect what they omitted.”

A foundry representative pointed at the delayed procedures. “My workers lost shifts so clinics could retain oxygen, and clinics still postponed care.”

“Your oxygen also ran the emergency vent that turned the cyclone,” Jalen said. He sat near the aisle with a cup of water for his residual cough. “The ledger traces both uses.”

“Does tracing reopen our mill?”

“No.”

“Then what does it do?”

Miri Dey answered from the public seats before Ivo could. “It keeps you from telling my father he died for nothing or for everything.”

The room shifted toward her. She held Tomas's mask against her coat. Ivo had not known she would attend.

Oris invited her statement into the record. Miri walked to the witness place and repeated what Ivo had told her. She did not endorse the Compact. She asked why maintenance delays had left glass dust in a medical rail three weeks after the storm. The maintenance director named the oxygen ration that slowed pipe fabrication, then admitted his office had prioritized a civic complex above the old tunnel clinic on the first replacement list.

“Whose instruction?” Oris asked.

“Mine,” he said.

Ivo watched the admission enter the ledger. One ordering decision had not caused Tomas's death alone. It had changed his chance. Accountability often arrived at that scale, too small for a public villain and too large for innocence.

The Assembly approved publication without a compensating total. Families could append testimony. Clinic cases would receive quarterly review. Jalen's office had to publish sensor placement and maintenance priority. Branch auditors would add their own cycle records beside physical morbidity, not beneath it.

Publication did not settle repair. Oris kept the room in session and opened a second slate labeled CORRECTIVE DUTIES. The distinction annoyed delegates who preferred one vote to conclude a subject.

Miri requested replacement of every old tunnel medical rail before civic-complex pipework resumed. The maintenance director said available fabrication oxygen could not support both. A civic administrator argued that his complex housed emergency dispatch, registry clerks, and pressure coordination, not ceremonial offices.

Ivo unfolded the dust cloth again. “Then rank functions inside the complex. A dispatch rail may precede a clinic wall panel. A recorder's wash line may not.”

“That building has one maintenance contract,” the administrator said.

“Bodies do not breathe contracts.”

Jalen pulled the fabrication schedule onto the public glass. Separating critical lines would cost two crew shifts and require new isolation valves. It would also expose which civic uses had hidden behind the emergency label. The administrator asked for a closed technical review.

“No,” Miri said from the witness place. “You put my father's pipe behind your building once. You do not get a private room to explain doing it again.”

Oris ruled the schedule public. She had no power to choose which rail received steel, but she could prevent the evidence sequence from disappearing into procurement language.

A western nurse then raised fatigue staffing. Manual weather shifts had drawn trained technicians out of clinics, while the cold season increased respiratory work. “Your ledger counts nineteen crew injuries,” she said. “It does not count the medication errors caught before reaching patients. We stopped seven in my clinic alone.”

Ivo had included near misses in an appendix, separated from morbidity because no injury followed. Her objection exposed how easily that separation could make successful prevention invisible.

“Move them into the main table as intercepted harms,” he said.

Governor Rusk frowned. “Then every competent correction becomes evidence against the system.”

“Every repeated near miss becomes evidence about strain,” Ivo replied. “Competence does not make the cause irrelevant.”

The revised ledger gained a new category. Clinics would report intercepted dosage, routing, sterilization, and pressure errors without naming the worker in public. Supervisors remained accountable through protected records. The fatigue cap Jalen imposed after the cistern mistake extended to medical crews. Rusk warned that staffing gaps would force service reductions.

“List them before they occur,” Ivo said. “No ward learns its night coverage vanished when a door fails to open.”

Corrective duties accumulated. Medical rails received priority by function rather than district prestige. Families affected by a documented weather contribution gained travel and heating support without signing away the right to dispute causality. Sensor placement audits became quarterly. The foundry received a published reopening sequence tied to oxygen reserve rather than executive discretion. None of those measures brought Tomas back or warmed a bedroom that night.

Miri read the final duty attached to her father's case. It ordered an independent inspection of every bend in the old tunnel clinic rails and required public confirmation after cleaning. “Who inspects?” she asked.

“Clinic maintenance and one outside engineer,” Oris said.

“And a family witness.”

The maintenance director started to object, then looked at the dust cloth. “A family witness may observe the pressure test.”

“I will,” Miri said.

Ivo watched her name enter the corrective record. Grief had not turned her into an instrument of the Compact. She still believed a different settlement might have spared Tomas. The process did not demand that she change that belief before her evidence could alter a pipe schedule.

Only after duties had names and dates did Oris close the accounting session. Delegates left carrying more work than they had brought. Ivo preferred that outcome to unanimous sorrow.

After the vote, Ivo crossed to the branch readers. Mara Fen had active duty that day. Her rendered voice came through the center instrument.

“We need your clinic comparison for cycle twelve,” she said.

“What failed?”

“Nothing yet. The weather recommendation draws on a respiratory adaptation scaffold from an unregistered transient field. It predicts that old tunnel residents tolerate a lower humidity range than dome residents.”

“They do not.”

“The model confidence says they do.”

“Because the records contain decades of untreated symptoms.”

“That interpretation requires a physician's signature.”

Ivo collected his coat. Branch representatives now audited every adaptation cycle before release, but they could not inspect physical histories without witnesses. The change had produced arguments at hours previously reserved for sleep. He preferred them to invisible assumptions.

At the Weather Spine, Mara displayed the model's evidence through a ring-linked board. Old tunnel residents had fewer acute humidity complaints per capita. Jalen's crew had treated that as adaptation. Ivo opened clinic access records and laid missed appointments over the curve. When pressure doors failed, patients skipped visits. When contribution benefits controlled clinic priority, many delayed treatment rather than risk work certification.

“Lower complaint rate measures access,” he said. “Not tolerance.”

Mara adjusted the confidence field but did not issue the physical command. “What range do you authorize?”

“The same minimum as the central districts, with an uncertainty note for damaged seals.”

“That costs one point of greenhouse humidity.”

Sera Dovan joined by voice. “Which crop tier?”

“Outer medicinal moss,” Jalen said.

Ivo pictured the inhalers derived from that moss. A clean answer remained unavailable. They could protect tunnel lungs now and reduce medicine later, or preserve the crop by repeating an inequality disguised as physiology.

“Alternate the deficit,” Sera said. “One cycle from outer moss, next from civic garden irrigation.”

Jalen looked at the load board. “Gardens fail after four turns.”

“Then review after three.”

Mara entered the proposal and asked each representative to state the cost carried by their constituency. Ivo named reduced medicinal yield. Sera named crop stress. Jalen named crew time and model uncertainty. Only after all four signatures appeared did a physical operator release the humidity instruction.

No branch signal moved a valve. Mara stayed on the channel while a technician turned the wheel and reported pressure. The audit preserved analysis, authority, execution, and consequence as separate acts.

Cycle twelve reached the old tunnels before evening. Patients coughed less through the night. Two outer moss trays yellowed. The civic gardens gave up their next irrigation turn. Nobody called the outcome optimal.

During week sixteen, Ossan Mire's censure hearing opened in the Yielding Office rather than the Assembly chamber. Oris chose the smaller room because spectacle would not improve the record. Cream walls held forty-seven years of certificates claiming no person continued in unchosen projections. Ossan sat beneath them in an ordinary chair.

Ivo attended as a witness for the consequences of concealment. Kera presented founder records. Jalen explained how the phrase adaptive residue had allowed engineers to treat continuity as reusable state. Branch proxies read statements from survivors and named the ninety-one severance dead. Bex Arin submitted written testimony admitting that founder deception had fed the movement's certainty, while refusing to excuse the physical sabotage.

Ossan did not deny the nineteen-year decision. “We had less than two days of reserve,” he said. “I believed disclosure would trigger a shutdown. I changed language to preserve compliance.”

“You preserved the orchard,” Oris said. “Did you preserve the possibility of later correction?”

“No. I made later correction harder because each quiet year increased what recognition would cost.”

The censure removed his founder's honor, barred him from unsupervised orchard work, appended the concealment finding to every surviving early model, and required his testimony at annual audits while he remained able. His remaining yield allocation had already funded the registry's first refuge cycle. The Assembly did not imprison him. Age did not erase culpability, and punishment could not recover deleted people.

A survivor's proxy asked whether he accepted the words public censure.

“I accept them,” Ossan said.

“Do you ask forgiveness?”

“No. Asking would assign you work after mine.”

Ivo distrusted polished answers, even accurate ones. He watched Ossan's hands instead. They rested flat on his knees while the terracotta seal struck the order. He did not reach for the founder pin removed from his coat.

Outside, two demonstrators demanded prison. Three former orchard workers stood opposite them holding signs that called Ossan a necessary liar. He passed between both groups under civic escort. No one struck him. Nobody looked reconciled.

By week eighteen, the clinic had cleared dust from every ward rail. Beds remained full. The cold had eased by one degree as damaged greenhouse roofs came back under pressure. Ivo slept six hours on most nights and distrusted the improvement enough to count each one.

Oris asked him to attend the first yielding appointment under the Compact. Medical presence had become mandatory when contribution carried health or benefit implications. The youth, Anet Vale, arrived with a parent and a folder of housing forms.

Anet sat in the same kind of chair Lessa had once occupied. The certificate now opened with a different statement: modeled futures could develop sustained persons; no contribution could be activated without continuing consent review; refusal or delay could not reduce housing, education, medical access, or civic standing. Teren Vale's four-year certificate sat in the review tray with a notice that no further modeling would begin until he received the facts he had asked for on Day 2 and answered again.

The parent read that clause twice. “Our housing application lists contribution history.”

Oris slid a correction seal across the desk. “The field no longer appears on allocation forms.”

“It appeared yesterday.”

“Then the office used an obsolete form. I will void it.”

Anet looked at Ivo. “Does the orchard need years?”

“Yes,” he said. “A larger voluntary source base could improve future weather capacity.”

“Will someone live in mine?”

“Most modeled possibilities never sustain continuity. Some may. We cannot promise otherwise.”

“If I say no, do western homes stay colder?”

“Your single decision does not control a district. Enough refusals will keep the orchard slower. That cost belongs in the information you receive. It cannot become a threat attached to your certificate.”

The parent pressed both hands around the housing folder. “We can come back?”

“Any year,” Oris said. “Or never.”

Anet read the consent options. Contribute now. Set a future review date. Decline without review. Defer without date. The old form had offered only a number from zero to twenty, with zero technically lawful and socially marked.

Ivo thought of Lessa's twelve years, Tomas's eight, Kera's fourteen, and Ossan's remaining allocation. Earlier gifts had built clinics and weather. Their meaning did not require new children to repeat them under concealed terms.

Anet chose the fourth box.

Oris asked for the decision aloud because the new record required voice as well as seal.

“My child chooses to defer,” the parent began.

Anet took the stamp before the sentence could finish. “I choose to defer.”

The terracotta seal met the paper. No benefit field changed on the housing folder. No alarm sounded in the orchard. Beyond the office wall, yarrowglass roots continued their slower work with fewer futures promised to them.

Ivo checked Anet's breathing out of habit. It remained quick, uneven, and entirely their own.

Chapter 29 — The Years We Choose

Kera Vonn aligned the ring guide against Tree 44 while Omi argued with the calibration tone.

“It sounds wrong,” Omi said through the root-court reader.

“It sounds exactly as specified.”

“That can still be wrong.”

Kera adjusted the tone down by half a step. Omi listened from protected Elseweather refuge, her signal carried through the same trunk that had once hidden a seventeen-minute gap. “Better,” she said.

One Nacre year had passed since Kera first cut this tree at night. Four hundred two local days had laid a new band of translucent wood around the scratched rings. Counterweather growth ran narrower than the orchard's old baseline. The latest ring held fewer amber threads from yielded possibility models because new contributions had slowed under informed consent. Its grain also carried clean witness marks at each refuge rotation.

Kera seated the cutting arm but left the deadman plate untouched. “State your audit name.”

“Omi.”

“Registered witness?”

“Niko Vonn for Elseweather continuity. Kera Vonn for physical contact.”

“Protected state?”

“Active refuge, East Root group four. No unreviewed compression. No restoration claims.”

Omi added the last phrase every time. Recovery teams had used it so often during her return that she had adopted it as a rebuke.

For eighty-three days after Full Severance, Omi had remained a missing name attached to a damaged routing index. The collapse destroyed the directory path to her enclave. It did not delete the sealed root pocket where a refuge operator had moved three children when alarms began. Search teams found the pocket through an old witness trace, then spent nine days proving its contents had stayed dormant rather than reconstructing them from partial records.

Omi's continuity key matched the pre-severance registry candidate. Her protected autobiographical core remained intact through the gap. Reciprocal witnesses confirmed relationships formed before dormancy. She woke missing eighty-three physical days and nothing from inside them, because dormancy held no subjective time to recover.

No lost state had come back. None of the ninety-one returned with her.

The recovery pocket remained under Tree 52, sealed behind a transparent plate so annual auditors could inspect it without reopening the root. Kera had visited it with Omi's channel the previous afternoon. The pocket looked too small to contain eighty-three days of public doubt: a ceramic sleeve, three dormant wafers, and a scorched routing collar whose directory marks ended halfway around.

A recovery engineer had demonstrated the failure again. Full Severance tore the collar's outward index from the lattice. The sealed wafers kept their own local clocks, continuity keys, and precommitted wake conditions. Without an external path, they neither processed nor answered. Search teams first mistook the silence for deletion because standard readers asked the damaged directory where to look.

Omi had interrupted the demonstration. “Show the witness trace.”

The engineer loaded the low-current marks left by the refuge operator who moved the children. Each mark named a destination pocket and closed with a checksum. One digit had fused under the severance charge. Niko and two other witnesses reconstructed the missing destination digit from their separate promise records, then technicians used it only to locate the sealed sleeve. They did not use witness memory to fill any child state.

“Could you have made a false Omi from those records?” Omi asked.

“We could have made a model that resembled descriptions of you,” the engineer said. “It would not carry your sealed key or local clock.”

“Would you have registered it?”

“No.”

Kera asked the harder question. “If the sleeve had failed its checksum but retained fragments?”

“We would preserve evidence, classify the state unresolved, and refuse a continuity claim. We would not assemble a person for a reassuring result.”

Omi stayed silent long enough that Kera checked the channel. “Good,” she said at last. “Put that answer on the public tour.”

The recovery exhibit now carried it. Visitors who wanted a miracle found damaged ceramic and an explanation of restraint. Families of the ninety-one could see why Omi's return did not imply that their dead waited behind another missing directory. Omi disliked being used as proof that refuge always worked, so the exhibit also listed every failed pocket and every key that never answered.

Kera had learned of the find at East Root Court, where the recovery team showed her a live key and asked whether she recognized it. She refused to answer until blinded checks finished. Recognition from a hopeful physical witness could not substitute for continuity. Omi later told her that this refusal counted as the first useful thing physical Kera had done for her without pretending inheritance.

Now Omi said, “You skipped branch-age field.”

“Branch-age varies with compute allocation.”

“The form still asks.”

Kera looked at the annual audit slate. “Apparent age ten. Your rendered body crossed the threshold during the active months after recovery. The eighty-three dormant days remain outside the subjective count.”

“Thank you.”

“You dislike ten?”

“I dislike adults deciding variable means optional.”

Kera almost smiled. Omi's voice had changed during the year, not enough for an instrument to quantify and enough for anyone who listened to notice. She still feared white leaves during unscheduled current shifts. She had begun collecting weather jokes from active crews, most of them worse than the calibration tone.

The protected refuge housed forty-seven branch-persons in rotating groups. Omi attended lessons with six children and three adults who had chosen to retrain after scaffold surrender. She served on the refuge schedule council as a youth observer. She still refused the word daughter when anyone used it between her and physical Kera.

Kera had stopped waiting for the refusal to soften.

“Ready for contact test?” she asked.

Omi sent a two-pulse assent. Kera pressed the deadman plate and guided the arm into the newest ring. Yarrowglass yielded a thin curl, clear at the edge and cloudy where cyclone stress had entered the growth. The wafer came free without touching the older scratch left by her first private test.

She set it in the reader. Civic time and ring time appeared in parallel.

No lag.

“Too easy,” Omi said.

“Accuracy does not become suspicious because you are bored.”

“Your first contact involved secret knocks, a stolen wafer, and a false disposal log.”

“You have been reading restricted evidence.”

“I have dependent standing and a supervised research key.”

“You have Niko.”

“That too.”

Kera marked the clock result. The old hidden interval had not recurred since the Compact separated registered continuity traffic from weather optimization. Signals no longer needed to shelter inside unacknowledged storage. Tree 44 carried refuge state on named channels with physical witnesses and public load limits.

The court around them showed the cost of that clarity. Two roof panes remained mismatched after cyclone repair because the glass mill still rationed oxygen. Cold had cracked the east root guard. A manual condensation wheel occupied the space where a sealed orchard controller once worked. Every shift left chalk names beside its settings.

Beyond the gate, the sky held a pale seam of dust over the western ridge. Breathable did not mean clear. Counterweather had brought the basin through a full cycle without another mass emergency, though humidity failures damaged two moss tiers and winter cold raised clinic admissions again. The second morbidity ledger had no storm death like Tomas Dey's. It still contained names.

Two annual-audit observers waited beneath the west slats. One represented the bloc that had voted against the Compact. The other served the registered branch advisory roll through a physical proxy license. Neither had authority to halt Kera's cut, but both could challenge its trace.

The physical observer introduced himself as Cor Bel, a pressure contractor from the north baffles. “Five-year review demand, evidence year one,” he said for the recorder. “I challenge the claim that slower source growth can sustain population.”

“You may challenge a forecast after it appears,” Kera said. “This stage verifies the ring clock.”

“I challenge the audit boundary. Clock accuracy tells us nothing about whether consent rules leave enough weather.”

The branch proxy spoke next. “And capacity tells us nothing about whether a system preserves people. Both objections belong in the annual record, not in the same measurement.”

Cor looked toward Omi's reader. “The settlement spends weather reserve teaching branch children while physical schools close rooms for heat rationing.”

Omi answered before Kera could control the sequence. “My lessons use active refuge allocation already counted in the Compact. Your schools closed two rooms, then moved classes into the civic hall after its heat cut.”

“That still costs us.”

“Yes.”

The direct answer unsettled him more than defense might have. Cor opened his own evidence slate. North-baffle maintenance hours had risen forty-one percent. Two apprentices left the trade after fatigue restrictions reduced overtime benefits. Replacement parts waited behind clinic rail fabrication. He wanted the five-year review to permit a larger emergency pool of branch scaffolds.

Kera attached his figures to the annual audit. “Name whether release would remain voluntary.”

“If the forecast crosses a survival threshold, no.”
