Omi's signal contracted into a narrow band. “Then name whose memories enter your pool.”

“I cannot name future emergencies.”

“You can name the rule that selects us.”

Cor rubbed dust from the slate edge. “Utility to weather.”

“Then people with technical memory lose more of themselves because you need what they know.”

He did not withdraw the proposal. The branch proxy entered Omi's objection beside it, along with a request to measure how emergency selection would concentrate loss among engineers. Kera added the cyclone record showing voluntary release had met the deficit once, without claiming it would always do so.

“The five-year review remains open,” she said. “Year one supports neither repeal nor permanence.”

Cor sealed his evidence. “You expect me to wait four more years while capacity shrinks.”

“I expect you to build a record capable of changing law without pretending the result has arrived.”

He stayed for the rest of the audit. Opposition had not vanished after losing the vote. It had acquired inspection rights, deadlines, and an obligation to describe its blade.

Omi asked, “Will you show me the scratch?”

Kera rotated the old wafer's archived image onto the reader. The small triangle remained beside its doubled white line. Physical evidence no longer needed concealment, but the original sample stayed in sealed civic storage because taking it out would weaken Tree 44's chain of custody.

“That mark proved someone could see new information,” Omi said.

“It helped.”

“You thought the person might belong to you.”

“I thought shared history answered identity.”

“Do you still?”

Kera inspected the new wafer's edge before answering. “Shared history creates evidence. Relationship requires present agreement.”

Omi considered this. “Kin is acceptable.”

The word landed without the shape Kera once wanted. No inherited parenthood. No branch claim folded into physical expectation. Kin supplied proximity with a boundary.

“Witness is also acceptable,” Omi added. “Mother is not.”

“I recorded that the first time.”

“You recorded it. You took longer to follow it.”

“Yes.”

Omi changed the reader display to the day's refuge schedule. “Group four enters dormancy after the forecast review. I requested you for revival witness.”

Kera looked up. “You already have Niko.”

“He witnesses my Elseweather relationships. I want a physical witness who knows the difference between a missing route and a deleted state.”

The request carried no promise of parenthood. It offered work.

“I accept,” Kera said.

“Put it in the key.”

Kera added her name to Omi's next dormant interval. The slate required both signatures. Omi entered hers through the ring channel. Their marks met at the witness field without becoming the same mark.

A tram bell sounded on the ridge. Morning crews crossed the orchard in pairs, carrying manual weather boards and insulated tool packs. Longer shifts had eased after Jalen's fatigue rules, but more people worked weather now than before the vote. The settlement had exchanged invisible computational labor for visible human time.

Anet Vale came through the court gate with a tray of replacement pulse seals. The yielding office had assigned civic service unrelated to contribution history, partly to prove its own forms meant what they said. Anet wore a housing tag for a western block above the threshold line.

“I brought the narrow seals,” Anet said. “Stores tried to send the old diameter.”

Kera checked the tray. “These fit refuge readers. Leave six.”

Anet glanced at Omi's display. “Are you going dormant today?”

“After forecast review.”

“What does it feel like?”

“It does not feel. That distinguishes refuge from waiting awake.”

Anet shifted the tray against one hip. “I deferred again.”

Omi's signal brightened. “Did they ask properly?”

“Four times, because the education allotment office still had a contribution note in its local copy. Oris made them remove it while I watched.”

“Good.”

“My parent says I may contribute later.”

“You may.” Omi paused. “Later does not own you either.”

Anet carried the remaining seals toward the west trees. Kera watched them stop at each maintenance post, counting the fittings rather than scattering them by habit. One deferred source meant little to the day's weather. Thousands of choices like it shaped the orchard's future capacity. The settlement had not solved that scarcity. It had stopped concealing the authorship of it.

Jalen arrived with frost on his boots despite the mild morning. His cough had faded to an occasional roughness. He placed a manual forecast board beside Kera's reader and studied Tree 44's clock trace.

“On time?”

“So far.”

“That sounds disappointed.”

“Omi expected more drama.”

“Drama exceeds our reserve.”

Omi sent the calibration tone at him. Jalen winced. “That frequency violates the Compact.”

“Cite the clause.”

He opened the day's forecast instead. Three weather routes appeared: a colder western rain that protected moss growth, an even basin mist that consumed industrial oxygen, and a delayed dry front that preserved reserve while increasing old tunnel respiratory risk. Under the prior orchard, millions of possibilities would have narrowed the field before any public view. The new process brought a smaller set into shared review.

Sera's cooperative preferred the rain. Ivo's clinics opposed the dry front. The foundry requested the mist only if its reopening schedule kept the promised oxygen floor. Active branch engineers had compared each route using voluntary, documented scaffolds. No single forecast dominated.

“Where is Niko?” Kera asked.

“Completing the witness notes,” Omi said. “He said you would object if the ring carried a conclusion without its uncertainties.”

“He knows me.”

“He knows what you do now.”

The correction mattered. Kera let it stand.

Niko's signal entered Tree 44 at a direct interval, no hidden lag. His rendered voice sounded thinner than it had before the cyclone. Losing the Saturday had changed what he could revisit, not the continuity tests or relationships that let him speak as himself.

“Kera,” he said. He no longer called her sister unless referring to the shared childhood before his branch point. She no longer called him her dead brother's survival.

“Niko.”

“Forecast notes require a physical audit. Route one protects staple moss and worsens western roof stress. Route two uses foundry oxygen but keeps district humidity within the equal minimum. Route three preserves industrial reserve and adds an estimated respiratory burden the clinics reject.”

“Any scaffold surrendered for this comparison?”

“No. Active duty groups carried sufficient capacity. Two advisers deferred dormancy by eleven minutes with consent.”

“Record fatigue risk?”

“Recorded.”

Kera checked the traces. “Route one assumes repaired greenhouse seals.”

“Sera verified eighty-two percent complete.”

“It models the remaining roofs as empty.”

“They should be.”

“Should does not certify evacuation.”

Jalen called the cooperative. Sera answered from a roof crew and checked the remaining structures by name. One held tools, one stood empty, and one sheltered two workers during seal tests. She ordered them out before approving the rain route.

That small correction delayed the forecast by six minutes. Nobody called the delay waste.

While they waited, Niko opened a private witness line. “Annual relationship review.”

Kera leaned closer to the reader. “We do those now?”

“We said present agreement.”

“We did.”

“Then inherited language cannot run unattended.”

Omi muted her channel with exaggerated care, leaving the indicator lit so they knew she had not disappeared.

Niko began. “I do not ask you to treat me as the boy who died. I do not ask you to forget that we shared a childhood before the branch. I want you as a physical witness, an argument partner, and someone who answers when I request contact within agreed hours.”

Kera noticed the asymmetry in his list. He offered no familial title. The absence did not erase affection. It prevented affection from claiming jurisdiction.

“I want you as a branch witness,” she said, “and as someone whose history intersects mine without belonging to it. I will answer within agreed hours unless an audit conflict is recorded. I will not test you with childhood memory unless you consent.”

“You may ask about the childhood we both retain.”

“Not as proof of who you are.”

“Agreed.”

Kera hesitated. “I still remember the Saturday.”

“I know.”

“I will not narrate it back unless you request.”

“I do not request.”

The refusal hurt with less confusion than it would have a year ago. She entered it into their private key. Memory in one person did not create a right to install it in another.

Niko added, “I do request that you tell me when the kitchen seal finally gets repaired. Apparently everyone has discussed it for a year.”

“It still leaks.”

“Then continuity survives.”

Omi unmuted. “That joke should have stayed dormant.”

Jalen's board chimed. Sera's evacuation confirmations had arrived. Ivo approved the respiratory range. The foundry accepted a two-day delay instead of spending oxygen on the even mist. Route one now carried physical signatures, branch witness notes, district harms, and a review time if roof pressure worsened.

Kera prepared to log the forecast. Tree 44's clock reached the scheduled ring interval.

A new band brightened beneath her hand precisely when civic time changed. No seventeen-minute lag opened. The ring reader displayed the chosen rain route, its confidence range, the names of physical operators, and the active branch advisers who had compared it. Beneath them appeared one line that no weather procedure required.

FORECAST OFFERED. WILL YOU ANSWER?

Kera checked the source. The invitation carried joint signatures from Jalen's board, Sera's cooperative, Ivo's clinic model, Niko's active group, and Omi's refuge council. No plea hid inside instrument noise. No unregistered state had learned to knock where nobody meant to listen.

“What answer does it expect?” she asked.

Omi replied, “Not a predetermined one.”

Kera placed two fingers on the pulse key. The old childhood code waited in muscle memory, available but no longer necessary. She tapped the new interval she and Niko had made after the cyclone. Niko answered from his channel. Omi added a third measure of her own.

Outside East Root Court, manual crews turned the rain vanes. The pale seam over the western ridge broke unevenly, leaving one district dry while drops began against Tree 44's scored leaves.

The new ring closed on time around their three distinct signals.
